# Chapter V Modifications and Extensions of Matrix Mechanics

certain consequences of the quantum-mechanical scheme, especially a deeper
look at the degenerate case leading to the recognition of an exchange
phenomenon, until it became clear that the energy states of helium would also eventually
come out correctly.
V.l Nonmechanical Stress versus Spin
The brilliant and successful application of matrix mechanics to the problem of
calculating the energy states of the hydrogen atom raised great hopes that it
might soon become possible to solve the outstanding problems of atomic
structure. Two sets of atomic phenomena, which had become intimately connected
with each other, required an explanation urgently: first, the anomalous Zeeman
effects; second, the multiplet structure, in general, of spectral lines and, in
particular, the doublet structure of hydrogen and alkali lines. Since Heisenberg
and Pauli were intimately familiar with the problems which had arisen in dealing
with these phenomena during the previous years and had now mastered the tools
of matrix mechanics, they became involved in investigating these tricky aspects
of atomic structure according to the new scheme.
The development began already before Pauli attacked the hydrogen problem.
In a letter to Ralph Kronig, dated 9 October 1925, Pauli discussed the so-called
intercombination lines, that is, the spectral lines arising from the combination of,
say, the singlet and triplet terms in alkaline earth atoms, and he suggested that
they might be explained on the basis of a heuristic {'Ersatz') mechanical model
and the application of 'sharpened' correspondence arguments.252 'Ersatz models'
of atoms had been constructed, especially by Heisenberg and Pauli since fall
1924, in order to be able to deal with the 'classically non-describable duplicity'
{'klassisch nicht beschreibare Art von Zweideutigkeit,' Pauli 1925a, p. 385) of the
states of the series electron in many-electron atoms. In spite of the successes
achieved in this way in explaining the available wealth of spectroscopic data,
Pauli noted:
The substitute models really suffer from all the deficiencies [against] which I have
always preached in the past years: 1. The "stress" {"Zwang") is not interpreted
physically, but just described formally by reformulating the relativistic energy
differences as such, which depend on the [relative] orientation of the [angular
momentum] vectors and are characterized by the notorious double magnetism. 2.
Hence one has here always two kinds of modification of the classical theory, which
appear to be logically independent of each other. One may now characterize these
as follows: (a) transition from the classical mechanics to Heisenberg^ mechanics;
(b) replacement of the classical Hamiltonian H{p,q) by the one describing the
substitute model. (Pauli to Kronig, 9 October 1925)
252The intercombination lines belonged to those phenomena which were the most difficult to
explain on the basis of atomic models. In his Handbuch article, Pauli had discussed them twice (see
Pauli, 1926b, Sections 36 and 44).

V.l Nonmechanical Stress versus Spin
Thus, even though Pauli accepted the new quantum mechanics, which involved
the reformulation of the classical Hamiltonian in terms of the corresponding
quantum-theoretical quantities of matrices, he believed that the scheme
developed so far could neither describe the anomalous Zeeman effects nor even the
fact that the helium atom possessed singlet and triplet states. Moreover, he
claimed, one had to incorporate certain essential features which had hitherto
been expressed by the 'Ersatz' models.
Pauli had asked Kronig to show his letter to Heisenberg, who was also in
Copenhagen at that time. Heisenberg, however, did not agree with the views
Pauli had expressed, for he wrote in his next letter: 'Further, I really do not
believe any word anymore of your theory of stress ("Zwang"). Although it [the
stress] also certainly exists in the case of hydrogen, nevertheless I don't believe
that it has the least bit to do with the nucleus; perhaps it is already present within
the mould ("Schimmel")' (Heisenberg to Pauli, 12 October 1925). Heisenberg
tended to believe that the replacement of the mechanical models of atoms—
which he and Pauli had designated as 'Schimmel9 for a while—by the matrix
representation might already be sufficient to account for the observed structure
of spectral lines. He especially expected that some special feature of the
quantum-mechanical scheme might prove to be helpful. Some indication was already
provided by the fact that the Hamiltonian matrices describing the dynamical
behaviour of atoms had both discrete and continuous energy eigenvalues. From
this he concluded: 'According to quantum mechanics, Larmor's theorem, for
instance, is not valid in general. For hydrogen, for example, there exists—even
for the orbits in the ground state—a relatively large probability to pass over by
absorption [of radiation] into free electrons, and for free electrons Larmor's
theorem certainly does not apply [because of the gyromagnetic ratio]'
(Heisenberg to Pauli, 12 October 1925). Heisenberg was aware of the fact that Larmor's
theorem was valid in the matrix treatment of the harmonic and anharmonic
oscillators, but in these cases only a discrete spectrum existed. For realistic
atomic systems, however, there was a good chance that the theorem would break
down and, as a result, the complex structure of spectra and the anomalous
Zeeman effects might perhaps follow.
Within a few weeks of this exchange with Kronig and Heisenberg, Pauli
arrived at two important conclusions, which emerged from his investigation of
the hydrogen atom: first, by quantizing the total angular momentum one would
not, at least in the case of the one-electron atom, obtain half-integral quantum
numbers for the electron; second, it became inconceivable that Larmor's theorem
could be violated at all in quantum mechanics. Heisenberg immediately
conceded to Pauli: 'With your objection against introducing hyperbolic orbits for
explaining the Zeeman effect, I surely agree. It also occurs to me fairly
improbable that these hyperbolae [which describe the orbits of free electrons] would be
sufficient to explain all the ugly features of the coupling [which gives rise to the
anomalous Zeeman effects]' (Heisenberg to Pauli, 3 November 1925). However,
Heisenberg was not yet convinced that his earlier speculation could be ruled out
entirely, and he wrote again to Pauli: 'I have also thought a bit about the "stress"

("Zwang"), and I still have the hope again and again that it might come in only
in the case of the coupling of two (or several) electrons (and, indeed, through
your exclusion of the equivalent orbits)' (Heisenberg to Pauli, 16 November
1925). Pauli did not share Heisenberg's opinion and, in writing to Bohr, stated
quite emphatically:
I could join in the opinion that atoms with one electron are fundamentally different
from atoms with several electrons only if one were able to establish a theory on a
unified basis, from which would follow, on one hand, no stress ("Zwang") for the
hydrogen atom and, on the other, the empirically-obtained anomalous Zeeman
effect correctly for the alkali atoms. In the present form of Heisenberg's theory,
however, this is not the case; it always leads to Larmor's theorem, and I believe I
can state with certainty that it [Heisenberg's theory] will lead in the case of helium
to one rather than two term systems. (Pauli to Bohr, 17 November 1925)
Pauli believed firmly that the occurrence of two term systems of the helium
atom (i.e., ortho-helium and parhelium) was closely connected with the fact that
already the ground state of the hydrogen atom (i.e., of a one-electron atom) in an
external field was twofold. 'In particular, I believe,' he remarked to Bohr, 'that
one would, if one performed the experiment of deflecting hydrogen atomic
beams in inhomogeneous magnetic fields . . . , see the same thing as in the case
of the Ag-atom, namely ± 1 magneton and not non-magnetic atoms' (Pauli to
Bohr, 17 November 1925). In the matrix treatment of the hydrogen atom just
performed, the ground state possessed the angular momentum zero (i.e., no
angular momentum whatever), and there was no experiment available which
contradicted this conclusion. Later, Erwin Wrede, a student of Otto Stern's,
began to work on a Stern-Gerlach experiment with atomic hydrogen. On 17 July
1926 he presented the results of his measurements in a talk entitled 'Atomic
Hydrogen Beams and their Magnetic Splitting' ('Wasserstoffatomstrahlen und
deren magnetische Aufspaltung) at the eleventh meeting of the Gauverein
Niedersachsen (Lower Saxony Section) of the German Physical Society in Göttingen.
He had found that under the action of an inhomogeneous magnetic field a beam
of hydrogen atoms split up into two distinct parts, consistent with the fact that
each atom possessed a magnetic moment of one Bohr magneton.253 Then,
however, this result, which confirmed Pauli's earlier speculation, did not imply
any difficulty concerning the theoretical understanding, for the spin of the
electron had meanwhile been postulated and accepted as a fundamental
property.
But in November 1925 the conceptual situation in this regard appeared to the
pioneers of quantum mechanics to be quite unpleasant. As Heisenberg recalled:
'We had this doubling [i.e., "two-valuedness"] and we really did not know what it
meant. That was disagreeable. We were a bit unhappy, but then so many things
253E. Wrede's talk was listed among the talks given at the meeting (see Verh. d. Deutsch. Phys.
Ges. (3) 7, p. 37). Wrede had reported his conclusions already earlier to Bohr (Wrede to Bohr, 6 July
1926). He obtained his doctorate with a thesis including the results of his hydrogen measurements
(Wrede, 1927).

V.l Nonmechanical Stress versus Spin
were still not understood that we thought, "Let's wait and see what will come
later.".Then the paper of Uhlenbeck and Goudsmit came' (Heisenberg,
Conversations, p. 377).
On 17 October 1925, exactly a month before Pauli wrote to Bohr about his
suspicion that the ground state of hydrogen ought to be twofold, George
Uhlenbeck and Samuel Goudsmit had signed their note for Naturwissenschaften
in which they proposed to replace the hypothesis of the 'non-mechanical stress'
{'unmechanischer Zwang') of an electron (Uhlenbeck and Goudsmit, 1925). On
the basis of a reanalysis of the existing models for explaining the spectroscopic
data on multiplet structure and the anomalous Zeeman effects, they came to the
conclusion that the entire empirical data—including relativistic and X-ray
doublets—could be accounted for in a simple and satisfactory way by ascribing a
'proper (i.e., intrinsic) rotation' to the electron such that the angular momentum
of this rotation assumed the value \(h/2m). Heisenberg read Uhlenbeck and
Goudsmit's note immediately on its appearance in Naturwissenschaften—it came
out in its issue No. 47 on 20 November—and wrote to Pauli about it on a
postcard: 'Since I have bothered you anyway with so many questions, a further
one certainly does not matter; hence I would like to hear from you, as the master
of criticism, what you think about [Uhlenbeck and] Goudsmit's note in Natur-
Wissenschaften' After mentioning a few difficulties arising from the intrinsic
rotation hypothesis in atomic physics, he concluded: 'It is funny that in the
model of [Uhlenbeck and] Goudsmit [they] can indeed get the Z4-law with
the magnetic hypothesis; hence I am not a priori against [Uhlenbeck and]
Goudsmit's theory. However, I would like to hear your objections' (Heisenberg to
Pauli, 21 November 1925).
With the 'magnetic' origin of the Z4-law Heisenberg had in mind the idea
proposed by Alfred Lande one and a half years previously. In discussing the
origin of the so-called relativistic X-ray doublets, Lande had considered a
magnetic coupling between the radiating electron and the atomic core of a
many-electron atom. He assumed that there existed two different states of the
atom, in which the core possessed the magnetic quantum numbers m and
m + Am, respectively, in the direction of the magnetic field produced by the
orbiting electron at the centre of the atom; then the energy difference of the two
spectral terms associated with the quantum numbers m and m + Am was given
by ÄA*>mag = (const.)(Z3Am/«3/c2), Z being the atomic number and n the
principal quantum number of the electron orbit. (See Lande, 1924a, p. 95; the constant
factor has the value S7r4meesh~4c~2, where me and — e are the mass and charge
of the electron, h is Planck's constant and c is the velocity of light in vacuo.)
Heisenberg now imitated Lande's procedure; i.e., he considered a one-electron
atom, having a nucleus with charge Ze, and he attributed to the electron, in
accordance with Uhlenbeck and Goudsmit's hypothesis, an 'intrinsic' angular
momentum of \{h/2m) and a magnetic moment of one Bohr magneton. Then,
for terms with principal quantum number n (and orbital angular momentum
quantum number k of the electron), he found for the energy difference according

to the Bohr-Sommerfeld theory the expression
rr nJk
whose value, in the case of hydrogen and the helium ion, was twice as large as
the one calculated from Sommerfeld's relativistic theory (Sommerfeld, 1915c).
That k2 occurs in the denominator instead of k(k — 1),' Heisenberg noted,
'would not bother anybody, but I cannot get rid of the factor 2. If one could get
rid of this factor 2, then the model [of Uhlenbeck and Goudsmit] would agree
indeed with your model 1925-10 [—this is how Heisenberg teasingly referred to
Pauli's Ersatz model, about which Pauli had reported to Kronig in the above-
mentioned letter (Pauli to Kronig, 9 October 1925); there was obviously no
dearth of such models—] and all difficulties would be removed with a magic
wand ("Zauberschlag")' (Heisenberg to Pauli, 21 November 1925). In his first
reaction to the spin hypothesis, Heisenberg was quite positive. He thought that a
quantum-mechanical treatment of an atomic system with intrinsic rotation might
—besides yielding some 'averaging' over the angular momentum quantum
number k—retain the essential features brought in by the new concept, especially the
explanation of the multiplet structure of spectral terms. However, he noticed that
besides the difficulty concerning the factor 2, there was another problem. 'In
addition one must however investigate,' he remarked to Pauli, 'where the really
existing relativity disappears in the case of hydrogen' (Heisenberg to Pauli, 21
November 1925). Three days later, in his next letter to Pauli, Heisenberg was
much more reserved; he had had time to reflect about certain implications of
Uhlenbeck and Goudsmit's proposal and had found further difficulties which
had to be removed before intrinsic rotation or spin—as it was to be called
later—could be accepted in atomic theory. Thus he wrote:
Nevertheless, I myself do have very great reservations against the attempt of
[Uhlenbeck and] Goudsmit to establish zoology [i.e., the term structure of many-
electron atoms] in such a simple way: 1. Also the nucleus must [then] have angular
momentum, and then the simplicity of the zoology could hardly be understood. 2. It
would of course be simpler if the electron possessed only charge and mass, but no
angular momentum; in principle, however, one cannot argue against the angular
momentum, but the idea that the electron has a structure (in particular, that several
types of electrons exist) appears to me to be detestable. 3. In the case of the
alkaline-earth atoms the energy difference between singlet and triplet atoms having
the same I [in the erstwhile atomic theory the quantum number / had been defined
as k - 1, where k was the quantum number of the orbital angular momentum (see,
e.g., Pauli, 1926b, p. 219, Eq. (38)); / assumed the values 0, 1,2, etc., for the j-terms,
/7-terms, d-terms, etc.] should then arise only through different interactions of the
magnets [i.e., magnetic moments] of the two electrons with each other. This yields a
totally wrong order of magnitude. (Heisenberg to Pauli, 24 November 1925)
These three objections were certainly serious. Two of them, (1) and (3), arose
from the analysis of the spectroscopic data, while (2) expressed the difficulty of

V.l Nonmechanical Stress versus Spin
understanding why the electron should have an internal structure. At a time
when one believed that the atomic nucleus consisted of protons and electrons, it
seemed evident that the nucleus must possess an intrinsic angular momentum
due to the electrons.254 With his remark concerning the difference of singlet and
triplet terms having the same quantum number /, Heisenberg drew attention to
the following fact: if one explained it, as appeared most obvious, as being due to
the magnetic interaction of the electrons—i.e., as arising from the interaction of
the magnetic moments of two or more electrons—then the term differences
would come out several orders of magnitude less than the observed ones. In spite
of these objections, however, Heisenberg felt that one should at least consider the
electron spin hypothesis as providing a reasonable possibility of arriving at a
unified explanation of the phenomena which had been attributed thus far to the
existence of a 'non-mechanical stress.' 'Nevertheless, Goudsmit has stimulated
me so far towards zoology,' he concluded the letter containing the above
objections, 'that I want to put his model once through the matrix-mill and see
whether at least the ^-formulae, intensities, etc., come out correctly' (Heisenberg
to Pauli, 24 November 1925).
Pauli received the proposal of Goudsmit and Uhlenbeck with much greater
reservation than had Heisenberg. After all, he had already turned down a similar
idea of Ralph Kronig's in early 1925, although it provided a mechanical model
for the extra quantum number involved in the exclusion principle. Now, in
November 1925, he began systematically to marshal arguments against the spin
hypothesis. He did not regard the factor 2 in Eq. (211) as the most serious
difficulty that had arisen; he had a deeper conceptual reason for turning down
the idea of electron spin.255 Especially, he 'strongly doubted the correctness of
the idea because of its classical mechanical character' (Pauli, 1946, p. 214). He
rather believed, as he had pointed out in his letters to Kronig (9 October) and
Bohr (17 October), in the necessity of extending the quantum-mechanical scheme
of Born, Heisenberg and Jordan in such a way that the features attributed before
to 'non-mechanical stress' ('Zwang9) would be incorporated. In Pauli's opinion,
this basically quantum-theoretical extension could not simply be replaced by
endowing the electron with an extra mechanical property, the 'intrinsic angular
momentum' of Uhlenbeck and Goudsmit. He completely agreed with Bohr when
the latter remarked that 'the definition of stress ("Zwang") really rests exactly on
254The protons would contribute a much smaller intrinsic momentum because of their larger
mass. In fact, Pauli, in a short note submitted August 1924 (Pauli, 1924c), had already discussed the
origin of the satellites accompanying the components of line multiplets that arose from the interaction
of the angular momentum of the nucleus with the angular momentum of the series electrons. This
interaction should give rise to a very small splitting of the multiplet components, which was called
'hyperfine structure'; hence it followed that, if the nuclear angular momentum was responsible for
hyperfine structure, then it had to be several orders of magnitude (exactly three orders, i.e., the ratio
of electron mass over nuclear mass) smaller than the angular momenta connected with electrons.
255 In fact, Pauli at first did not notice the difficulty concerning the factor 2 and requested
Heisenberg to give him the steps leading to Eq. (211). Heisenberg, in his letter to Pauli dated 24
November 1925, gave the derivation and mentioned: The above calculations have been given already
by Lande (Z. /. Phys. !A, 88, 1924); there, however, Z3 always replaces Z4.'

the proof that mechanical pictures are inadequate for atoms having several
electrons' (Bohr to Pauli, 25 November 1925). With great relief, Pauli replied to
Bohr: 'I am very happy that we are essentially in agreement concerning our
judgment about the present situation in atomic theory. Also Heisenberg, with
whom I recently had an active exchange of letters, has similar views. Hopefully
we shall soon make progress' (Pauli to Bohr, 1 December 1925).
The agreement between Bohr, Heisenberg and Pauli in their skeptical attitude
towards the spin hypothesis did not last long. On 9 December 1925 Niels Bohr
travelled to Leyden in order to attend the celebrations connected with the fiftieth
anniversary of Hendrik Antoon Lorentz' doctorate. In Leyden he not only made
the acquaintance of Uhlenbeck and Goudsmit and their ideas, but also had
occasion to discuss the spin hypothesis with Albert Einstein. A few months later,
Bohr recalled the situation in a letter to Ralph Kronig, who had returned from
Copenhagen to the United States before the Lorentz jubilee. He wrote:
Since you left Copenhagen we have often missed you. Indeed, I have been deeply
interested in just these spectral problems to which you have given so much
thought.... From the discussion after your beautiful lecture on the Zeeman effect
you know how unhappy and desperate I felt over the state of things. Certainly I had
heard then that in a letter to Naturwissenschaften Uhlenbeck and Goudsmit had
proposed to provide a basis for a new quantum number by ascribing a vector to the
electron itself; but not understanding how this vector could materially influence the
motion of the electron to which it was attached, I had thought so little about this
idea, that I even quite forgot to question you about this possibility [during] the
discussion that evening. When I came to Leyden, however, to the Lorentz festival,
Einstein asked the very first moment I saw him what I believed about the spinning
electron. Upon my question about the cause of the necessary mutual coupling
between the spin axis and the orbital motion, he explained that this coupling was an
immediate consequence of the theory of relativity. This remark acted as a complete
relivation [sic, revelation] to me, and I have never since faltered in my conviction
that we at last were at the end of our sorrows. (Bohr to Kronig, 26 March 1926)
After leaving Leyden, Bohr visited Göttingen for several days and spoke,
especially in conversations with Heisenberg, in favour of the electron spin
hypothesis. Then, on his way back to Copenhagen, he passed through Berlin
where he met Pauli. 'We spent a beautiful day in Berlin,' Bohr reported to James
Franck, his host in Göttingen. T have delivered your message to Planck, and I
have succeeded with Pauli at least to the extent that he is himself extremely
interested to find out whether or not the matrix reformulation of the electron-
magnet gospel correctly represents experience' (Bohr to Franck, 22 December
1925). At that time Heisenberg, assisted by Jordan, was treating the anomalous
Zeeman effect in matrix mechanics by including the electron spin hypothesis. On
24 December 1925 he wrote to Pauli about the initial results, and remarked:
As agreed, we have attacked the quantum mechanical calculations of this theory;
however, "due to lack of time and spirit" we have not proceeded beyond the
quasi-trivial results. The g- and y-formulae are really entirely trivial, nearly so is
V.l Nonmechanical Stress versus Spin
also the sum-rule and it comes out as was to be expected; for the doublets there
results Voigt's formula, and for triplets a cubic equation (which I believe agrees
with the cubic equation contained in my first paper [Heisenberg, 1922a]?!), etc.
[Then he continued:] What is still missing in order to calculate the absolute values
of the splitting, are "only" the two average values r~2 and r~3 [i.e., their matrix
reformulations]; the first one for [calculating] the relativity, and the second one for
the magnetism [i.e., for the matrix calculation of the relativistic splitting and of the
magnetic splitting of the terms caused by the interaction of electron spins, which
would yield the quantum mechanical formula replacing the classical Eq. (211)]. As
soon as one has these average values, one can decide whether or not [Uhlenbeck
and] Goudsmit's theory is a possible description, but I do not yet know how to
compute these average values. (Heisenberg to Pauli, 24 December 1925)
While Heisenberg expressed some optimism that one might eventually obtain
the right answer concerning the relativistic fine structure by calculating the
inverse powers of the radial variable—especially a correction of the wrong factor
2 in Eq. (211)—Pauli, towards the end of 1925, again fell back into his previous
negative attitude. 'After thinking about it again, I am still skeptical, and I believe
that Goudsmit is on the wrong track,' he wrote to Bohr on 30 December 1925.
He then pointed out, first, the difficulty of understanding, at least in the limit of
high quantum numbers k, the existence of screening doublets. 'And secondly,'
Pauli continued, 'when one looks at it more closely, it becomes difficult to
understand why an electron with angular momentum ^(h/lir) does not possess a
much larger energy (mass) than the one that is actually determined.' That is,
Pauli expected that the existence of an intrinsic rotation would contribute
considerably to the self-mass of the electron. 'Though I admit,' he went on, 'that
one will be able to come to a final conclusion only on the basis of detailed
quantum mechanical calculations, and that one cannot see at the moment any
other way of understanding the failure of Larmor's theorem and the duplicity,
yet I can so far only shake my head about Goudsmit's idea and say: "I don't like
the thing!'" (Pauli to Bohr, 30 December 1925).
In spite of their slightly different attitudes, Pauli and Heisenberg agreed on
one point: the next task consisted in calculating in matrix theory the averages of
the inverse powers of the radial matrix quantities, r~2 and r~3, over full periods.
On returning to Göttingen after his Christmas vacation, Heisenberg reported to
Pauli: T have used the railway travel from Munich to think a little about the
famous average values. It is true that I have not arrived at any definite result, but
perhaps you may be able to do something with the following idea' (Heisenberg to
Pauli, 7 January 1926). Then he sketched a description of the electron's motion
in the hydrogen atom in terms of polar coordinates. In particular, he proposed to
introduce a quantum-theoretical angle
through the reformulated classical
relation
= arctan(^V (212)
where x and y were the Cartesian coordinate matrices. He added: 'That this

quantity cannot be expanded in a [quantum-theoretical] Fourier series, is of no
significance, according to a recent paper of Born and Wiener' (Heisenberg to
Pauli, 7 January 1926).
A week later Heisenberg informed Pauli that he might pass through Hamburg
on 20 January in order to discuss the problem of evaluating the averages of the
radial matrix quantities, r~2 and r~3, with the help of an action-angle formalism.
On the same postcard he wrote: 'Herewith I am sending you the paper of Born
and Wiener and a similar paper of Lanczos for [our] discussion on Wednesday'
(Heisenberg to Pauli, 14 January 1926). The papers of Born and Wiener (1926b)
and of Lanczos (1926a), to which Heisenberg referred, involved certain
modifications of the matrix scheme; with these modifications one could try to establish a
consistent quantum-mechanical scheme of action and angle variables, which
seemed to be necessary in order to solve problems of relativistic fine structure
and of the anomalous Zeeman effects.256
V.2 Field-Like Representation of Quantum Mechanics
The new theory of quantum mechanics of Heisenberg, Born and Jordan, is related
most closely with the theory of integral equations. The equations of motion and the
quantum conditions can be written down in the form of integral equations. Thus
there results a continuum interpretation, which must be placed side by side with the
discontinuum interpretation [of Heisenberg, Born and Jordan] as having equal
rights, since there exists a one-to-one relation between the two. For the fundamental
interpretation of the theory, the integral formulation has the advantage that it is in
immediate harmony with the usual field-like conceptions of physics. (Lanczos,
1926a, p. 812)
These programmatic lines constituted the abstract of the first paper on quantum
mechanics that was written by a physicist outside the Göttingen and Copenhagen
circles. It was entitled 'Über eine feldmäßige Darstellung der neuen
Quantenmechanik' ('On a Field-Like Representation of the New Quantum Mechanics')
and its author was Kornel (or Cornelius, as he called himself later on) Lanczos,
then an assistant at the Institute for Theoretical Physics of the University of
Frankfurt-am-Main. Who was Cornelius Lanczos and how did he come to work
on the new theory?
Lanczos was born on 2 February 1893 in Szekesfehervär, Hungary.257 From
256 Heisenberg had received from Born a copy of the paper of Born and Wiener and read it during
the Christmas vacation; it was recieved by Zeitschrift für Physik on 5 January 1926 and appeared in
its issue No. 3 of 12 March 1926. On his return to Göttingen after the Christmas vacation he found a
copy of Cornelius Lanczos' paper on a field-like representation of the new quantum mechanics
(which, evidently, Lanczos had sent to Göttingen); this paper was received by Zeitschrift für Physik
on 22 December 1925 and later appeared in its issue No. 11/12 of 26 February 1926.
257Lanczos used the Hungarian spelling of his first name, Kornel (without the accent on e), in
most of his German publications, changing in 1929 to Cornel and later to Cornelius. It is of interest
to note that his family name, Lanczos, appeared in an early publication of 1923 as Lancius. Finally, it
should be mentioned that his father's name was Carolus Loewy—'Lanczos' was the Hungarian
translation of 'Loewy.'

V.2 Field-Like Representation of Quantum Mechanics
1911 to 1916 he studied physics and mathematics at the University of Budapest,
where his teachers were Loränd Eötvös and Lipot (Leopold) Fejer; Eötvös was
known for his experimental researches connected with establishing the
equivalence of gravitational and inertial masses and Fejer for his contributions to the
theory of Fourier series. Upon graduation Lanczos became an assistant at the
Polytechnical University of Budapest where, for the next four years, he worked
on experimental physics. In 1921 he finally received his doctorate from the
University of Szeged, which was also situated in Budapest at the time, with a
thesis on The Function-Theoretic Behaviour of Maxwell's Vacuum Equations,'
supervised by Rudolph Ortvay, a former student of Sommerfeld's. Adverse
political circumstances prevailing in Hungary made it difficult for Lanczos to
pursue his plans there upon completing his doctorate. Like many another young
man at the time with an ambition to embark upon a career in scientific research,
Lanczos went to Germany. He found a position at the Physics Institute of
Freiburg-im-Breisgau and stayed there for three years.258 In fall 1924 he went to
the University of Frankfurt, where the prospects for his scientific career seemed
to be more promising.
In spite of the fact that he worked in places like Freiburg and Frankfurt,
which did not count themselves among the principal centres of theoretical
physics in Germany, Lanczos soon managed to achieve a good name for himself
by his work on general relativity theory.259 During a visit to Berlin he had come
under the spell of Albert Einstein, the founder of this theory. 'I had my first
discussion with Einstein when I was in Germany in 1921,' Lanczos recalled
forty-five years later. T had a chance to talk to him for a few minutes between
two lectures. Of course, I had already had a tremendous admiration for him in
my student days, and so I was in a great palpitation of heart when this
opportunity came' (Lanczos in Whitrow, 1972, p. 48). Lanczos had at once
admired the relativity theory because of its conceptual power and, as he
expressed it in 1923 during a discussion, because of 'its logical consistency
[Folgerichtigkeit], in that it takes the foundations of differential geometry—which
258 In the list of members of the German Physical Society (which Lanczos had joined in spring
1920), published at the end of the year 1921 (see Verh. d. Deutsch. Phys. Ges. (3) 2, p. 87), Lanczos'
home address was given as Berlin. Hence he spent some time in late 1921 in Berlin before going to
Freiburg. In Freiburg the leading physicist was Franz Himstedt who, in 1924, was succeeded by
Gustav Mie. Another member of the faculty was Emil Cohn, formerly professor at the University of
Strasbourg (he was known for his research on the electrodynamics of moving bodies) and since 1920
'ordentlicher Honorarprofessor' ('honorary full professor') of theoretical physics; he had a deep interest
in the physics of space and time. Among the mathematicians in Freiburg was Alfred Loewy.
259In none of the papers which Lanczos submitted from Freiburg, and in hardly any of the ones
submitted later from Frankfurt, did Lanczos thank one of the professors or the senior people at these
universities. Hence one may conclude that he worked on his papers entirely by himself, guided only
by the ideas of Einstein and a few others. Gustav Mie, with whom some of his interests may have
overlapped, came to Freiburg only in fall 1924 when Lanczos had already gone to Frankfurt.
On the other hand, Lanczos already had heard about Einstein's work on relativity theory in
Hungary since he studied with Eötvös, whose experimental results had provided one of the
fundamental principles (i.e., the equivalence of inertial and gravitational masses) that was a
cornerstone of general relativity theory, and Ortvay, who had introduced the recent ideas of modern physics
to his students.

are free of any arbitrariness—as the general foundation of the description of
nature, so that the imperative of thought becomes the necessity of nature'
(Lanczos in Raschevsky, 1923, p. 441). Thus, Lanczos, the former disciple of
Eötvös, became a devoted researcher in general relativity theory during the
following years, treating such topics as the equations of gravitation, the
properties of de Sitter space, the problem of rotation, the theory of the perihelion shift
of Mercury, and cosmology.260
It was while investigating the behaviour of weak fields in Einstein's theory of
gravitation that Lanczos discovered an interesting fact (Lanczos, 1925a). He had
to solve a differential equation for a tensor field with certain boundary
conditions imposed on the solutions; he converted the differential equation into an
integral equation and discovered that the latter was of the Fredholm type. This
discovery pleased him very much, especially since he had arrived at the integral
equation not by mathematical but by physical argumentation. Indeed, Lanczos
did not know then that Hilbert had established the equivalence of differential
equations (with boundary conditions) and integral equations nearly twenty years
before.261 It was perhaps a charming accident that Lanczos, who was well-trained
mathematically, had not come across this result earlier; yet his rediscovery of the
old methods of integral equations had a certain advantage which showed itself
very soon in his work.
In fall 1924 Lanczos went to Frankfurt where Erwin Madelung, the Director
of the Institute of Physics, was just preparing the second edition of his book, Die
mathematischen Hilfsmittel des Physikers, a compendium of the mathematical
tools of physics (Madelung, 1925). Madelung added a number of new topics,
such as Green's functions, tensor analysis and general relativity theory in the new
edition, and Lanczos helped him with their presentation.262 In the course of this
260In the 1920s, when debates on relativity theory often took place in the discussions at physics
meetings, Lanczos took a strong stand in favour of Einstein's theory. (By the way, Lanczos served as
a secretary—Schriftführer—of the German Physical Society from 1922 to 1924.) At the annual
meeting of the Society in Bonn in September 1923, Lanczos expressed his opinion in support of
relativity theory. Although the atmosphere then was less controversial than at the Bad Nauheim
meeting three years earlier, some people still raised doubts about the validity of general relativity
theory. New results, such as the measurement of the deflection of light in the gravitational field of the
sun made by the American Einstein Expedition of 1922, seemed to be compatible with a different
theoretical description than the one provided by Einstein's theory (Hopmann, 1923). Lanczos,
however, argued that any numerical agreement obtained from a nonrelativistic theory had to be
considered an extreme coincidence {'kolossaler Zufall,' Lanczos in Hopmann, 1923, p. 484).
261 Lanczos discovered this only after he had submitted the above-mentioned paper and added the
following remarks in proof:
In writing this paper I have unfortunately not taken enough notice of the existing mathematical
literature. Therefore, I wish to point out here that already Hilbert, in his fundamental memoirs
on integral equations (which appeared together in one volume, Teubner, 1912), applied
"Green's tensor" to solve simultaneous differential equations. (See pp. 206-212 [of Hilbert,
1912c]; see also the book of Courant-Hilbert, Methoden der mathematischen Physik I, Springer,
1924, p. 316, which provides an excellent review of the theory of integral equations.) He deals
naturally only with Euclidean domains. The presentation given above should be closer to the
line of thought of the physicist interested in theory. (Lanczos, 1925a, p. 124, footnote 1)
262 Madelung, in the preface to the new edition, acknowledged the help of his assistant. One finds
that in the section on relativity some of Lanczos' recent results on the theory of gravitation were
included.
V.2 Field-Like Representation of Quantum Mechanics
work for his new boss Lanczos had to study the mathematical literature, in
particular the subjects treated in the book of Courant and Hubert (1924), which
had just been published and to which Madelung referred frequently. A short
section on quantum theory was also included in the Mathematische Hilfsmittel,
and it was more than a coincidence that, at about the same time as he assisted
Madelung on the book, Lanczos began to address himself to an outstanding
problem of quantum theory.
The current status of quantum theory had been reviewed recently, in
September 1924, at the Naturforscherversammlung in Innsbruck, where Hendrik Kramers
and Arnold Sommerfeld presented differing views concerning the structure of
atoms. The most controversial question, as revealed by the discussions in which
Einstein participated, was the status of the radiation problem. Two theories of
radiation competed with each other at that time, each describing a certain set of
phenomena adequately: one was the theory of Bohr, Kramers and Slater, which
had just been successful in accounting for the dispersion of light by atoms; the
other was Einstein's old theory of light-quanta, which had recently been
supplemented by the statistical method of the Indian physicist Satyendra Nath Bose.
However, the two theories seemed to be completely at variance, and Lanczos
became interested in the question whether general relativity theory might help in
resolving the discrepancies. He hoped, especially, that perhaps some of the
quantum features of radiation would follow by considering radiation in a closed
universe.
Lanczos concerned himself with this question in the first half of 1925, making
ample use of the methods of integral equations. The latter implied that the
electromagnetic four-potential <&, (/ = 0,1,2,3; <&0 denoting the electric potential,
and
,, <&2 and
3 forming the magnetic vector potential) satisfied an integral
equation of the form
*/=( 2 *(K*'*')*y(*W 1 = 0,1,2,3, (213)
where KJx9x') is a kernel which depends on two points, x and x'9 of the
four-dimensional space, and the path of integration extends over the entire
universe. As a special case, Lanczos treated the situation in a spherical universe
of finite, constant radius containing an atom at its origin (Lanczos, 1925b). The
eigenfunctions solving Eqs. (213) in this example could be expressed as products
of periodic functions in the spherical (spatial) coordinates (i.e., the distance from
the origin and two angles). Every eigenf unction,
(W), was then characterized by
a number vn9 which Lanczos interpreted as the frequency of the 'standing ether
waves' (Lanczos, 1925c, p. 129, p. 137). The 'ether waves' could be excited by an
electron of the atom, which performs a periodic motion with frequency co. If
integral multiples of co coincide with the frequency of one of the ether
oscillations, then the amplitude of the latter would become infinite according to the
usual dynamical considerations (because the denominator, p2 — k2co29 is zero),

unless one assumed zero coupling between the electron's motion and the ether-
vibration. Lanczos now made this assumption and claimed that it provided an
explanation for the stationary orbits of electrons in atoms as postulated by Bohr's
theory in the following sense:
Of all orbits, which are dynamically possible under the action of the Lorentz force,
there exist only certain definite ones in which charge and current density possess
such a distribution that they are in accord with the condition of stationarity [of the
orbits]. Only on such orbits are permanent revolutions [of the electrons] possible, for
then no transfer of energy to the ether occurs. In all other situations the electron
emits radiation, probably not in the classical sense by the overflow of energy into
space, but by the process that the amplitude of the forced ether vibrations increases
steadily due to resonance [with the motion of the electron]. Because of the reaction
of this radiation [i.e., of the ether vibrations] on the electron a new force, which is
not of a conservative nature, arises in addition to the Lorentz force; and, as a
consequence of this force, we have to understand [the fact] that the electron is
always again driven back into the stationary orbits. (Lanczos, 1925c, pp. 140—
141 )263
Two types of radiation, had, therefore, to be distinguished in Lanczos' theory.
The first, consisting of all the standing vibrations of the ether of constant
amplitude, were described by the orthogonal and normalized eigenfunctions
which were solutions of Eqs. (213). In the latter, the kernel K^x.x') took into
account the motion of all charged particles (electrons and others) in the universe.
Although Lanczos was aware of the fact that these 'eigenoscillations' of the ether
had not been experimentally detected, he referred to the concept of 'virtual
oscillator fields' in the theory of Bohr, Kramers and Slater (1924) as giving
support to the existence of a permanent radiation of the ether. The second type
of radiation was produced by the transitions of an electron from one stable orbit
to another stable orbit. In this case, the energy of transition was regarded as
being transferred completely to the ether, exciting in it a large number of
eigenoscillations which interfered in such a way as to give rise to the
monochromatic emission line of an atom.
With the help of these considerations, Lanczos reformulated the standard
problem of describing the radiation emitted by atoms in a completely new
manner. Instead of studying the mechanics of the atom one had now to
investigate the excitation of the eigenoscillations in the ether (or the universe) by
the motion of electrons in atoms. The fundamental difficulty of Lanczos'
programme was how to construct the universal kernel function entering Eq.
(213), and he therefore concluded: 'On the basis of this conception the solution
of the quantum problem therefore appears to be reduced to the knowledge of a
Hamiltonian principle, which for the time being still lies far in the dark, and its
263 Lanczos proved himself again to be a follower of Albert Einstein's ideas about the origin of
quantized electron orbits in quantum theory. Since many years Einstein had speculated that the
stationary orbits may be obtained as specific solutions of some generalized field equations (see, in
particular, Einstein, 1923d).

V.2 Field-Like Representation of Quantum Mechanics
connection with the eigenf unctions' (Lanczos, 1925c, p. 141). He suggested that
the action principle of general relativity could possibly give a hint for finding the
appropriate action principle in radiation theory. Realizing how difficult this task
might be, Lanczos hoped to be able to throw new light on further details by a
'closer investigation of the powerful ("weitgreifende") "method of eigenf
unctions," even without an explicit use of a Hamiltonian principle' (Lanczos, 1925c,
p. 141).
While he was pondering about the continuation of this programme, Lanczos
read the papers of Heisenberg (1925c) and Born and Jordan (1925b). He
immediately grasped that these authors had made important progress and, in the
introduction of his next paper, he noted:
The far-reaching thoughts of Heisenberg might be of path-breaking importance for
research in quantum theory. In a logical continuation of the new ideas, Born and
Jordan succeeded in giving Heisenberg^ conceptions an adequate mathematical
expression by a large-scale generalization; thus they discovered the general formal
foundations of the new theory. One arrives at a logically established discontinuum
theory, for which the classical concepts are of importance merely through
correspondence and as heuristic guidelines; however, the new theory proceeds from the
very beginning along its own directions and associates with the old symbols a
completely new meaning. The fundamental principles of quantum theory have thus
obtained an unexpected deepening. (Lanczos, 1926a, p. 812)
To Lanczos the new development in quantum mechanics came as a big
surprise, for it broke completely with the physical conceptions he had grown up
with, especially the field theory of matter. However, he quickly saw that, in spite
of the apparent differences in formulation, the Heisenberg-Born-Jordan
quantum mechanics made some connection with his earlier work on relativity and
quantum theories. And the key to this connection was provided by the method of
integral equations which had become his favourite tool. Since Lanczos had
become familiar with Hubert's work, he knew perfectly about the equivalence of
matrix equations, such as the ones that appeared in the paper of Born and
Jordan, and integral equations, which he had used earlier. To exploit this
mathematical equivalence now, in late 1925, became his goal. Within a few
weeks he was able to accomplish this task, and before Christmas 1925 he
submitted a paper on it to Zeitschrift für Physik.264 He summarized its content in
the following words:
In the following we wish to point to an extraordinarily simple and beautiful
connection that can be established between the Heisenberg-Born-Jordan theory
and the theory of integral equations. We shall see that all results of the theory may
also be expressed in the form of integral equations; thus they obtain a formulation,
which may appear more natural to the physicist—who is used to analytical methods
264Born and Jordan's paper (1925b) on quantum mechanics was published in late November
1925. Lanczos' acticle on the field-like representation of quantum mechanics was received by
Zeitschrift für Physik on 22 December 1925 (Lanczos, 1926a).

—than the matrix formulation. Simultaneously, there arises with it also a
continuum interpretation, which, as far as the description of empirical facts is concerned,
can stand with equal rights beside the discontinuum interpretation, since there exists
a unique correspondence between the two interpretations. However, what concerns
the physical interpretation of the facts, that is, the real nature of the quanta, it is not
excluded that the integral formulation might even be superior to the matrix
formulation; the reason is that it has the advantage of being immediately consistent
with the field conceptions, in fact it is even built on them, while the concept of the
field is apparently far removed from the discontinuum interpretation. (Lanczos,
1926a, pp. 812-813)
For the purpose of establishing the relation between matrix methods and the
methods of integral equations, Lanczos introduced a finite, bounded
mathematical space of arbitrary extension and dimensions, whose points he denoted by s
and a. In this space he assumed a complete orthogonal set of eigenfunctions,
§l{s) (/= 1,2,3, . . .), to be given; this set belongs to a fundamental kernel
function, K(s, a), which is not specified for the moment. Thus, each eigenfunc-

Equations (216a, b) established a one-to-one correspondence between the infinite
matrices, (aik), and the piecewise continuous kernel functions, f(s, a). Lanczos
was thus able to re-interpret without difficulty all the matrix operations and
V.2 Field-Like Representation of Quantum Mechanics
equations, which Born and Jordan had used to establish quantum mechanics, by
means of integral equations containing 'field-like' kernel functions.265
As the most important matrix operations to be translated, Lanczos considered
the operations of taking the diagonal sum of a matrix, the product of two
matrices, the differentiation of a matrix function with respect to one of its matrix
variables, and the definition of the time derivative of a matrix. The first of these
operations was easy to express because taking the diagonal sum of a matrix, (aik\
was just equivalent to integrating the associated kernel for equal points, s = a,
over the entire space.266 The matrix product, on the other hand, was represented
in Lanczos' formalism by folding the corresponding kernels, that is,

derivative of a dynamical variable. In matrix mechanics, where a variable had
been represented by a Hermitian matrix, the operation of taking the time
derivative always produced a matrix whose elements were equal to the elements
of the original matrix multiplied by the factor 2

Hence the kernel K(s, a), apart from a constant factor, could replace the function
H(s,o) representing the Hamiltonian in Lanczos' scheme. Equation (218) led to
important consequences concerning the dynamical equations which had to be
developed.
Lanczos did not have the slightest doubt that the dynamics in quantum theory
had to be derived from a Hamiltonian principle. He was satisfied that the
relevant Hamiltonian principle could be related to the fundamental integral
equation (214) in the following way. He considered the special kernel function,
pq(s,o)- H(s,o), which represented the Langrangian in his scheme, and
expressed the Hamiltonian principle by the equation
/= \[pq(s,s) - H(s,s)]ds= f[(pKq - pqK)(s,s) - H(s,s)]ds = extremum,
(220)
where / is the action integral and H(s,o) denotes the Hamiltonian kernel
function. This formulation implied, of course, that Lanczos now identified the (so
far) purely mathematical space of points s with the physical space of four-dimensional
(space-time) points x. Then Eq. (220) was simply equivalent to the extremum
principle, Eq. (88b), of Born and Jordan.268 Evidently, it is possible to derive the
Hamiltonian equations of motion from the extremum principle, Eq. (220), by
variation of the action-integral with respect to p(s,o) and q(s,o), the kernel
functions representing the quantum-mechanical momentum and position
variables. For this purpose, Lanczos had just to consider H(s,o) as a function of
p(s,o) and q(s,o); then he obtained equations of motion which were equivalent
to those in matrix mechanics.269
In varying the action-integral for obtaining the equations of motion the
quantum conditions must be satisfied. Lanczos reformulated the Born-Jordan
quantum condition, Eq. (67), as the integral equation
(pq-qp)(s,o) = JL-.E(s,o), (221)
