V.2 Field-Like Representation of Quantum Mechanics
where E(s,a) is the 'unit kernel' ('Einheitskern,9 Lanczos, 1926a, p. 818), which
has the property that all functions
(s) are eigenfunctions (satisfying a linear
integral equation of the type of Eq. (214) with E(s,o) as the kernel) with the
eigenvalue A= 1. He observed immediately that the function E(s,o) was not
regular in its variables; it had, in particular, the property that it was zero
everywhere except when s = o, where it became infinite in such a way that the
a-integral assumed the value unity for every given s.270 The occurrence of the
irregular kernel function E(s,o) bothered Lanczos, and he stated in his paper:
'From this we see that the functions p and q cannot stay finite in the entire
region, since otherwise their products would also become finite everywhere. Thus
we arrive at the following interesting conclusion: If the functions p(s,o) and
q(s,o) should remain finite everywhere, then the Born-Jordan quantum condition
cannot be valid rigorously, but rather only in an arbitrarily good approximation'
(Lanczos, 1926a, p. 819).271
In order to weaken the quantum condition Lanczos proposed two possibilities.
The first was to replace the unit kernel E(s,o) by a regular kernel function
having the following property: for fixed s this function would be finite in a small
sphere around the central point s such that the a-integration would yield unity
(Lanczos, 1926a, p. 819). Hence the quantum condition of Born and Jordan
would not be satisfied in the strict sense for sharp points s and o, but only if the
space resolution was coarse enough that the small sphere did not dissolve into
discrete points. In a note added in proof to his paper, Lanczos described another
idea for obtaining regular kernel functions in quantum mechanics (Lanczos,
1926a, pp. 827-830). In particular, he proposed to modify the quantum
condition, Eq. (67), in such a way that it would remain valid only for finite sub-
matrices of pq - qp; that is, the diagonal elements with higher indices would
deviate appreciably from the standard value h/lmi and go to zero. This would
mean that {h/2iri)E(s,o) is replaced by the regular function U(s,a), which has
the property that the integral, jU(s,s)ds, is zero.272 After the only irregular
function of the scheme was thus eliminated, the functions p(s, o) and q(s,o) as
well as all other functions describing quantum-mechanical variables could be
treated as regular functions.
Lanczos did not worry about the violation of the Born-Jordan quantum
condition in the case of matrix elements with high indices. Arguing from the
point of view that in a finite universe, with only regular mass and charge
distributions, the energy is always finite, he assumed that higher energy values
270For example, one also has lim€^0 fE(s,s + e)de = 1. Hence E(s,o) had similar properties as
the expression which Paul Dirac would later call a 'delta-function' (Dirac, 1927a).
271 Another way out of this difficulty seemed to be to use the quantum condition (221), not
directly but only when both sides of it were folded with a regular kernel, say K(s, o). Lanczos did not
find this satisfactory, for it did not guarantee that the p and q were regular functions in his scheme.
272 Evidently, the matrix corresponding to U(s,o\ according to Eq. (216a), has the diagonal sum
zero. This can be achieved, e.g., when one approximates the matrix by a finite, diagonal (n X n)-
matrix, having all elements equal to h/lni, except the last one which assumes the value (1 - n)h/2wi,
and then takes the limit «->oo. (See Lanczos, 1926a, p. 829.)

corresponding to very high indices of the matrices do not occur in reality. All
matrices, he concluded therefore, had to be finite and the regular function
U(s,o) should always replace—up to a factor 2m/h—the unit kernel E(s,o) in
Eq. (221).273
Lanczos' quantum-mechanical scheme may be summarized by two
fundamental equations, the action principle and the quantum condition. Using the regular
kernel U(s,o) in both instead of E(s,o), these equations assume the form
1= - ([KU(s,o) + H(s,o)]ds= extremum (220a)
and
(pq-qp)(s,o)=U(s,o), (221a)
where K(s,o), U(s,o) and H(s,o) have to be considered as functions that are
given.274 Disregarding, for the moment, the modification of the quantum
condition envisaged by Lanczos, there existed a complete equivalence between his
scheme and that of Born and Jordan. Thus, in principle, it did not matter
whether quantum systems were described by means of equations involving
matrices or integrals over field-like kernel functions. However, in practical cases
the choice of one method or the other would depend upon the nature of the
problem under investigation.
And yet, there was a considerable conceptual difference between the two
formulations. In matrix mechanics a mechanical system was completely defined
by its Hamiltonian matrix and the commutation relations for its dynamical
variables. In Lanczos' integral equation scheme one had to know the
corresponding kernels H(s, o) and U(s, o) and, in addition, the fundamental kernel K(s, a).
'For the matrix interpretation,' Lanczos noted, 'only the eigenvalues of this
kernel are of importance, while the orthogonal system of eigenfunctions
associated with it remains completely undetermined' (Lanczos, 1926a, p. 820).
Indeed, Eq. (219) meant only that the eigenvalues of the kernels K(s, o) and H(s, o)
had to be, apart from a constant factor, exactly the same.275 But from the
mathematical theory it followed that different integral equations of the type
of Eq. (214)—i.e., equations which have different kernels, K(s,o), K\s,o),
K"(s,o), . . . , and a different set of eigenfunctions, ^i{s\^i{s\^,i{s\ . . . ,
273 In drawing the above conclusion, Lanczos thought of the harmonic oscillator as a typical
system in quantum mechanics. There are other systems, however, such as the hydrogen atom, in
which infinitely many states occur without Wn going to infinity in the limit of large n.
Beginning with his earliest papers on general relativity theory, Lanczos had always argued in
favour of nonsingular field solutions. His insistence on the regular solutions, pis,a) and q(s,o), in
quantum mechanics may be explained very well from this background.
274Equation (220a) is obtained by rewriting the first term, f[pKq - pqK](s, s)ds, as f[K(qp-
pq)](s, s) ds, which can be done if all factors in the products are regular (i.e. in that case, the factors
in the products pKq and pqK can be cyclically permuted to give Kqp and Kpq, respectively).
275Note that in the integral equation approach the eigenvalues of H(s,o) are W~x rather than
W .
V.2 Field-Like Representation of Quantum Mechanics 215
may yield the same eigenvalues \. If, however, the eigenvalues of different
integral equations were identical, the associated sets of eigenfunctions,
l(s) and
"(s) (i = 1,2, . . .), would be connected by orthogonal transformations. Now it
was clear why the eigenfunctions played no role in matrix mechanics because
there two quantities related by orthogonal transformations had to be considered
identical. Lanczos concluded: Therefore one can never assert anything from
matrix equations about the basis functions '0s)' (Lanczos, 1926a, p. 820).
In a previous paper on the quantum problem, Lanczos had claimed that the
eigenfunctions should have a physical meaning if the integral equation approach
were to make any sense at all. This claim had grown out of his deeply felt
scientific belief, for he had declared: The author confesses his adherence to that
Pythagorean view of the world, which sees in numbers more than just a formal
and convenient tool for the purpose of describing [empirical] facts, which rather
recognizes in mathematics an unavoidable symbolic language of nature, and
which assumes that a certain harmony exists between mathematical aesthetics
and Reality' (Lanczos, 1925b, pp. 148-149). He was convinced that 'the progress
of mathematical knowledge is simultaneously accompanied by an increasingly
more deeply penetrating knowledge of Reality' (Lanczos, 1925b, p. 149). One of
the most fundamental mathematical objects in the theory of integral equations
was evidently the kernel, K(s, a), and the question arose whether nature provided
any information about it beyond the eigenvalues \. Lanczos now argued in his
discussion of quantum mechanics:
If all physical facts are of such a nature that they can always, in principle, only
yield the coefficients of matrices, then the matrix representation deserves to be
preferred (at least from the positivistic point of view!), for it does not introduce any
fundamentally unrealizable element in the description of [empirical] facts. The
situation changes, however, if the kernel possesses a physical meaning. In that case
the field-like representation must be considered as the more adequate one, since the
matrix formulation provides less information insofar as it can only furnish the
eigenvalues of the kernel but leaves the system of eigenfunctions completely
undetermined. (Lanczos, 1926a, pp. 820-821)
Evidently Lanczos, in agreement with his work on the electromagnetic
radiation in a closed universe (Lanczos, 1925b, c), favoured the possibility that the
kernel function K(s, o) occurring in his fundamental equation (214) had a
physical significance and he made an effort to grasp it. He recalled the method
he had employed earlier in general relativity theory for determining the kernel
functions of the gravitational field (Lanczos, 1925a), and which he had later
taken over in the case of the electromagnetic field (Lanczos, 1925c): it was to
write down the differential equations for the fields, including all the source terms,
and thereby constructing the kernels of the equivalent integral equations, which
(i.e., the kernels) are identical to the Green's functions of the problem. By
analogy to these cases, the kernel K(s, a) of Lanczos' field-formulation of
quantum mechanics could be interpreted as a universal Green's function of the
theory; that is, the systems which it described embraced all the atomic systems

existing in nature (each of which could be regarded as a subsystem in the
approximation in which the interactions between the atoms were neglected).
Hence \, the eigenvalues of K(s,o), and the corresponding eigenfunctions
l(s)
at once gave the energy states of all the atoms in mutual interaction.
Lanczos was further aware of the fact that, in constructing the fundamental
kernel K(s, o) in quantum mechanics, one had to take into account the peculiar
features of the quantum-theoretical fields, such as their dependence on two space
variables, that were not common to classical fields. 'However, it must be noted,'
Lanczos remarked, 'that the kernel functions p(s,o) and q(s,o) are associated
with the system as the totality of all possible states, while a definite real quantum
state is characterized by the usual field functionspt(s) and q^s)9 (Lanczos, 1926a,
p. 823, footnote 2). The latter could be obtained by a space integration over the
fields as
Pi(s)= Jp(s9a)4>i(a)da9
(222)
ai(s)= fq(s,o)
\o)do.
Thus, the variables in the actual quantum states, pt(s) and qt(s\ were obtained by
folding the field kernels p(s, o) and q(s,o) with
'(s\ the eigenfunctions of the
fundamental kernel K(s, o).
Another unusual situation occurred with respect to the description of the
space coordinate, which appeared in Lanczos' theory in two ways: first, as a
dynamical variable q(s, a); second as a geometrical variable s or a. Although this
was a completely new, nonclassical feature of the theory that required proper
interpretation, and although he could not yet provide any, Lanczos was not
discouraged at all. Rather, he concluded his paper on the field-like representation
of quantum mechanics with the words:
This much, however, we do believe that we are allowed to conclude from the
possibility of bringing the entire theory [quantum mechanics] in full accord with our
field-like conceptions: that the modifications, which we must apply to our classical
views in order to reach an understanding of the quantum problems, must lie in a
totally different direction than could be characterized simply by the contrast
between continuum and discontinuum; and that the solution of the quantum
mystery should have hardly anything to do with a quantum-like re-interpretation of
geometry or infinitesimal calculus. (Lanczos, 1926a, p. 823)
With this conclusion he stated clearly that the new development in quantum
theory deviated from the line which it had taken during the previous years; not
so much the discretization of the classical laws had to be achieved, but a more
general modification which would exhibit both discrete and continuous features.
Lanczos himself expected firmly to be able to contribute to the establishment of
such a quantum mechanics.
V.2 Field-Like Representation of Quantum Mechanics
In his second paper on quantum mechanics, submitted to Zeitschrift für Physik
in the beginning of March 1926, Lanczos urgently pursued the problem of
determining the fundamental kernel function K(s,o) or the fundamental,
complete set of orthogonal eigenf unctions
l(s) (Lanczos, 1926b). He replaced this
task by the equivalent one: If one starts out from the variational principle, Eq.
(220a), in which K(s,o) only occurs folded with the kernel U(s,o\ one may
consider the fundamental set of eigenf unctions,
'(s), to be as much the solutions
of the integral equation (214) as of a similar equation with U(s,o) as the
kernel.276 Lanczos, therefore, concluded: 'It is not the energy function [i.e.,
K(s,a)] which we must consider as the given basis kernel, but the quantum
function [U(s,a)], and it must yield that system according to which the other
functions should be expanded' (Lanczos, 1926b, p. 408).
The advantage of reformulating the problem as the determination of the
fundamental set of eigenfunctions solving an integral equation with U(s, o) as the
kernel function was evident. First it was the nonzero kernel, U(s, a), the so-called
quantum function, which made the system under consideration, i.e., the universe,
a quantum system; hence U(s,o) had to be treated as the primary concept in
quantum mechanics. Second, unlike the energy function, which depends on the
details of what constitutes the universe, the kernel U(s, o) was a global function,
which was rather insensitive with respect to the specific properties of the systems
constituting the universe.277 Lanczos therefore believed that he had simplified the
problem considerably. Moreover, the new situation agreed better with the
principal idea which had guided his previous work on the quantum problem (Lanczos,
1925b, c): namely, the assumption that the structure of the universe, as expressed
by the set of eigenfunctions '(*?), was responsible for the deviations from the
classical description that were accounted for by the quantum functions U(s, a).
As for the explicit determination of U(s,o) or
'(s\ Lanczos was still not able to
report any progress when he submitted his third paper on quantum mechanics to
Zeitschrift für Physik in April 1926 (Lanczos, 1926c).278
276 In matrix mechanics this situation is reflected by the fact that the matrices corresponding to
the kernels K(s, a) and U(s,o) are simultaneously diagonal. Evidently, Lanczos took the kernel
function U(s, a) as being different from the unit kernel, E(s, a), because the latter did not allow one
to single out a fundamental set of eigenfunctions.
277 For example, the quantum function U(s,o) was the same for all systems having one degree of
freedom. For systems having / degrees of freedom, Lanczos proposed the same quantum function,
i.e., U(s,o) = ^fk = ](pkqk - qkpk)(s,o). Born, Heisenberg and Jordan—as well as Kramers, Weyl
and Pauli—had introduced the quantum condition for each degree of freedom; this corresponded to
U(s,o) = (pkqk — qkpk)(s,o), k = 1, . . . ,/. Lanczos believed that his definition had to be preferred
(Lanczos, 1926b, p. 407).
278 In this paper Lanczos investigated, especially, the possibility of representing the position and
momentum variables in matrix mechanics by real matrices, he found that in this case the quantum
function U(s,o) had positive and negative imaginary eigenvalues and its 'diagonal sum,' i.e.,
fU(s,s)ds, was automatically zero. As a consequence, however, to each energy state Wn there
corresponded another energy state — Wn. This seemed to be at variance with the spectroscopic
observation, since one would also expect term sums, Wn + Wm, besides term differences, Wn — Wm,
determining the frequencies. In the example of the perturbed harmonic oscillator, Lanczos proved
that the transition elements from states with positive energy to states with negative energy were zero;
thus, he showed that in this case no contradictions to the observation followed.

By the time Lanczos realized that his programme on a modified formulation
of quantum mechanics had become stuck, Erwin Schrödinger's first paper on
'Quantization as an Eigenvalue Problem' appeared in Annalen der Physik
(Schrödinger, 1926c). In his paper Schrödinger proposed a treatment of atomic
systems based on continuous field functions similar to those of Lanczos' integral
equation scheme. By imposing a boundary condition, Schrödinger obtained the
orthogonal set of eigenfunctions for a specific atomic problem, the nonrelativistic
hydrogen atom, as solutions of a differential equation. In his further papers,
which appeared in a rapid sequence, Schrödinger was able to treat other atomic
systems as well by solving their particular differential equations (Schrödinger,
1926d, f). Schrödinger's differential equation method was found to be equivalent
to Lanczos' integral equation method; especially, Eq. (214) could be rewritten as
a Schrödinger equation if the fundamental kernel K(s, o) were identified with the
Hamiltonian function H(s, a). Lanczos immediately recognized that by means of
this step his own field-like representation of quantum mechanics was completed
in some sense, and he refrained from further work on this theory. 'Afterwards it
was too trivial,' he recalled several decades later. 'It was no longer of interest,
because Schrödinger came along and he did it. As it often happens, it is the
second man who hits the nail on the head and not the first one' (Lanczos in
Mehra, 1973b, p. 287).
The Schrödinger-equation method completed Lanczos' integral-equation
reformulation of quantum mechanics in the sense that it made a specific assumption
about the nature of the fundamental kernel, K(s,o), which determines the
orthogonal set of basic eigenfunctions
'(s) according to Eq. (214). That is, one
now described different physical quantum systems by different sets of
orthogonal, normalized eigenfunctions, \pu(s\ where the latter obeyed a Schrödinger
equation involving a given Hamiltonian operator H (the equivalent of Lanczos'
Hamiltonian kernel function H(s,o)). Thus, the Schrödinger functions, i/>#0s),
characterize only the quantum states of a given system, like the hydrogen atom,
provided it can be considered as being isolated from the rest of the universe.
Since in our actual universe matter and energy are distributed very thinly, it was
plausible to regard atomic systems as being isolated from the rest of the world to
a certain extent. Therefore, the replacement of the kernel, K(s, a), by H(s, a), the
Hamiltonian of the atomic system under investigation, in Eq. (214) provided a
suitable description of the actual situation, and the success achieved by using the
Schrödinger equation confirmed this assumption.
Lanczos, however, was not psychologically prepared to pursue his programme
in this direction. For him the quantum problem had a cosmic dimension. In the
beginning of 1925, in his search for a field theory unifying gravitation and
electricity, he had concluded the existence of eigenoscillations in a closed
universe as offering the explanation of certain quantum effects observed in
electromagnetic radiation. Stimulated by the development of matrix mechanics
in Göttingen he had then thought of formulating the quantum problem on the
assumption that the universe as a whole was responsible for the behaviour of
atomic systems. This explains why he chose to identify the kernel determining the
V.2 Field-Like Representation of Quantum Mechanics
basic set of eigenfunctions with a universal quantum function, U(s,o), rather
than with specific Hamiltonian functions, H(s, a). Schrödinger's wave mechanics
did not achieve the goal Lanczos had set himself: it offered no way of calculating
U(s, a), and assumed it to be given instead.279 Lanczos, on the other hand, did
not entirely give up his plan and returned to it several years later.280
Lanczos' work on quantum mechanics attracted Heisenberg^ attention when
he was looking for a way of dealing with the position variables that do not
change periodically with time. But neither Heisenberg nor Pauli, who were both
interested in enlarging the formalism of matrix mechanics, made use of the
integral equation scheme to treat angular variables. And, surprisingly, Lanczos
himself never considered any systems other than the purely periodic ones which
had already been successfully treated by matrix methods. One reason why
Lanczos restricted himself in this way was that his picture concerning the motion
of particles stemmed from a generalized field theory; and the difficulty in that
theory was how to explain the existence of something which corresponded in
classical theory to the very small closed electron orbits in atoms.
Practically the most serious obstacle in Lanczos' approach was that it could
not help in solving the actual problems of atomic physics until the fundamental
kernel and the basic system of eigenfunctions had been properly defined. As
soon as Schrödinger's work helped to replace this kernel by the Hamilton
function or operator, the preferred approach to follow was based on his
differential equation rather than on Lanczos' integral equation. The direct impact which
Lanczos' work made on the quantum physics of the day was negligible; his idea
of representing matrices by continuous field variables was not acknowledged
279 In the Schrödinger equation the commutation relation is automatically satisfied on account of
the structure of the momentum operator (p = (h/2wi)(d/dq), where q is the position variable).
280In the years following 1926 Lanczos turned again to general relativity theory. Later, he became
interested in the Dirac equation and worked on it especially during the year in Berlin that he spent
close to his idol Einstein. The opportunity had arisen in 1928 because Einstein's assistant left and
Einstein was looking for somebody to assist him in his mathematical work. Leo Szilard, a Hungarian
physicist who was close to Einstein, intervened on behalf of Lanczos. 'At that time I was very much
interested in the equation of motion in general relativity,' Lanczos recalled later. 'It was a sideline of
his [Einstein's] investigations, so he thought it might be a good occasion for us to work together'
(Lanczos in Whitrow, 1972, p. 49). Lanczos was awarded a fellowship by the Deutsche Not-
gemeinschaft to spend one year in Berlin, where he had the 'greatest experience' of his life, seeing
Einstein 'almost daily and have discussions with him of both a scientific and a non-scientific nature.'
(Lanczos in Whitrow, 1972, p. 49).
During his stay in Berlin, Lanczos considered the possibility of deriving the universal quantum
function or quantum condition from a nonlinear generalization of the Dirac equation. In August
1929 his hopes of completing his field-like representation of quantum mechanics ran high. 'If the
possibilities surmised here should be capable of being realized, then quantum mechanics would cease
to be an independent discipline,' Lanczos remarked in conclusion of his renewed effort. 'It would fuse
with a deepened "theory of matter", which must be built upon regular solutions of nonlinear
differential equations, that is, ultimately merge into the "world equations" of the universe. The
dualism of "matter and field" would then be overcome just as the dualism of "corpuscle and
wave" ' (Lanczos, 1929, p. 493). Had Lanczos succeeded in deriving the quantum condition from field
theory, he would have been able to answer at the same time Einstein's question, namely, whether field
theory offered the possibility of solving the quantum riddle, in the affirmative sense (Einstein, 1923d).
He never did, nor did anyone else after him.

beyond a mention in footnotes.281 However, in the wider perspective of the
development of quantum theory, Lanczos' was the first field-like approach to
quantum mechanics; it raised many questions which, to some extent, were
answered only later in the context of the physical interpretation of quantum
mechanics and the systematic formulation of quantum field theory. In Lanczos'
long scientific career, his concern with quantum mechanics remained a brief
episode which lasted about one and a half years.282 He first made use of the
Schrödinger equation as late as 1930, when he applied it to calculate the effect of
very strong electric fields on atoms; in this problem the usual perturbation
methods failed and Lanczos developed an elegant mathematical method for
obtaining an asymptotic solution (Lanczos, 1930).
V.3 The Operator Mechanics
At the end of October 1925 Max Born left Göttingen for the winter semester to
give a series of lectures at the Massachusetts Institute of Technology in
Cambridge, Massachusetts.283 From 14 November 1925 to 22 January 1926 he
281 For example, Schrödinger referred to him in his paper on the equivalence of matrix and wave
mechanics (see Schrödinger, 1926e, p. 754, footnote 1). Note, however, that Schrödinger
misunderstood Lanczos' approach, claiming that Lanczos' theory would lead to the wrong eigenvalues (i.e., to
inverse energy eigenvalues). The same misunderstanding occurred when Pauli wrote to Jordan: 'As
for Lanczos, my considerations [concerning the relation of wave mechanics to matrix mechanics]
have only a few points of contact with his. He looks at a problem in which the energy values are the
reciprocal eigenvalues, while here the energy values are the eigenvalues themselves. Further, in his
treatment the functions, which like the Green's functions depend on two points, play an essential
role; functions like that are not used here. I really believe that Lanczos' Ansatz is not of much use'
(Pauli to Jordan, 12 April 1926). Apparently, both Schrödinger and Pauli did not think of the fact
that the eigenvalues of Hubert's integral equations correspond to the inverse eigenvalues of the
Schrödinger equation.
282 Lanczos' later scientific career developed as follows. He became Privatdozent at the University
of Frankfurt during the winter semester 1926-1927 and was appointed Extraordinarius for
theoretical physics at the same place by the end of 1932. Being on leave of absence at that time, however, he
did not return to Germany, but remained at Purdue University, Lafayette, Indiana, where he had
been a professor of mathematics since 1931. Lanczos remained at Purdue until 1946, working on
polynomials, Fourier analysis and mathematical methods. (In the year 1943-1944 he was on the staff
of the National Bureau of Standards, contributing to the Mathematical Tables Project.) In 1946 he
joined Boeing Aircraft Company in Seattle, Washington, as senior research engineer. From 1949 to
1952 he worked at the National Bureau of Standards' Institute for Numerical Analysis at the
University of California, Los Angeles. During 1953-1954 he returned to industry as a specialist in
computing with North American Aviation. Having spent the year 1952-1953 as a visiting professor at
the Institute for Advanced Studies in Dublin, Ireland, he was offered a permanent position as a
senior professor there in 1954. From then on he made his home in Ireland, marrying Ilse Hildebrand
in 1954. (He had been married earlier to Maria Rupp from 1927 until her death in 1939; they had a
son, Elmar, who was born in 1933.) From 1954 onwards he travelled frequently, lecturing at many
places, especially in the United States and in Hungary, the land of his birth. He died in Budapest on
24 June 1974.
283 Max Born had mentioned his plan of going to Boston as early as April 1924 in a letter to Niels
Bohr. At that time Born expected that he 'probably will spend half of the winter semester [1924-1925]
in America (Boston)' (Born to Bohr, 16 April 1924). The plan was finally realized a year later. In this

V.3 The Operator Mechanics 221
delivered thirty lectures: the first twenty dealt with the structure of the atom and
the last ten with the lattice theory of rigid bodies (i.e., of the solid state). They
were all published under the title: Problems of Atomic Dynamics (Born, 1926d). In
his lectures Born included accounts of the most recent discoveries of matrix
mechanics that had been made by Heisenberg, Jordan and himself and by Pauli.
At M.I.T. Born again met a young mathematician, Norbert Wiener, who had
visited Göttingen during the previous summer and whose work 'had attracted a
certain amount of attention' (Wiener, 1956, p. 108). Thirty years later Wiener
recalled Born's arrival as follows:
He was enormously excited about the new basis Heisenberg had just given for the
quantum theory of the atom. This theory was an essentially discrete one, and the
tools for its study consisted in certain square arrays of numbers known as matrices.
The separateness of the lines [i.e., rows] and columns of these matrices was
associated with the separateness of the radiation lines in the spectrum of an atom.
However, not all parts of the spectrum of an atom are made of discrete lines, and
Born wanted a theory which would generalize these matrices or grids of numbers
into something with a continuity comparable to that of the continuous part of the
spectrum. The job was a highly technical one, and he counted on me for aid . . . .1
had the generalization of matrices already at hand in the form of what is known as
operators. Born had a good many qualms about the soundness of my method and
kept wondering if Hubert would approve of my mathematics. Hubert did, in fact,
approve of it, and operators have since remained an essential part of quantum
theory. (Wiener, 1956, pp. 108-109)
The new stage of development of quantum theory was particularly favourable
for the collaboration between Born and Wiener, and just a few weeks later Born
reported to Bohr about its success: T have also, I believe, made fundamental
progress with it [i.e., quantum theory] with the help of a young mathematician,
Wiener, who has just recently developed the mathematical methods that one
needs for the theory of aperiodic motions' (Born to Bohr, undated, most
probably early December 1925).
Norbert Wiener, Born's new collaborator, was born on 26 November 1894 in
Columbia, Missouri. His father, Leo Wiener, an immigrant to the United States
from Byelostok in White Russia, soon after Norbert's birth obtained a position at
Harvard University, becoming a respected professor of Slavic language and
literature there. Leo Wiener was keenly interested in his son's education and
instructed him early in languages and mathematics.284 A child prodigy, Norbert
context, it should be mentioned that at M.I.T. the custom existed 'to invite from time to time
distinguished scientists and engineers to address its classes and students' (C. L. Norton in the
foreword to Born, 1926d). Thus, for example, Peter Debye had spent some time there in the
beginning of the year 1925 as a Foreign Lecturer.
284Though Leo Wiener and his wife Bertha, nee Kahn, had three other children, two girls and a
boy, the father's attention was very much devoted to Norbert. This resulted in a one-sided education
for the boy who, in addition, was plagued by nearsightedness and physical clumsiness. Norbert
inherited a somewhat aggressive character from his father.

made rapid progress in his studies, entering high school at the age of nine and
graduating in 1906 at the age of eleven. He then entered Tufts College in
Medford, near Boston, where he studied Greek, German, philosophy,
mathematics, chemistry and biology, and obtained his bachelor's degree in 1909. After
having enrolled in the Harvard Graduate School to study zoology, Wiener soon
changed both his field and his university and began to study philosophy at
Cornell University, where his father's friend, Frank Thilly, occupied the chair of
ethics. In September 1911 Wiener returned to Harvard as a candidate for a
doctorate in philosophy; for his thesis he chose a topic in mathematical logic and
obtained his Ph.D. in spring 1913 at the age of eighteen.
After his doctorate Wiener was appointed by Harvard University to a
travelling fellowship. He first went to Cambridge, England, where Bertrand Russell
became his 'chief teacher and mentor' with whom he 'studied mathematical logic
and a good many more general matters concerning the philosophy of science'
(Wiener, 1956, p. 21).285 On Russell's advice Wiener learned more about
mathematics, both in Cambridge and later in Göttingen, where he spent the summer
semester of 1914 while Russell was invited to lecture at Harvard.286 In Göttingen,
Wiener was exposed to an atmosphere of serious and sustained intellectual work,
and he retained many fond memories of his stay there: of the meetings of the
Mathematische Gesellschaft (The Mathematical Society) and the following bull
sessions ['Nachsitzungen9] in Cafe Rohn's, of the excellent reading room
established by Felix Klein, of the people he met in Göttingen and, above all, of his
appreciation of David Hilbert's universal outlook.287
Upon leaving Göttingen, Wiener continued his studies in logic, first with
285 Wiener attended two courses of Bertrand Russell, a reading course on Principia Mathematical
and another one, in which Russell presented his views of sense data (i.e., the data received by human
senses) and in which he introduced such topics as Einstein's theory of special relativity. 'Besides his
consciousness of Einstein's importance,' Wiener recalled, 'Russell also saw the present and future
significance of electron theory, and he urged me to study it, even though it was very difficult for me
at that time, in view of my inadequate preparations in physics' (Wiener, 1953, p. 194).
Besides Russell's lectures Wiener attended, for instance, a course given by Godfrey Harold
Hardy, starting out from mathematical logic and theory of assemblages and extending up to the
Lebesgue integral. Wiener found this course particularly satisfying because he had not received any
training in rigorous mathematics earlier. 'Hardy, however,' recalled Wiener, 'led me through the
complicated logic of higher mathematics with such clarity and in such detail. . . and he gave me a
real sense of what is necessary for a mathematical proof.' (Wiener, 1956, p. 22).
286Wiener had already been in Germany during a European trip with his family in summer 1901,
which took him to Cologne, Vienna and London. This time again his family came along; however,
unlike Norbert, the other family members spent most of their time in Munich.
287 Russell had suggested that in Göttingen Wiener might study mathematics with David Hubert
and Edmund Landau and philosophy with Edmund Husserl. While Wiener at least tried to follow a
course given by Hubert on differential equations and another by Landau on finite groups, he did not
know enough German to follow Husserl's courses on Kant and phenomenology.
Later, Wiener did not remember whether he had seen Felix Klein in summer 1914. However, he
became acquainted with two young mathematicians, Felix Bernstein and Otto Szasz. The latter
especially took care of him in Göttingen, and Wiener was happy to return this help many years later
when Szasz came to the United States.

V.3 The Operator Mechanics
Russell in Cambridge from fall 1914 to spring 1915, and afterwards, when war
conditions made it advisable to return to America, with John Dewey at
Columbia University in New York. During the academic year 1915-1916 he was invited
as a Docent Lecturer at Harvard to give a series of lectures on mathematical
logic, but he was not able to secure a position for himself in future there. Instead,
after spending three years on various jobs at various places, he finally obtained,
with the help of William Fogg Osgood—a colleague and friend of his father's at
Harvard—the position of an instructor of mathematics at the Massachusetts
Institute of Technology.288 Thus, in fall 1919, Wiener began his academic career
in earnest.
M.I.T. was not regarded as one of the best places for mathematics in those
days; mathematics served mainly to implement engineering training there.
However, this situation ideally suited the stranded logician Norbert Wiener, for he
began to appreciate the importance of applying mathematics to solve engineering
problems. Whenever he needed it he could, of course, draw upon the advice of
pure mathematicians from neighbouring Harvard and other universities. Thus,
for example, he obtained the first mathematical problem he worked on from
Irving Barnett of the University of Cincinnati. Barnett directed his attention to
the recent fruitful work on 'the generalization of the concept of probability to
cover probabilities where the various occurrences being studied were not to be
represented by points or dots in a plane or in a space but by something of the
nature of path curves in space' (Wiener, 1956, p. 36). Wiener became interested
in this and he investigated various possibilities of extending Lebesgue's integral
for that purpose, looking simultaneously for the physical applications of the new
technique. The papers which I wrote on this subject,' he remarked later, 'were, I
believe, the first to discuss anything new—combining the Lebesgue technique of
integration with the physical ideas of Gibbs' (Wiener, 1956, p. 39). Hence the
work on Brownian motion constituted the new instructor's first successful
contribution to mathematical physics.289
During his early years at M.I.T., Wiener frequently travelled to Europe and
collaborated with some colleagues there. For example, in Strasbourg he worked
with Maurice Frechet for a few weeks before the beginning of the International
288As Wiener related in his autobiography (Wiener, 1956), his lectures at Harvard (on a
postulational and constructive treatment of analysis situs) did not leave a favourable impression on
George David Birkhoff, who pointed out to Wiener many difficulties in his treatment.
In the years following the docentship at Harvard, Wiener held for a year an instructorship of
mathematics at the University of Maine in Orono; then he worked as a staff writer for the
Encyclopedia Americana in Albany, New York; in 1918 he joined the ballistics staff, directed by
Oswald Veblen, at the Aberdeen Proving Ground in Maryland; finally, at the end of the war, he did
some journalistic work for the Boston Herald.
289Wiener published his early scientific papers on mathematical logic during his Cambridge stay
in British journals; he continued to publish on various topics of logic, philosophy and mathematics
without, however, drawing much attention. Wiener's work on the path integration (Wiener, 1921a,
and later papers), on the other hand, was taken up and continued later by Bernard Koopman, John
von Neumann and G. D. Birkhoff.

Congress of Mathematicians in September 1920. He also presented a paper at the
Congress dealing with a normed vector space (Wiener, 1921b); this paper became
the first publication in a field that was later called Banach spaces.290 In 1922,
1924 and 1925 Wiener again visited Europe, renewing his acquaintances and
establishing new contacts with mathematicians in France and Germany. Thus, he
obtained a reputation abroad, especially through some work on the boundary
value problem in potential theory; and Leo Lichtenstein, in an addendum to his
Encyklopädie article on recent developments in the theory of elliptical partial
differential equations, remarked with respect to the boundary value problem:
'With these problems ... a series of papers by G. Bouligand as well as
H. Lebesgue and, in particular, by N. Wiener is concerned' (Lichtenstein, 1924,
p. 1334).291
Besides potential theory Wiener became more and more involved in another
mathematical problem that led to some of his more important work. The
problem in question arose when the electrical engineers at M.I.T. wanted to have
a proper mathematical foundation of communication theory, that is, of the
290The Polish mathematician Stefan Banach presented the idea of Banach spaces already in June
1920 in his doctoral thesis; however, he published his work only in 1922. Wiener, on the other hand,
took up the problem shortly before the International Mathematical Congress at Strasbourg, at which
he presented his results (Wiener, 1921b); he was stimulated to do this work by Maurice Frechet's
postulational approach to topology. Wiener conceded later that Banach had slight priority in
developing the concept. In 1920 the Banach spaces did not have any physical application; but later
Wiener discovered that the differential space which describes Brownian motion was very closely
related to them.
291 On his visits to Europe Wiener got to know especially Henri Leon Lebesgue and George Louis
Bouligand in France and Leo Lichtenstein, a distant cousin of his father, in Berlin. All three of them
worked on potential theory in which Wiener had become interested. Wiener had noticed that the
method of generalized integration, which had led him to work on Brownian motion, was also
applicable to the problem of boundary conditions in potential theory. (For the origin of this work, see
the story which Wiener told in his autobiography, Wiener, 1956, pp. 79-88, 90-93.) Lebesgue,
however, already had studied the problem of boundary conditions in 1913. In December 1923
Bouligand submitted a note to the Academy of Sciences, Paris, on the solution of the Dirichlet
problem, i.e., the problem of finding a solution of an elliptic differential equation with boundary
values which connect continuously to the values in the interior of the domain (Bouligand, 1924a).
Lebesgue took up the problem immediately and presented his own communication to the Paris
Academy in its session of 21 January 1924, in which he pointed out the conditions under which the
Dirichlet problem had no solution (Lebesgue, 1924a). In January 1924 Wiener also published a paper
on potential theory (Wiener, 1924a) in the Journal of Mathematics and Physics of the Massachusetts
Institute of Technology (which had been founded in 1922 and directed during its early years by
Wiener and then by his friend Philip Franklin). When he saw the notes of the French mathematicians
in print in the Comptes Rendus, Wiener knew that he had to hurry if he were to compete successfully
in that field. Thus, he prepared a short note in French, assisted by a student who was better in French
than he, and mailed it to Lebesgue for publication in Comptes Rendus. It arrived just in time, for
Bouligand had meanwhile also obtained similar results and sent them to his former teacher Lebesgue.
In keeping with the best traditions of the Paris Academy the two papers were presented at the same
session of 24 March 1924 (Wiener, 1924b; Bouligand, 1924b), and Lebesgue added remarks on both
notes (Lebesgue, 1924b). Unlike many other scientific competitions, this one ended with Wiener and
Bouligand becoming friends and collaborators over many years. 'Bouligand was even more prepared
than I was to admit the somewhat greater completeness of my paper,' Wiener noted thirty years later,
'and we made arrangements to meet when I should come to France for a visit' (Wiener, 1956, p. 93).

V.3 The Operator Mechanics
theory describing the electrical oscillations which account for the transport of
messages along a telephone line, and Dugald C. Jackson, Chairman of the
Electrical Engineering Department at M.I.T., sought the help of his
mathematical colleagues. Until then the mathematical basis for dealing with this problem
had been the so-called harmonic analysis based on Fourier series and Fourier
integrals. It enabled one to deal either with strictly periodic oscillations or with
oscillations which had a very general structure, provided their amplitudes could
be regarded as being very small in the remote past and in remote future. The
electrical oscillations which occurred in a telephone line were of neither type;
hence it became Wiener's task to extend harmonic analysis for that purpose.
Wiener knew that the engineers had previously used with considerable success
another methcd in the same context: the operator calculus of Oliver Heaviside.
Wiener now combined the two methods: he generalized harmonic analysis, and
with its help he put the Heaviside formalism on a more secure foundation.
In order to understand Wiener's task we must briefly describe the operator
methods, especially those of Heaviside. Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz and Joseph-
Louis Lagrange may be considered as the founders of operator methods.292 Their
ideas were taken up and further developed in the late eighteenth and early
nineteenth centuries by numerous mathematicians; however, the lack of care in
handling the mathematical operations involved gave rise to many errors in these
early papers, and it became imperative to establish formal rules which were
satisfied by the specific operations in question. This was achieved during the first
half of the nineteenth century, principally by Francois Joseph Servois, Robert
Murphy and George Boole.293 As a particular example, the action of the «th
power of the operator D (= d/ di), i.e., the differentiation with respect to the
variable parameter /, on a function u(t) was considered, with n assuming both
positive and negative values or even rational values. It was this operator which
Heaviside used primarily in the electromagnetic theory with great success but, at
292 Leibniz noted the resemblance between the formulae of the «-fold differentiation of the
product x(t)y{t) of two functions, x(t) and/(/), with respect to the common variable /, on one hand,
and the nth power of the algebraic sum of x and/, on the other (Leibniz, 1710). The observation of
the 'modus operandi9 in dn(xy) is usually identified with the invention of operator calculus, although
its first explicit formulation is found in a much later paper of Lagrange (1772). Lagrange especially
proposed to use the calculus symbol in the same manner as an algebraic quantity.
293 While Servois studied the properties of the operations used (Servois, 1814), Murphy analyzed
the logical structure of the operator method (Murphy, 1837). In particular, Murphy distinguished
between what he called the 'subject,' i.e., the symbol on which a certain operation had to be
performed, the 'operation' itself represented by its own symbol, and the 'result' which may be
connected with the former two by the algebraic sign of equality. Thus, for example, in the algebraic
equation, ab = c, he considered the number a to be the symbol of the subject, b the symbol for the
operation of multiplication by the number 6, giving rise to c as the result. Murphy also noted that
some of the operations he had treated commuted with each other while others did not. Finally, Boole
not only turned operator methods into an effective tool for solving linear differential equations
(Boole, 1844), he also founded mathematical logic on them, thereby seeking to explain all of
mathematics on the basis of a few fundamental operations.

the same time, to the discomfort of his mathematically more rigorously oriented
contemporaries.294
Heaviside's operator methods were as unusual as his education. Oliver Heavi-
side, a self-made scientist, had published a large number of papers on various
aspects of electromagnetic theory before he recognized the importance of
operator calculus to the investigation of the transients and discussed the use of
'operators in physical mathematics' in detail in two papers published in
Proceedings of the Royal Society (Heaviside, 1893a, b).295 In the application of his
operator methods to solve problems in electrical circuit theory, Heaviside went
beyond everything which had been established in this field before him. The main
point was that in order to study the response to a given circuit having the
(complex) impedance Z,
Z(iu) = R + iuL, (223)
where R denotes the (real) resistance, L the (real) inductance and co the frequency
impressed upon the system (/ being the imaginary unit), he introduced the
step-function, H(t), defined by
„(,,.( 0 |or,<0 (224)
294The history of operator calculus is discussed, for instance, in the Encyklopädie article of
Salvatore Pincherle, who contributed himself many papers on that subject (Pincherle, 1906). It should
be mentioned that the early work on operator methods was frequently motivated by the desire of
mathematicians to remove a metaphysical interpretation of the differential calculus, i.e, to avoid
talking about infinitely small quantities, such as the differential dt\ this goal was certainly achieved
by treating, say, the symbols dt and d/dt like finite algebraic quantities.
The applications of operator methods, as discussed by Pincherle in his review article, were
confined to the theory of differential and difference equations and to selected problems of number
theory. If one operated with inverse powers of the differential operator D (= d/dt), one could also
treat linear integral equations. Heaviside's operator calculus was not mentioned by Pincherle.
295 Oliver Heaviside was born on 13 May 1850 in Camden Town, London, being the youngest son
of the artist Thomas Heaviside and his wife Rachel Elizabeth, nee West. Rachel's sister had married
the physicist and inventor Charles Wheatstone. However, Wheatstone did not influence his nephew's
education, and young Oliver learned physics entirely on his own. After leaving school, Heaviside
served as a telegraph operator with the Great Northern Telegraph Company in Newcastle-on-Tyne,
but he retired from this appointment in 1874 because of increasing deafness. Supported by his
brother and later on by well-wishers and a pension from the Government, Heaviside lived in a seaside
cottage in Devonshire. Although he had not had the benefit of a formal higher education, he trained
himself in mathematical physics and played an important role in the development of Maxwell's
electromagnetic theory and its practical applications. He published his first paper already in 1873 in
Philosophical Magazine and continued to contribute regularly in the following years. The value of his
work was only gradually appreciated by engineers and physicists—such as Lord Kelvin, who made
public some of Heaviside's results in his Presidential Address to the Institution of Electrical Engineers
in 1889—but his reputation was firmly established after his papers were collected and published as
separate books.
Heaviside was elected Fellow of the Royal Society in 1891. The Institution of Electrical Engineers
made him Honorary Member in 1908 and awarded him the first Faraday Medal in 1921. He received
an honorary doctorate from the University of Göttingen. Heaviside became a legendary figure long
before he died in his seaside cottage at Torquay on 3 February 1925. His name has been attached to
one of the reflecting ionized layers (of the ionosphere) surrounding the earth, whose existence he had
predicted in 1902.
V.3 The Operator Mechanics
as a characteristic measure of action. That is, H(t), the so-called 'Heaviside
function,' describes an external potential difference of one unit (volt), impressed
from the time / = 0 onwards. The response, A(t\ called the Hndicial admittance''
—i.e., the current resulting in the circuit because of the unit action—is then given
by the solution of the linear differential equation
Z(D )A (/) = H(t) with D = -j . (225)
The differential operator Z(D) in Eq. (225) is obtained from the impedance
function Z(/co), Eq. (223), by writing the operator D instead of the variable /co.
Heaviside claimed that Eq. (225) could be replaced by the operator equation
A(t) = QH(t), (226)
where the operator ß is just the inverse of the operator Z(D), and he proposed
several rules for the practical solution of this equation. For example, if it is
possible to expand ß into a negative power series in D, say as ^™=nbrD ~r (with
constant coefficients br\ then one obtains as solution for A (t) a positive power
series in the time variable /, i.e., ^,%„(l/r\)brtr for />0 (with the same
coefficients br). By carefully applying this rule and some others, Heaviside and
his followers were able to solve the differential equation (225) for almost every
electrical circuit under consideration.296
For a long time, Heaviside's operator calculus remained suspect for the
mathematicians. One of the reasons was that the step-function H(t) was not an
analytic function; hence the replacement of the differential equation (225) by the
operator equation (226) did not seem to be allowed under any conditions
whatsoever. Those who insisted on mathematical rigour, on clear definitions of
the operators and well-defined equations obeyed by them could not take
Heaviside's solutions seriously.297 Against these objections Heaviside held that
'mathematics is an experimental science, and definitions do not come first, but
later on' (Heaviside, 1893b, p. 121). He was completely vindicated, for soon after
his death in 1925 John Renshaw Carson and, independently, Norbert Wiener
provided a complete justification of his operator methods.298
296Of course, in practical cases the observation helped that if Ü is a sum of two operators, £2, and
ü2y and the solutions of Eq. (226) with these operators, namely, Ax(i) and A2(t), are known, then the
response A(i) may be obtained as the sum A](t) + A2{t).
297 By the turn of the century two methods had been developed to define operators in a rigorous
mathematical sense. First, linear operations of 'subjects' were considered as being similar to linear
transformations of a point in an «-dimensional (or even infinite-dimensional) space. Otherwise, one
used the method of 'Laplace transforms.' However, both methods were not applicable to deal with
Heaviside's problem.
298 Carson (1887-1940), an engineer of the American Telegraph and Telephone Company, worked
mainly on transmission theory. In a series of papers, ending with a long memoir (Carson, 1925), he
proved the validity of Heaviside's Eq. (226) by replacing it by the integral equation, [Z(p)]~]
= pf™A{t)exp(-pt)dt, with/? = /«. (The equivalence of this equation with Eq. (226) is obtained by
observing that the integral pf™ H(t)e\p(-pt)dt is unity; furthermore, it is assumed that all
derivatives of A{t) vanish at / = 0.) Then [Z(p)]~] represents a function similar to a Laplace
transform. With the help of the above equation Carson succeeded in establishing Heaviside's rules.

The earliest attempts to establish Heaviside's operator calculus rigorously was
based upon the use of integral transforms. It was found that, with certain
restrictions, the operator ß in Eq. (226) could be replaced by a well-defined
integral, and from it the response function A(t) could be obtained. For this
purpose the concept of the Laplace transform had to be extended in such a way
that it made sense to talk about the transform of the nonanalytic Heaviside
function.299 The possibility of an appropriate extension appeared to be likely in
the early 1920s, but a systematic mathematical theory had yet to be developed.
At this stage Norbert Wiener became interested in supplying the proof of
Heaviside's methods and results. He decided to achieve his goal by making use
of harmonic analysis because, in principle, the Fourier integral allows one to deal
with nonanalytic functions. However, he noticed at once that 'the functions to
which the Fourier integral applies are subject to severe restrictions at infinity'
and 'these restrictions are much more severe than those to which Heaviside
applies his method' (Wiener, 1926a, p. 560). He had, therefore, to generalize
harmonic analysis.
In seeking to formulate a consistent theory of electric circuits, Wiener had to
solve two problems: First, he had to extend the method of Fourier integrals in
such a way that all time-dependent functions (e.g., the external function H(t)
and the response function A{t)) could be represented as harmonic integrals, say
as /+£ /(co)exp(/co/)rfco; second, he had to define adequately the operators
occurring in the theory, especially the differential operator Z(D) appearing in
Eq. (225). If the former task were achieved, the latter would be straightforward,
simply because the differential operator D acts on a given harmonic component,
say /(co)exp(Zco/), like a multiplicative factor /co. However, it is difficult to
understand that the inverse operator [Z(D)]~l (which is identical with ß), when
applied to the step-function //(/), should yield results like the ones claimed by
Heaviside. The reason is that, for instance, the solution of Eq. (226) for ß—being
a series in inverse powers of D—involves integrals in which [Z(/co)]_1 has to be
expanded over the entire range of co. In spite of this difficulty Wiener succeeded
in his task. In particular, he was able to generalize harmonic analysis in such a
way that it applied to all functions /(/), which are integrable and square
integrable over any finite range of the variable / and, for large /, do not grow
faster than a finite power of /.30° And he showed further that a well-defined
meaning could be given to the action of all the operators on the functions that
Heaviside had envisaged (Wiener, 1926a).
The main idea, which enabled Wiener to carry out his programme, was to
approximate any function /(/) by functions fx(t\ whose expansion contained
only frequencies up to a given one, coA. The action of the operator ß on/A(/) was

300Evidently the step-function H{t) is a function of this class since for large t (> 0) it is constant
(and equal to unity).

230 Chapter V Modifications and Extensions of Matrix Mechanics
After successfully applying his generalized harmonic analysis to establish
Heaviside's operator calculus, Wiener continued to extend its use in different
directions, both physically and mathematically. In this way he discovered, for
example, that rather irregular functions, such as those which describe the time
independence of noise in an electrical line, or the intensity of white light emitted
by a continuous source, could be treated by means of the generalized harmonic
analysis (Wiener, 1926b, c). Then, however, arose the problem of investigating
the frequency spectrum of the function involved. In particular, Wiener became
interested in the problem of describing a continuous spectrum, and he succeeded
in doing so by again using the mathematical tool of Lebesgue's integral in a
functional space of paths, the latter having properties similar to the space which
he had introduced in the theory of Brownian motion (Wiener, 1926b, c). Not only
was he able to examine in many cases the 'spectral intensity' of the functions/(/),
but he also showed how an essential statistical feature enters into the analysis of
phenomena like white light.304 Although the work on generalized harmonic
analysis had a considerable impact on mathematical physics later on, it did not
influence the development of quantum mechanics; only by helping Born to apply
operator methods did Wiener contribute to the new theory in an important way.
This was the more surprising as in summer 1925 when Wiener had passed
through Göttingen and had presented there a seminar on his recent attempts at
generalizing harmonic analysis it had seemed that this new mathematical tool
might be appropriate for formulating quantum-theoretical problems.305
304The problem of the analysis of white light in terms of the frequency spectrum had been studied
already a long time before Wiener, especially by Lord Rayleigh and Arthur Schuster. A major
difficulty had turned up when one wanted to explain why the intensities of two independent light
sources added, despite the fact that in electromagnetic theory it is the amplitudes of the waves that
add up. Lord Rayleigh had proposed that the reason was the following: different parts of a source
emitting a continuous spectrum are not correlated in phase, hence the interference terms in the
intensities drop out (Rayleigh, 1880). Schuster, on the other hand, had introduced special methods to
perform the analysis of light and its intensities with the help of the so-called 'periodogram' (Schuster,
1894, 1897).
In 1926 Wiener finally put Schuster's intuitive procedure on a secure mathematical foundation. In
agreement with Schuster's intent, he considered all Lebesgue measurable functions /(/) which were
square integrable and derived their spectral intensities.
305 Wiener had revisited Göttingen, the place where he had studied in 1914, on his European trip
in 1924. At that time he had found that his results on potential theory interested the Göttingen
mathematicians, especially David Hilbert and Richard Courant. The interaction between Wiener and
the Göttingen scientists became stronger a year later. As Wiener recalled: in 1925, after a little
mountain climbing with [J. W.] Alexander, of Princeton, I again visited Göttingen, where my work on
generalized harmonic analysis was beginning to attract real attention' (Wiener, 1956, pp. 95-96).
Wiener gave a seminar on this subject. There is an interesting episode connected with this seminar.
When, after the session, people gathered in Cafe Rohrts to have supper, Hilbert is reported to have
said:
The speeches that are given nowadays are so much worse than they used to be. In my time
there was an art to giving speeches. People thought a lot about what they wanted to say and
their talks were good. But now the young people cannot give good talks any more. It is indeed
exceptionally bad here in Göttingen. I guess the worst talks in the whole world are given in
Göttingen. This year especially they have been very bad. There have been—no, I have heard no
good talks at all. Recently it has been especially bad. But now, this afternoon, there was an
exception .... This afternoon's talk was the worst there ever has been. (Reid, 1970, pp.
169-170)
V.3 The Operator Mechanics
In fact, in the beginning of the summer semester of 1925 Max Born had
already turned his attention to the treatment of those phenomena in which all the
atomic motions could not be considered as being periodic. Especially, he had
studied, together with Pascual Jordan, the action of aperiodic electric fields on an
atom by reformulating the classical expressions for the resulting electric dipole
moment in agreement with the correspondence principle (Born and Jordan,
1925a). In their work they had encountered the problem of representing the
coordinates occurring in collision processes—in which the ingoing and outgoing
electrons and atoms move along approximately straight-line trajectories—in
terms of Fourier integrals. On hearing about Wiener's new mathematical theory,
Born had thought that it might provide a general method which would allow the
decomposition of all variables used for describing the motion of electrons in
atomic systems, including the position variables of electrons colliding with atoms.
Born, who was looking forward to visiting M.I.T. to give a series of lectures
during the winter semester 1925-1926, had hoped that he would be able to carry
on the work on aperiodic motion with the help of generalized harmonic analysis
and the personal assistance of its expert, Norbert Wiener. However, by early
November 1925, when Born arrived at M.I.T., the status of quantum theory had
changed considerably from what it had been earlier that summer; he now
brought along with him a new quantum theory, matrix mechanics, by means of
which the problems of atomic physics were approached in a manner quite
different from the one he had followed with Jordan just a few months previously.
In making use of the results of the theory of infinite Hermitian matrices and
quadratic forms, Born had recognized the possibility of being able to describe the
continuous spectra emitted by atoms; yet, in formulating matrix mechanics in
collaboration with Heisenberg and Jordan, he had not succeeded in developing a
suitable method on which the calculation of continuous energy states could be
based. An even more serious difficulty remained: the matrix methods failed
completely in dealing with the problems of atomic collisions. Born still hoped
that Wiener's mathematical technique, which the latter had presented in his
Göttingen seminar, might provide the key for dealing with these questions, and
he counted on Wiener's assistance during his stay at M.I.T. Norbert Wiener, who
had just then become interested in quantum theory, felt enthusiastic about
working with Born. He immediately recommended the use of operator methods,
the intimate connection of which with generalized harmonic analysis he had
already explored, as the appropriate tool for treating aperiodic motions.306
Wiener had been led to discover the deeper mathematical justification of
Heaviside's rules (in the operator theory of electrodynamics) by first recognizing
306When Wiener began to study operator methods he had in mind only the physical applications
to problems of classical electrodynamics. (See the discussion in the introduction of Wiener, 1926a, pp.
557-562; the paper was submitted in April 1925.) Even when he stayed at Göttingen in summer 1925,
he did not think of using his methods, whether of generalized harmonic analysis or of operator
calculus, in quantum theory. As he recalled later in his autobiography: 'What I did not realize at that
time was that my talk was closely keyed to the new ideas of physics which were about to burst into
bloom at Göttingen in the form of what is now known as quantum mechanics' (Wiener, 1956, p. 97).

that it was possible to define more general operations than had been employed in
the theory of integral equations up to that time. Now, in November 1925, he
directed Born's attention to the fact that the Hermitian matrices of matrix
mechanics 'could be regarded as operators, acting on vectors in a
multidimensional space, and he suggested a generalization of matrix mechanics into a
kind of operator mechanics' (Born, 1978, p. 226).307 Born was attracted at once
by Wiener's proposal because it connected well with the ideas he had developed
in Chapter 3 of the three-man paper (BHJ, 1926). There he had discovered a
considerable advantage in extending the matrix description of dynamical
variables in such a way that the infinite matrix, say (qmn) with m,n = 1,2,3, ... ,
always occurred in equations of the type
ym = S qmn*n > ™ = l>2>3 • • • • (229)
n = 0
That is, it acted like a linear operator, as the mathematicians would say, on a
sequence of variables or on a vector, (xn), having an infinite number of
components, in order to produce another such vector, (ym). From Hilbert's theory of
integral equations Born also knew that, if certain conditions were imposed upon
the matrix elements, qmn, and the vectors (xn) and (ym), Eq. (229) was equivalent
to an integral equation; but the restrictions were very severe and the matrices
used in matrix mechanics did not satisfy them. On the other hand, Wiener had
just shown that operator equations could deal with physical problems of
electrodynamics where the familiar integral equation methods failed (Wiener, 1926a).
Born did not yet know much about operator methods. He somehow felt that the
methods to be used had some resemblance to those of almost-periodic
functions.308 In any case, he entered into the collaboration with Wiener with
optimism. They were soon able to establish an operator formulation of quantum
mechanics which proved to be more powerful than the previous matrix methods,
especially in dealing with such problems as those occurring in the description of
atomic collisions. Before the end of the year they submitted papers on their
results, both in English to the M.I.T. Journal of Mathematics and Physics and in
German to Zeitschrift für Physik; these papers were identical in content and
appeared a few months later in print (Born and Wiener, 1926a, b).
Their first task was to find the relation between Born's algebraic equations
(229) and the corresponding operator equation that would arise in Wiener's
formalism. In order to arrive quickly at the result, the authors turned to a system
having only discrete eigenvalues, Wn (n = 1,2,3, . . . ). Then they claimed: 'In
307 The idea to connect infinite matrices with operators had already occurred earlier in the
mathematical theory of operators. As we have mentioned in footnote 297, the operators had been
interpreted as causing linear transformations of points in a finite- or infinite-dimensional space;
hence they could be described in principle by infinite quadratic matrices.
308He expressed this feeling in a letter to Niels Bohr, where he remarked: 'Please convey my
greetings to your brother Harald, and tell him that Wiener and I are using methods which are closely
related to his "almost periodic functions'" (Born to Bohr, undated, probably early December 1925).

very obvious physical interpretation, we may consider transformations of
functions x(t) of the time' (Born and Wiener, 1926a, p. 84).309 The functions x(t)
were defined by the series

309 In matrix mechanics the quantities or vectors (xn) or (ym) could not be related to a physical
quantity. One had just identified the (xn) as columns of the unitary transformation matrix which
brings the Hamiltonian matrix to diagonal form. (Note that in matrix mechanics the lowest value of
the indices m or n is 0 instead of 1.) Thus, the significance of the (xn) had remained 'still very
obscure' (Born, 1926d, p. 125). The German version of the above quote was even more definite, for
Born and Wiener remarked: 'In place of the transformation of any array of variables *,, x2, . . . , which
lack any obvious explanation, we may consider transformations of functions x(t) of the time, which can
be interpreted in physics more easily' (Born and Wiener, 1926b, p. 174). They did not mean that the
quantity x(t) had an obvious meaning; it expressed only the hope that a physical interpretation might
be easier to obtain.

With the help of this procedure a connection was established between the
algebraic equations (229) and the operator equation (229") provided, of course,
the expression on the right-hand side of Eq. (229'a) existed. However, for the
matrices (qmn\ which were considered in matrix mechanics, that was not
generally true; for example, if for the qmn one substituted the transition amplitudes of
the harmonic oscillator, which grow with increasing indices (m and n = m ± 1),
then the double sums in Eq. (229'a) would diverge. Therefore, Born and Wiener
dropped the requirement that the kernel q(t,s) should be represented by a
trigonometric series as in Eq. (229'a). At first, they decided to admit all integral
operators of the form (229"a), where the kernel q(t,s) is an arbitrary function,
compatible with the convergence of the integral on the right-hand side of Eq.
(229'). But even this generalization did not reach far enough, for Wiener knew
from previous experience that very important operations in physics (notably
electrodynamics) could not be represented in this way. Hence he proposed to
Born that Eq. (229), instead of being generalized into Eq. (229'), should be
replaced right away by an operator equation of the type of Eq. (229"); however,
the operator q should be an arbitrary linear operator, which in general would not
be written as in Eq. (229"a).310
Although Eq. (229") admitted operators, which were much more general
objects than the matrices which had been considered in matrix mechanics until
then, Born and Wiener claimed that 'there corresponds to every operator a
"matrix"' (Born and Wiener, 1926a, p. 86). In order to find the generalized
matrix corresponding to a given operator, say q, they first generated the function
q(t,W) of time, /, and energy, W, by writing
q(t, W) = exp(- ^p Wt\qexp( ^f- Wt\. (232)
The harmonic analysis of the function q(t,W) was then made into components
of the frequencies (V- W)/h\ the corresponding amplitudes, q{V,W\ were
given by
q{V,W)= Urn -L J_+TTexp( ~^fVt)q exp( ?f Wt) dt. (233)
The significance of these generalized harmonic amplitudes is not difficult to
recognize. If, in Eq. (233), the energy parameters, V and W, assume only discrete
values, Wm and Wn, respectively, with m,n = 1,2, . . . , then q(Wm, Wn) be-
310A linear operator satisfies the equation, q[cxxx(t) + c2x2(t)] = cxqxx{t) + c2qx2(t). Simple
examples of linear operators are the multiplication by a constant or by a function /(/).
That Eq. (229"), with q a linear operator, generalizes Eq. (229) can be seen at once from the
example of the unit operator (i.e., the operator which transforms the function x(t) into itself).
Evidently, it is a linear operator. On the other hand, when one seeks to represent this operation by the
integral equation (229'), one recognizes that no kernel, q(t,s\ which is a function of both time
variables, / and s, in the conventional sense, can be used to describe it.

V.3 The Operator Mechanics
comes identical with the matrix element qmn\ hence all the values, which the
function q(V,W) can assume in this case, comprise the quantum-mechanical
matrix (qmn)—or (q(mn)), as it was written in matrix mechanics—and the
operator possesses a genuine matrix representation. The function q(t, W) also has
a straightforward meaning: its value for the particular energy parameter, W
= Wn, becomes equal to the sum of elements in the nth column of the matrix
(qmnexp[(27ri/h)(Wm — Wn)]). If, however, one or both energy parameters
assume continuous values, the function q(V,W) describes a matrix with (one or
two) continuous indices, thus providing just the formalism which Born,
Heisenberg and Jordan had searched for in vain a few months earlier. In this formalism
the function q(t, W)—which Born and Wiener, in an obvious generalization of
the case of discrete energy values, called 'the sum of the line [i.e., column] of the
"matrix" of q corresponding to the value W of the energy' (Born and Wiener,
1926a, p. 87)—characterizes the system under consideration in the following
manner: namely, q(t, W) may be represented by a Fourier sum in case all energy
states of the system are discrete; or by a Fourier integral when the energy
spectrum is purely continuous; or by a sum of both when discrete and
continuous energies occur.311
The description in terms of operators could account for even more general
quantum systems than the ones treated earlier by Born, Heisenberg and Jordan.
However, the function q(V, W\ as calculated from Eq. (233), does not exist (in
the conventional sense) for all linear operators even if the associated function
q(t, W\ Eq. (232), is perfectly regular. An example of such an operator is the
differential operator D (=d/dt). Evidently, its associated function, D(t,W)
= (2iri/h)W9 is finite for all systems having finite energies; but its harmonic
decomposition, D(V,W\ is not a conventional function.312 Like D, many
operators, describing dynamical variables of systems having continuous energy
eigenvalues, have a 'matrix' which is singular in a manner similar to D(V, W). As
a result, conventional harmonic analysis, using either Fourier sums or Fourier
integrals, cannot be applied to the differential operator D or positive integer
powers of it in such systems, and one has to use a procedure similar to the one
proposed by Wiener for Heaviside's operators (see Eqs. (227) and (227a)).
Before developing their operator formulation of quantum mechanics, Born
and Wiener defined the Hermiticity property of operators. To begin with they
noticed that any linear operator might be expressed as a sum of terms, each of

After these technical preliminaries Born and Wiener turned to the equations
of quantum mechanics. They remarked: 'We represent a coordinate by a linear
operator q instead of by a matrix. This has the advantage of enabling us to treat
cases where the matrix representation goes to pieces, even though we introduce
the notion of a continuous matrix. We must seek for the meaning of the
statement that an atom is represented by a number of operators in the fact that
an atom is a mechanism the characteristic oscillations of which are in some way
given when the coordinate operators are determined' (Born and Wiener, 1926a,
p. 92). Similarly, they represented the momentum variable by an operator p and
required the quantum conditions to assume the form
M-W-^> (236)
where 1 is the unit operator, i.e., the operator which when applied to a function
yields the same function. In agreement with matrix mechanics Born and Wiener
further assumed that all operators representing the dynamical variables of a
quantum system were Hermitian.
With the above formulation, however, the description of mechanical systems
was not yet complete because, as Eq. (229") showed, the operators had always to
be applied to the functions x(t). Born and Wiener, therefore, associated with
each system a set of functions ('zugehörige Funktionen,' Born and Wiener, 1926b,
p. 182), [x(t)}9 and required that this set be closed with respect to all linear
operations occurring in the system. That is, if in Eq. (229") one replaced the
operator q by any other Hermitian operator describing a dynamical variable of
the same system, and applied it to any function x(t) of the set {x(t)}9 the
resulting functiony(t) should also belong to {x(t)}.315
313This situation is a generalization of the one in electrodynamics where the operators Z and £2
are only functions of the differential operator.
314Evidently, the 'matrix elements' of a Hermitian operator q must satisfy the condition
q(V, W) = q*(W, V). Born and Wiener showed that this is so provided Eq. (235) holds (Born and
Wiener, 1926a, pp. 90-91).
315The closure relation for {x(t)} ensures that all operations for a given mechanical system can be
defined in operator mechanics. As long as the physical significance of the functions x{t) is not
known, the closure relation remains the only requirement that can be imposed on them.
V.3 The Operator Mechanics
Born and Wiener now postulated that the laws of quantum mechanics could be
formulated in terms of the operators just as well as in terms of the matrices. In
particular, the Hamiltonian equations of motion for each pair of variables, q and
/?, formulated as the operator equations,
,_ 9H(P>1) _2mfrr, n„.
