Again, Eqs. (237) are a generalization of the corresponding matrix equations and,
in agreement with matrix mechanics, H is assumed to be an operator function of
the dynamical variables, having the same functional dependence on the operators p
and q as the classical Hamiltonian has on its dynamical variables. In addition, the
theory of canonical transformations and perturbation theory could be formulated
as operator equations, being an obvious generalization of the corresponding
matrix equations. Moreover, in analogy to matrix mechanics, the time derivative
of any dynamical variable, say the operator q, might be replaced by the
difference of operator products given by
q= Dq- qD, (238)
which was assumed to hold in all cases.316 From Eq. (238) the frequency
condition for the radiation emitted in the transitions between discrete states, in
the transitions between discrete and continuous states, as well as in the
transitions between continuous states, could be obtained. On the other hand, the
conservation of energy followed from Eqs. (237) because the time derivative of
the operator, H(p,q), becomes identical to zero.

In Born and Wiener's operator mechanics the Hamiltonian operator exhibited
properties which had no direct analogue in matrix mechanics. For instance,
because of the operator identity
H=^-.D, (239)

The expression on the right-hand side becomes equal to W for V = W and zero
otherwise. Hence, the 'matrix,' H( V, W\ associated with the Hamiltonian
operator, is automatically diagonal. However, this fact alone did not contribute to the
solution of the fundamental problem of operator mechanics, that is, to determine
the energy eigenvalues of a system. The reason was that as long as x(t), the
associated functions of that system, were not known, the energy eigenfunctions
could not be found by simply applying the differential operator, (h/2iTi)D, to
x(t). Since Born and Wiener were not aware of any procedure for obtaining
these functions, they were obliged to calculate the eigenvalues in the same way as
Heisenberg had done in the case of the anharmonic oscillator (Heisenberg,
1925c): that is, for a given system (determined, for example, by its Hamiltonian
//(/?, q) or the Newtonian equations of motion), they integrated the equations of
motion, taking into account the restrictions imposed upon the variables by the
Hermitian property and by the commutation relations. The operators occurring
in these equations were tacitly assumed to act on the functions x(t) or on their
harmonic components, *(co)exp(/co/), where the frequencies co were related to the
energy eigenvalues of the system as co = lirW/h. Thus, for a given operator
equation, an ordinary equation was obtained involving the still unknown 'Fourier
amplitudes' x(co):
With the generalization of matrix mechanics into operator mechanics Born
and Wiener hoped that those systems in which continuous eigenvalues occurred
could now be treated. Before pursuing this matter they demonstrated that the
new methods yielded the same result as matrix mechanics for the simple system
of the harmonic oscillator. They observed that the operator equation of motion
in this case,

From the operator solutions for p and q the authors calculated the functions,
q(t, W) and/?(/, W\ obtaining

and
p(t,W) = (2iiW)l/2. (251b)
Both assumed real values, apart from a time-dependent imaginary part in
q(t, W), which, according to Born and Wiener, could be neglected because 'if W
is large, h/Air{2iiW)x/1 is small'; moreover, 'in this case the sum of the terms in a
column really represents the motion' (Born and Wiener, 1926a, p. 98).324 The
'matrix' q(V, W) of the position operator is degenerate because, considered as a
function of the variables V and W, it is zero everywhere except when V assumes
sharply the value W. The function q(t, W) cannot, therefore, be represented by a
Fourier sum or integral, and the motion is free of any periodicity. Since the real
parts of q(t, W) and/?(/, W) showed the same dependence on time / and energy
W as the corresponding classical variables of an accelerated particle, Born and
Wiener considered them as the proper quantum-mechanical representations of
position and momentum variables. They concluded that: 'Our representation is
as close to the classical treatment as seems to be consistent with the foundations
of quantum theory' (Born and Wiener, 1926a, p. 98).
The quantum-mechanical solution of the problem of uniform motion, notably
Eqs. (251a) and (251b), which had been obtained by a straightforward
application of the operator methods, cleared an important puzzle of the theory of atomic
systems. This puzzle consisted in the observation that many features pertaining to
moving electrons and atoms could be described perfectly by means of classical
equations, though one expected that quantum theory would alter the description
completely. Max Born, who was deeply concerned with these questions and had
discussed them at length with Niels Bohr during the previous year, had good
reason to be pleased with the results of his collaboration with Norbert Wiener; in
particular, they had been able to demonstrate how, in spite of the restrictions
imposed by quantum theory—especially, the commutation relations, Eqs. (236)—
an unaccelerated mass-particle followed a classical trajectory. The position
function, q(t, W) in Eq. (251a), seemed to possess all the observed properties: in
particular, it yielded the correct space-time interpretation, as can be seen by
examining the structure of the 'kernel' function, q{t,s\ entering into the integral
equation of the type of Eq. (229'). Born discussed this point in a letter to Niels
324In Eq. (251a), we have corrected the second term, i.e., an error of a factor 2 in the denominator
under the square root. We have also replaced in the quotation the word 'row' by 'column,' in
agreement with the (correct) German text {'Spaltensumme,'' Born and Wiener, 1926b, p. 187).
V.3 The Operator Mechanics
Bohr: This q(t,s) possesses even a much more beautiful physical significance
than the matrix,' he wrote. 'It is closely connected with the probability that the
coordinate q makes at the time / a quantum jump up and at time s a jump down.
In the case of the uniform motion in a straight-line, for example, q(t,s) is zero
everywhere except at / = s (one must formulate it mathematically correctly,
which is not difficult); that is, if a jump up occurs, then it is followed by a jump
down, hence no jump occurs at all' (Born to Bohr, undated, most probably early
December 1925).325 Born proposed to interpret this situation in the following
manner: 'In this, and only in this, case can one state that q(t,s) is not a
probability of transition [jumping], but also a coordinate, a position in the sense
of geometry. In all other cases the "positions" can be determined only
approximately; in those regions, where the probabilities of transition are appreciable,
one cannot fix any "position"' (Born to Bohr, ibid.). Thus, Born believed that the
operator formulation had provided the key to an understanding of the
relationship between quantum mechanics and the description of phenomena in space
and time. By pursuing similar ideas he hoped to resolve the difficulties which still
remained in incorporating the conception of the light-quantum in a consistent
atomic mechanics. He concluded that 'for the moment, however, our
mathematical formulae are in most cases wiser than we are; the formulae arise entirely
automatically, the interpretation is often [more] difficult' (Born to Bohr, ibid).
Born and Wiener's operator formalism seemed to open a new avenue for
approaching the problems of atomic physics. Thus, it should have been only a
matter of time and skill before the operator equations describing realistic atomic
systems would be solved. However, the events developed differently than Born
had foreseen. Almost simultaneously with the paper of Born and Wiener,
Schrödinger's paper on the wave equation appeared in print (Schrödinger,
1926c), and this equation proved to be a remarkably adequate tool for treating
atomic problems, which finished off any attempt of further pursuing the methods
of Born and Wiener. Many years later Born regretted the fact that he and

'matrix' q(V,W) as
q(V,W) =

dt.
The right-hand side of this equation describes an irregular function having the same structure as the
matrix of the differential operator D; i.e., q(V,W) is zero everywhere except when V— W. By
substituting q(V, W) into the equation for the kernel q(t,s) which is obtained from Eq. (229'a) by
changing the m, «-sums into integrals over V and W, one finds (apart from normalization factors) the
double integral fdVfdWq(V, W) exp[(2m / h)(Vt - Ws)]. One integration, say over V, is trivial
because it leads only to replacing the variable V by W. Hence q(t,s) becomes proportional to
fdWq(W, W)exp[(2m/h)W(t- s)]. The final step, the ^-integration, gives the function q{t,s)\

Note that we have replaced Born's notation of Q(t, t) by q(t,s), in agreement with our previous
notation (which is the one used in the paper of Born and Wiener).

Wiener had not developed operator mechanics in the direction of wave
mechanics. He remarked then: 'First we did the right thing. We expressed the energy as
d/ dt and we wrote the commutation law for the energy and time as an identity
by applying (td/dt — dt/dt) to a function of /. It was absolutely the same as for
p and q, but we did not see that. I shall never forgive myself, because if we had
done this, we would have had the whole wave mechanics at once, a few months
before Schrödinger' (Born, Conversations, p. 16). Born even went on to blame
Wiener for the clumsy parts of their formalism and lamented that: 'In one case
he saw that it was a simple differential representation [i.e., for the energy in terms
of d/dt\ but in the other case [i.e., for the operators/? and q] he insisted on the
very clumsy integral equation [Eq. (229')], which I did not quite understand. And
we could understand only after having the wave theory [of Schrödinger]. Then
we knew why the exponential factors [occurring, e.g., in the function x(t)] came
into these integrals' (Born, Conversations, p. 17).
Though one may sympathize with Born and Wiener's failure to see, in
November 1925, the connection between/?, the momentum operator, and d/dq,
the operator denoting the derivative with respect to the position operator, which
plays an important role in wave mechanics, several reasons can be given for their
failure. To begin with, one finds that nowhere in the Born-Wiener paper or in
Born's letter to Niels Bohr of December 1925 was there any mention of a
commutation relation between energy and time; Born and Wiener were therefore
not likely to notice the analogy of this relation with the standard /^-commutation
relations.326 Second, in order to consider a relation between the momentum /?
and d/dq as being analogous to the relation between the energy W and d/dt,
one has to regard the position variable q on the same footing as the time
parameter /. To Born, such a treatment would have been unacceptable because it
seemed to contradict the principle on which matrix mechanics had been
founded; namely, the fundamental difference between dynamical variables and
mere parameters. However, Schrödinger, who developed wave mechanics on the
basis of concepts quite different from those of matrix mechanics, introduced the
coordinate q and the time / on the same footing. In Schrödinger's theory, the
ideas of de Broglie concerning matter waves played an important role. It is
surprising that Born, who knew about the possibility that mass-particles might
exhibit wave properties—e.g., from Einstein's work on quantum statistics
(Einstein, 1925a), Louis de Brogue's thesis (1924e, 1925), and Walter Elsasser's
explanation of the Ramsauer effect (Elsasser, 1925)—did not think of it when he
dealt with the uniform motion of a particle in operator mechanics. Had he tried
326Of course, it would not have been difficult to find such a relation from the formulae of Born
and Wiener. For example, one might have just substituted for q the time / in Eq. (238) to obtain the
relation, Dt - tD = 1. And, with the help of Eq. (239), the equation, Ht - tH = {h/2mi)\, would
then have followed.
A commutation relation of energy and time was first considered by Wolfgang Pauli after he had
learned the results of Born and Wiener (Pauli to Heisenberg, 31 January 1926).

V.3 The Operator Mechanics
to incorporate the wave aspects into his formalism he might have been led to
consider operators which could be applied to functions periodic in both the time
and the position variables. However, in winter 1925-1926 de Broglie's waves did
not occupy his mind; in fact, he ignored a hint concerning them that had
occurred in the solution, Eqs. (250) and (251), for the position variable of the
uniform motion.327
As for Wiener, he had only recently become interested in quantum theory and
was not fully acquainted with its problems. The physical ideas involved in
formulating operator mechanics came from Born; Wiener executed the
mathematical parts of Born's programme on the basis of his knowledge of operator
methods.328 The path to wave mechanics and the Schrödinger equation was thus
not close to Wiener's thinking. Moreover, in Heaviside's electrodynamics it had
not been necessary to introduce—in addition to the pair of operators / and
D—another pair consisting of the position operator q and the differential
operator 3/3q. And the examples, which he treated in quantum mechanics with
Born, did not require such an extension either. Wiener always remained pleased
with the fact that, at the right historical moment, he had developed the
mathematical methods by which matrices could be generalized, and that with their help
he had assisted Born in solving an important physical problem in quantum
mechanics. He was also happy with the fact that 'operators have since remained
an essential part of quantum theory' (Wiener, 1956, p. 109). But in 1926 and 1927
when 'quantum mechanics went into an active phase of growth in which young
men like Heisenberg himself, Dirac, Wolfgang Pauli and John von Neumann
were making new discoveries almost every day,' he stayed out of it. He found
that: 'This feverish atmosphere is not one in which I function well, nor did I feel
the need of intervening in a subject which was already so completely handled'
(Wiener, 1956, p. 109).329
Nevertheless, it is surprising that in 1926, when the full power of operator
methods began to be displayed in wave mechanics, Wiener's name was no longer
associated with the advances in quantum mechanics. Wiener did go to Göttingen
in April 1926 and stayed there during the summer. Though this visit turned out
to be less successful than Wiener and his hosts had hoped, he did have a fruitful
327As mentioned above, Born and Wiener, in the position function, Eq. (251a), neglected the
imaginary term, (i/4'ir)(h/(2iiW)]^2\ because they considered it to be too small if W was large.
However, by introducing the de Broglie wavelength X (= h/(2fiW)]/2), the second factor in the
imaginary term is recognized to be identical with it. Hence the uniform motion is described by a real
position variable only when X can be neglected as being negligibly small.
328Born recalled later that at M.I.T. he was kept very busy with his lectures and was involved in
lots of discussions with other people, while Wiener spent much time in working out the results. Said
Born: 'He showed it to me and I made my remarks and suggestions' (Born, Conversations, p. 16).
329 In spite of Wiener's personal explanation, quoted above, it seems quite remarkable that
Wiener, who had had the opportunity of contributing so early to the mathematical parts of quantum
mechanics, and whose later work on harmonic analysis was closely connected with the foundation of
the theory, left the field entirely to John von Neumann. The latter began to contribute to quantum
mechanics only in spring 1927.

association with Born.330 Born, who was just then treating another problem of
aperiodic motion, the collision of atomic particles, though now with the methods
of wave mechanics, again received Wiener's assistance. He acknowledged it in a
footnote in the extended paper on the statistical interpretation of Schrödinger's
wave function in the following words: 'In the mathematical realization of this
work I have been assisted most kindly by Professor N. Wiener of Cambridge,
Mass.; I wish to thank him here and acknowledge that I would not have arrived
at the goal without him' (Born, 1926b, p. 805). This footnote of July 1926
described the actual role of Norbert Wiener in the formulation of quantum
mechanics more adequately than the complaints which Born began to harbour
against him later. At a time when just a few physicists struggled to develop a
consistent theory of quantum mechanics, the Born-Wiener collaboration not
only indicated the way for handling the problem of aperiodic motion but also
contributed to the physical interpretation of the theory. Wiener himself had no
reason to look back with regret on his work with Born, and he continued to
return to the problems of quantum mechanics whenever his kind of mathematics
seemed to offer an interesting and original application.331
330On Richard Courant's suggestion, made in summer 1925, Wiener had applied for a fellowship
of the John Simon Guggenheim Memorial Foundation. Upon receiving the award of the fellowship
for one year, he had planned to spend it entirely in Göttingen, where he expected a warm welcome.
However, when he arrived, he found that Courant was 'somewhat hostile' towards him. In his
autobiography, Wiener explained this change in Courant's behaviour by the fact that, before leaving
the United States, he had given an interview to an American newspaper, and Courant had learned
about its contents as well as Leo Wiener's anti-German activities during World War I. As Wiener
recalled; 'Courant scolded me for my newspaper publicity and was disposed to deny me the assistant
and the complete official recognition he had promised to me . . . . Nevertheless he allowed me to
continue in Göttingen in an unofficial way, and after some persuasion found an able young
mathematician to aid in the preparation of my lectures, provided I should pay him out of my own
pocket' (Wiener, 1956, p. 114).
In addition to the difficulties with Courant, Wiener's lectures were not successful at all. Born
recalled: 'When the students complained about his teaching, there was trouble between him and
Courant, which I tried to smooth out' (Born, 1978, p. 227). After the summer semester Wiener and his
wife (whom he had married just before leaving the United States) left Göttingen for an extended
honeymoon trip to Italy. They did not return to Göttingen; Wiener decided to spend the remaining
months of his fellowship with Harald Bohr in Copenhagen. He received a warm welcome from both
Harald and Niels Bohr.
331 Wiener kept up his interest in quantum mechanics for many years. For example, after his
return to M.I.T. in 1927 he collaborated with a new colleague, the Dutch mathematician Dirk Jan
Struik, applying the methods which the latter had used in differential geometry to differential
equations, especially the Schrödinger equation (see, e.g., Struik and Wiener, 1927, and further
papers). Wiener continued to publish papers on quantum theory occasionally, but then he stopped
suddenly for more than twenty years. In 1953 he returned to the subject and tried to formulate,
together with his student Armand Siegel, a new approach to the statistical interpretation of quantum
mechanics, based on the mathematical concept of 'differential space' which he had used in the early
1920s to describe Brownian motion (Wiener and Siegel, 1953, and later papers).
Wiener spent many years of his career to extending harmonic analysis. He had numerous students
and collaborators. For example, in 1932 he worked with the English mathematician R. E. A. C. Paley,
and they wrote a book on complex Fourier transforms (Paley and Wiener, 1934). Wiener had another
successful collaboration with Eberhard Hopf on certain specific integral equations, the so-called
Wiener-Hopf equations. The latter played, together with harmonic analysis, an important role in the
development of communication theory which occupied Wiener during the 1940s. This work not only
V.4 Action-Angle Variables and the Method of Complex Integration 247
V.4 Multiply Periodic Systems: Action-Angle Variables and
the Method of Complex Integration
Born and Wiener's paper on operator mechanics had an immediate impact on
Heisenberg who saw it about the same time as it reached Zeitschrift für Physik on
5 January 1926.332 Since November 1925 Heisenberg and Pauli had been trying
unsuccessfully to calculate the energy states of the hydrogen atom, taking into
account relativistic effects and perhaps the possibility that the electron possessed,
as proposed by George Uhlenbeck and Samuel Goudsmit, an intrinsic magnetic
moment. In order to achieve this goal, one had to compute the averages of
certain negative powers of the radius variable of the electron, and this could be
done only by introducing an angle variable , as in classical theory. Pauli, in his
matrix theory of the nonrelativistic hydrogen atom, had made use of the 'Lenz
vector' just because it had not been possible to express an angle coordinate as a
Hermitian matrix. Now Born and Wiener's work suggested to Heisenberg that it
might be possible to treat the angle, which is an aperiodic position variable, as an
operator. He was very happy about it and he proposed immediately, in a letter
written to Pauli on 7 January 1926, to treat the two-dimensional hydrogen atom
in terms of polar coordinates, the radius r and the angle . While r could be
considered, in its quantum-theoretical reformulation, as a genuine matrix, like
any other dynamical variable which had so far been used in matrix mechanics,
the angle
was certainly not a Hermitian matrix, although it could be defined,
according to Eq. (212), as a function of the matrices representing the Cartesian
coordinates of the electron (which, in classical theory, moves in a plane around
the nucleus). Furthermore, Heisenberg defined the azimuthal momentum/?^, the
canonically conjugate variable to the angle , by the equation
P
= xPy-yPx- (252)
The variable p^ could, of course, be represented by a Hermitian matrix like the
variables x,px,y and/y But since he was dealing with a formalism, in which the
variables (including the angle ) were represented by generalized matrices—
influenced the practical construction of computers, it also led Wiener to create a new mathematical
field, 'cybernetics,' dealing with the problems of communication and control in artificial machines as
well as in living organisms.
Wiener's accomplishments received increasing recognition. In 1929 he was invited to lecture on
his researches at Brown University. He spent the academic year 1931-1932 in Cambridge, England,
where his former teacher G. H. Hardy asked him to give a course on Fourier integrals. During
1935-1936 he visited China, lecturing at the Tsing-Hua University in Peking. In 1951 he served as
Fulbright Lecturer at the College de France in Paris and spent the academic year 1955-1956 as a
guest professor at the Indian Statistical Institute in Calcutta. In 1933 Wiener received the Bocher
prize for analysis. Shortly before his death President Lyndon Baines Johnson awarded him the
National Medal of Science. Norbert Wiener died in Stockholm on 18 March 1964.
332Heisenberg mentioned the 'recent work of Born and Wiener' ('neuere Arbeit von Born und
Wiener') in his letter to Pauli, dated 7 January 1926, which he wrote from Göttingen shortly after his
return from Christmas vacation in Munich.

notably the operators of the type which Born and Wiener had employed in their
paper—Heisenberg dropped the matrix notation completely.333 From the matrix
commutation relations for the variables, x and px, y and/^, respectively, and
using the formal expansion of the function defining  in Eq. (212), Heisenberg
obtained the commutation relation
M-«P* = ^j. (253)
for
and p^. Equation (253), which had to be considered as an operator
equation (including an operator 1 on the right-hand side), confirmed the
correctness of the definition of p^ through Eq. (252).
With the help of these results, Heisenberg was able to proceed with the
calculation of the average values of the negative powers of r (=yx2 + y2 ), the
radius variable of the electron (for motions in a plane). He noted that/?^ could be
replaced, as in classical theory, by the expression mer2
, where me is the mass of
the electron. Assuming that
, the time derivative of the angle, depends (again as
in classical theory) only on the principal quantum number of the atom, he
concluded that r~2, the average over a full period of the inverse square radius,
depended on the orbital momentum quantum number k in the same way as
(1//?^). Since the values of p^ were known from the spectroscopic data, the
quantity r~2, which entered into the calculation of the relativistic correction to
the hydrogen terms, could be evaluated. Heisenberg was very encouraged by this
result and continued to work along the same lines. On 14 January 1926 he wrote
again to Pauli, informing him about the progress he had made during the
previous week. Heisenberg had accepted to give a talk on 19 January before the
Kegelklub in Stade, close to Hamburg, and he planned to pass through Hamburg
the following day before returning to Göttingen.334 'I hope,' he wrote in
anticipation of his intended meeting with Pauli, (on 20 January), 'to spend many hours
discussing physics with you' (Heisenberg to Pauli, 14 January 1926). One week
after this meeting Heisenberg presented, in another letter to Pauli, a final report
on what he had learned about 'cyclical variables' ('das Kapitel "zyklische
Variable999) (Heisenberg to Pauli, 27 January 1926).
In his letter Heisenberg outlined the treatment of the problem of periodic
motion with one degree of freedom, which he formulated in terms of /, the
action variable, and w, the angle variable, satisfying the commutation relation
jw-wj=JL. . (254)
333 Born and Wiener used their quantum-mechanical operators to describe dynamical variables,
employing the same symbols as were used in classical theory. By applying the operator methods the
difference between the quantum-theoretical and the classical variables was so obvious that it did not
have to be emphasized by a different notation. We shall follow the same procedure in the following
sections and use the matrix notation only when absolutely necessary.
334See the letters from Heisenberg to Pauli, 24 December 1925 and 7 January 1926.
V.4 Action-Angle Variables and the Method of Complex Integration 249
Again Heisenberg considered the variables / and w in this equation as operators
in the sense of Born and Wiener. If it is assumed that //, the Hamiltonian
operator, depends only on the action variable /, then due to the equation of
motion (i.e., w = dH/dJ) the time derivative of the angle w does not depend on
time either. Thus, Heisenberg could write down the expression
w = /•«(/)) + w,(Z>), (255)
for w, where the operator co(D) denotes the frequency of the atomic system
(multiplied by In) and w,(D), the angle at time / = 0, both quantities being
independent of /. The Cartesian coordinates, x and^, representing the motion, as
well as their conjugate momenta, px and/^, are periodic functions in time, being
proportional to the exponential function exp(zVr), where t(=±1,±2, ...)isa
positive or negative integer. The action variable / then assumes only the discrete
values, Jn = const.+ «(/z/27r), n being a positive integer. By considering the time
derivatives of the periodic variables, Heisenberg obtained the relation

which includes, provided (tw) is interpreted as the transition frequency from the

Equation (256) and the fact that the action variable / assumes discrete values,
separated by integral multiples of (h/2ir), showed that an operator scheme of
action-angle variables, as proposed by Heisenberg, would indeed yield the
desired quantum-theoretical results for periodic systems. The important question
still remained whether it was justified to regard the Hamiltonian operator as a
function of the action variable only, that is, H = H(J). If the answer was
affirmative, and Heisenberg had little doubt that it was, he could prove his
earlier assumption concerning the average value of <£> (namely, that it depended
on the principal quantum number alone). Then he would be permitted to
calculate the average values of r~2 and r~3 for the hydrogen atom as he had
indicated in his letter to Pauli on 7 January.

In the meantime, Pauli, who shared Heisenberg's interest in developing a
complete theory of the hydrogen atom (that is, a relativistic hydrogen atom with
or without electron spin), 'had not remained idle' {'Ich war inzwischen auch nicht
müßig' Pauli to Heisenberg, 31 January 1926). He had himself pursued a
systematic formalism for treating action-angle variables. Unlike Heisenberg, he
did not employ the operators of Born and Wiener. The introduction of the
"operators,"' he commented in a letter to Heisenberg, 'is at any rate superfluous
for many purposes. They are related to the calculus with functions of "time" and
energy—which I shall sketch in the following—just as the theory of principal-
axes transformation [is related] to your matrix calculus. I am glad to have the
operators behind me, and shall now happily go on with my calculations' (Pauli to
Heisenberg, 31 January 1926).336
The principal idea involved in Pauli's formulation was to represent the
dynamical variables in quantum mechanics by functions of time / and energy W\
for instance, a position coordinate by q(t, W). Argued Pauli: 'The only essential
thing is that the quantities q(t, W) must be considered as continuous functions of
the energy, as soon as one deals with non-periodic quantities besides periodic
quantities' (Pauli to Heisenberg, 31 January 1926). Although the functions
q{t, W) were formally identical with Born and Wiener's generalized column-sums
(which they had denoted by the same letters), Pauli gave them a quite different
meaning. In particular, he remarked: 'In general the "time" / has, however, no
real physical significance, but only a formal one. For nonperiodic quantities
q{t, W) the connection with observable phenomena remains an open question;
there is no harm in this, if finally everything reduces again to periodic quantities
x, with the x(t, W) representing the sums of columns of matrices' (Pauli to
Heisenberg, 31 January 1926). Pauli emphasized the different, formal
significance of the 'time' variable in his scheme by postulating the fundamental
commutation relation
Wt-tW=-£-.\ (257)
between / and the energy W. Pauli conceived Eq. (257) as a generalization of a
matrix equation, as is evident from the use of the factor 1 (denoting something
like a unit quantity) on the right-hand side. The energy variable, W, embraces all
possible energy values of the system. If it could be represented by a matrix, W
would have to be identified with (WmSmn), the matrix obtained by diagonalizing
the Hamiltonian matrix of the system, and Eq. (257) would be a genuine matrix
equation. '
336There is little doubt that Pauli thought that the operator formalism was again part of Born's
formal erudition. He believed that he could develop a method serving the same purpose (namely, to
deal with aperiodic variables), but 'simpler and better than that of Born and Wiener' (Pauli to Bohr,
5 February 1926).
337 Equation (257) can be derived in Born and Wiener's operator mechanics from the
commutation relation of / and D (= d/dt) and the relation between the Hamiltonian operator H and D, Eq.
(239).
The idea that the time variable / might enter into quantum mechanics in a more 'formal' manner
than in classical mechanics occupied Pauli for some time. He mentioned it first in a letter to Niels

V.4 Action-Angle Variables and the Method of Complex Integration 251
In contrast to Born and Wiener, who had embarked upon the formulation of a
completely new mathematical scheme of quantum mechanics, Pauli wanted to
stick as closely to matrix mechanics as the physical situation would allow, for he
believed that the relations governing the dynamical variables, as represented by
matrices, were well defined, and their interpretation in terms of empirical facts
was clear. Thus, he proceeded to develop his formalism (or 'Kalkül') first for his
description of a system which had the discrete energy eigenvalues Wn, and whose
position coordinate was represented by all possible matrix elements, q(n,n — t) •
exp[27riv(n,n — t)/], where t assumed positive and negative integral values. 'In
order also to be able to calculate with non-periodic quantities (as, for instance,
polar angles), we must pay the price that the discontinuous index n is replaced by
as many continuous variables as the number of degrees of freedom which the
system possesses' (Pauli to Heisenberg, 31 January 1926). Restricting himself to
systems of one degree of freedom, he replaced the discrete quantities, x(n, n - t)
and v(n,n — t), denoting respectively, the amplitudes and transition frequencies
in the matrix scheme, by xr(W) and vr(W\ i.e., by functions of the (discrete and
continuous) energy W. Then he represented x, say the position variable of the
system, by the sum
+ 00
x(t,W)= 2 xT(W) ap[2*Vr(W)t], (258)
T= — 00
where the underlined exponential function had the special properties

and

Pauli emphasized that 'only the xT(W) possess physical significance via their
transition probabilities,' and he thought that / and exp[27ripT(W)t] were 'for the
Bohr on 17 November 1925. There he suggested that the time / did not have the usual meaning in
matrix mechanics, and that the frequencies in the exponential factors of the matrix elements were
also rather 'formal' quantities; in order to find the real time variable, he proposed to define it with the
help of energy rather than introduce it as an a priori given variable. Pauli came back to this idea in
fall 1926 when he discussed with Heisenberg the interpretation of the quantum-mechanical scheme.
Several years later, however, Pauli realized that the description of time in quantum mechanics by a
Hermitian operator made no sense; the difficulty arose, especially, when one wanted to describe a
system having both discrete and continuous values of the energy W (see Pauli, 1933c, p. 140,
footnote 1).
Finally it should be mentioned that Cornelius Lanczos also arrived independently at the
observation that /, the time variable of the matrix scheme, had a formal character. In his first paper
on quantum mechanics Lanczos stated, in particular, that the time derivative of Born and Jordan in
matrix mechanics did not refer to real motion, but rather served as the definition of a special property
of the Hamiltonian matrices describing dynamical variables; he, therefore, wrote the time derivative
in quotation marks (Lanczos, 1926a, p. 815).

Having thus shown that his formalism, based on the functions x(t, W)
provided a suitable extension of the matrix description of periodic variables,
Pauli turned to the problem of introducing action-angle variables for periodic
systems of one degree of freedom. Assuming that the energy of the system,
following a canonical transformation of the variables, could be written as a
function of /, the action variable, alone, that is, W — W(J\ he sought to
represent the angle variable w by the equation
w = a(J)'t + wl(J), (260)
where the factor co(J)—which, apart from a factor 2tt, is a frequency—is
determined by the equation of motion
dW(J)
«oo-^-äH- (261)
Equation (260) was completely equivalent to Heisenberg's operator equation
(255). Pauli also proved Heisenberg's commutation relation between J and w, Eq.
(254), with the help of Eq. (257), his commutation relation between / and W.
Finally, he concluded that vr(W\ the frequencies occurring in Eq. (258), which
in the case of a periodic system of one degree of freedom, depended on the
action variable alone—that is, vr(W)=vr(J)—,were the quantum-theoretical
transition frequencies.
Pauli's formalism for treating action-angle variables was not yet complete, for
he did not have a method that would allow him to transform the Hamiltonian of
a given periodic system into a function W(J). However, it was important for him
that 'now the non-periodic coordinates have been stripped of their horror' (Pauli
to Heisenberg, 31 January 1926). And he was anxious to apply the new methods
to the hydrogen atom to see whether they would work in this case. For the actual

V.4 Action-Angle Variables and the Method of Complex Integration 253
calculation, in particular to obtain the energy eigenvalues as functions of the
action variable /, Pauli found it preferable to extend the perturbation scheme of
matrix mechanics. Before, however, he was ready to publish any of his results, he
came across a paper of Paul Dirac's dealing with the same problems (Dirac,
1926a). Pauli was disappointed. He had worked hard and fast enough to obtain
an elegant formalism for treating action-angle variables, but Dirac had beaten
him in the race. Pauli complained to Kramers: 'In the March issue of the
Proceedings of the Royal Society there is a very beautiful paper of Dirac [Dirac,
1926a], which contains all the results that I have found since Christmas
concerning the generalization of the matrix calculus to non-periodic quantities (e.g.,
polar angles). I regret that in this way I have lost so much time; I could well have
done something else meanwhile' (Pauli to Kramers, 8 March 1926). Though Pauli
felt that Dirac's paper had rendered most of his efforts on action-angle variables
superfluous, he found that 'Dirac's calculations on the H-atom are, however,
unnecessarily complicated. It can be done much more simply according to the
methods of Wentzel and myself (Pauli to Kramers, 8 March 1926).
Gregor Wentzel, to whose work Pauli referred to Kramers, spent the winter
semester 1925-1926 at the University of Hamburg. He had been a member of
Sommerfeld's Institute since 1920, staying on there after obtaining his doctorate
in 1921, and had become a Privatdozent in 1922. He had worked mainly on the
X-ray spectra of atoms, which had also been the subject of his thesis (Wentzel,
1921a), and he had become an expert in this aspect of atomic theory as well as in
the theory of spectra of complex atoms.338 For instance, in a paper submitted to
Annalen der Physik in February 1925, Wentzel had thoroughly analyzed the
difficulties arising from the current theoretical explanation of doublet spectra
(Wentzel, 1925b), and his former teacher, Arnold Sommerfeld, had not hesitated
to call this account 'the best that has been written on the doublet-mystery of
optical and X-ray spectra' (Sommerfeld to Pauli, 18 June 1925). And Pauli, who,
having just expounded his exclusion principle, was struggling with the same
problem at the same time, had agreed with Sommerfeld (Pauli to Sommerfeld, 22
June 1925). In fall 1925, when Wentzel arrived in Hamburg, Pauli had completed
the calculation of the energy states of the hydrogen atom with the help of matrix
methods; he was very excited about the Göttingen scheme of quantum
mechanics and hoped to exploit the possibility of extending it to deal with more complex
atoms. The latter had been treated earlier by employing action-angle variables in
the theory of multiply periodic systems. However, the difficulty of being able to
represent angles in matrix theory seemed to prevent all progress. Wentzel was a
witness when, after certain not very promising attempts of Heisenberg and Pauli,
the important new hint as to how to proceed in the case of aperiodic position
variables was provided by the operator methods of Born and Wiener and, as a
result, Heisenberg and Pauli were able to envisage an action-angle formalism in
quantum mechanics. The programme of developing a unified scheme, in which
338Gregor Wentzel's background and his contributions to spectroscopy have been mentioned in
Volume 1, Section III.4, and footnote 609.

both periodic and aperiodic motions could be treated, reminded Wentzel of an
earlier work of his own on the same problem, a work in which he now discovered
certain parallels to the new ideas of Born and Wiener and of Heisenberg and
Pauli.
In a paper submitted to Zeitschrift für Physik in July 1924, Wentzel had
attempted to calculate the properties of X-ray bremsstrahlung spectra, that is, the
radiation emitted by electrons moving in hyperbolic orbits around the atomic
core or nucleus in accordance with the Bohr-Sommerfeld theory of atomic
structure (Wentzel, 1924d). In classical theory, the radiation from an accelerated
atom could be obtained from the kinematics of the electron. Wentzel had
proposed to take over the same formula into quantum theory, with the only
change that the acceleration v was replaced by a vector quantity q.339 He had
found this quantity in the following way (see Wentzel, 1924d, appendix). For a
system of /degrees of freedom a 'characteristic phase,' , is introduced as

Though Eq. (263') had been derived on the basis of correspondence arguments
long before matrix mechanics and operator mechanics made their appearance, it
was entirely compatible with the spirit of quantum mechanics. It represented
nothing less than a generalization of Pauli's expression (258) to nonperiodic
systems of / degrees of freedom. Because of this previous experience with
aperiodic motions and his considerable skill in formal, complicated calculations,
Wentzel was well prepared to enter into discussions with Pauli on action-angle
variables in the beginning of 1926. He quickly mastered the new ideas of matrix
and operator mechanics and began to build on Pauli's results on angle variables.
He directed his attention primarily to those aspects of the theory which
Heisenberg and Pauli had left out of their earlier discussions.342 He developed a general
theory of multiply periodic systems and answered, in particular, the question as to
how to obtain the energy of these systems as a function depending on the action
341 To calculate the bremsstrahlung intensities, Wentzel had studied the motion of an electron in
an elliptical orbit, described by action and angle variables, J and w. He had then taken over the
equations expressing J and w in terms of the energy W also for the hyperbolic motion, except that W
was always replaced by — W. With these results he had evaluated Eq. (263).
Note that Wentzel used as angle variables the angles divided by lit and action variables
multiplied by the same factor.

V.4 Action-Angle Variables and the Method of Complex Integration 257
Again Eq. (267) generalizes Pauli's result for periodic systems of one degree of
freedom.343 From it Wentzel concluded: 'If one requires, in addition, that the /'s
form a "closed set," it follows at once that they assume discrete values, separated
from each other by integral multiples of h/2ir9 (Wentzel, 1926a, p. 82).344 Finally,
again under the assumption that the energy is a function of the action variables
alone, i.e., W= W(Jk), and with the help of the commutation relations, Eqs.
(265), Wentzel showed that the variables q obey Heisenberg's multiplication rule
for quantum variables.345
One question still remained open in this scheme of action-angle variables:
Could general methods be established that would allow for the determination of
energy W as a function of the action variables for a given multiply periodic
system described by the variables qk and pk, k = 1, . . . ,/? The same method
would then yield directly, via Eq. (267), the transition frequencies of the system.
This question had not been answered thus far in quantum mechanics, not even
by Heisenberg and Pauli. Hence Wentzel noted in the introduction of his paper:
Tn Heisenberg's quantum mechanics [as also in the operator mechanics of Born
and Wiener] there still is not available a general procedure for calculating the
frequencies of spectral lines independently of their intensities' (Wentzel, 1926a, p.
80). However, after the introduction of action and angle variables the quantum-
mechanical theory of multiply periodic systems showed a great similarity with the
previous Bohr-Sommerfeld theory of atomic systems. It now occurred to
Wentzel that: 'Since only the frequencies were determined in the previous quantum
theory, one should ask the question: could somehow the old methods not be
translated in the language of matrices so that the terms [i.e., energies] can be
determined directly in the new theory as well?' (Wentzel, 1926a, p. 80).
In the classical theory of multiply periodic systems the problem of obtaining
the energy, say of a system of /(periodic) degrees of freedom, as a function of the

pk and qk, to the action-angle variables, Jh and wk. Expressed in terms of the
action-angle variables, the Hamiltonian will depend only on the Jk, which are
constants of motion; hence the energy of the system will be given in terms of the
values of the action variables. Thus, the energy of a classical system can be
obtained, and it was this procedure which Wentzel wanted to reformulate in
quantum theory.
In quantum theory all canonical transformations from one complete set of
dynamical variables of a given system into another complete set—according to
the investigations of Born, Heisenberg and Jordan (1926, Chapter 1, Section
4)—were unitary transformations. Since the angle variables did not admit of a
matrix representation, Wentzel reformulated the classical action function not as a
unitary matrix but as a more general object, say of the type of a unitary operator
in the language of Born and Wiener (i.e., an operator which when multiplied by
its Hermitian conjugate yields the unit operator), or of Pauli's functions of energy
and time, or as the ^-number objects of Paul Dirac.346 By referring to the analogy
argument, according to which the differential expressions of classical mechanics
are always replaced by the differences of products in quantum mechanics,
Wentzel suggested that the quantum-mechanical action function satisfies the

These results enabled Wentzel to reformulate in quantum mechanics the

Therefore, the closed path in the integral on the right-hand side of Eq. (271)

point, rmin, into the complex plane of r on one Riemannian sheet, that is, a little
below the real axis, until the second branching point, rmax, was reached; then the
path returned from rmax on the other Riemannian sheet of the square-root
function, that is, a little above the real axis to rmin. This path is denoted by (I) in
Fig. 1. Sommerfeld had proposed to evaluate the integral (271) according to the
rules of complex integration as follows. He turned the original path (I) into a
large circle (C), within which all singularities were contained. Then the integral
along the path (C) contributed nothing to the right-hand side of Eq. (271). But
this was not the entire result of the integration; there still remained integrations
to be carried out along the paths (IF) and (IF), which encircled the point
singularities of the integralpn Eq. (272), lying at r = 0 and r = oo, in the opposite
sense of the original path (I). Their contribution was obtained from the residue
formula (i.e., according to Cauchy's integral theorem) as
AS, = -2w/[Res(0) + Res(oo)], (273)
where Res(0) denotes the residue of pr at r = 0 and Res(oo) the residue at r = oo,

V.4 Action-Angle Variables and the Method of Complex Integration 261
Wentzel recognized that a similar situation arose in his theory of multiply
periodic systems (Wentzel, 1926a, §3). When the quantum system is separable in
the quantum variables, pk and qk, such that each/?^ becomes a function of the
conjugate variable qh only, then the quantum-mechanical action function also
becomes a sum of individual terms, Sk, k = 1, . . . ,/. Every Sk satisfies the /
equations,
SkJt - JtSk = \{pk(qkJl ~ J fa) + (qkJl - Jtqk)pk\ / = 1, . . . ,/, (274)
which follow from Eq. (269a) and the commutation relations between pk and qk.
Since the dependence of the momentum pk on the coordinate qk of a separable
system in quantum mechanics is the same as in the Bohr-Sommerfeld theory, pk
may be expressed as a square root of a function of qk similar to Eq. (272). Hence
one has to take into account the cut in the complex plane associated with the
quantum variable qk when evaluating the periodicity modulus AS, Eq. (270).
Wentzel assumed that in the complex plane associated with the quantum variable
qk a theorem similar to Cauchy's integral theorem would be valid; that is, the
function Sk9 defined by Eq. (274), returns to its initial value when the g^-path is a
closed curve in the complex plane within whose interior there is no singularity of
the functionpk(qk), but it will change its value when the closed g^-path encircles
a pole or a cut of the same function. In particular, Wentzel expected that: 'One
can, therefore, obtain the periodicity modulus of S, defined above, in the
following way. Instead of taking the path around the branch-cut one goes around
the poles of p in one of the Riemannian sheets, exactly as in Sommerfeld's
procedure of evaluating the phase-integrals Jpdq9 (Wentzel, 1926a, p. 84).
With this idea in mind, Wentzel first calculated the energy eigenvalues in two
cases in which the results were well known from previous matrix calculations: the
harmonic oscillator (Heisenberg, 1925c; Born and Jordan, 1925b) and the
nonrelativistic hydrogen atom (Pauli, 1926a). Both calculations provided a
welcome check upon his methods. However, Wentzel was particularly interested in
treating the problem of the relativistic hydrogen atom, in which Pauli had not
gotten anywhere with matrix methods. Wentzel succeeded brilliantly in
demonstrating in this example the power of the action-angle scheme and, especially, of
the reformulated method of complex integration. We shall now turn to it and
present the principal steps of his solution.
In the relativistic hydrogen atom an electron, of rest mass me and charge — e,
moves in a planar orbit (say, in the xy-plane) around the proton, whose charge is
+ e and whose mass is so much larger than me that its motion can be neglected.
The Hamiltonian, H, of the system is given as a function of the Cartesian
coordinates, x smdy, and their conjugate momenta,/?^ and/> , by means of the
equation
A2 + ^ = 2me(//+^) + ±(tf+^)2, (275)

In quantum mechanics the Hamiltonian and the Cartesian dynamical variables
are related formally in the same way as in classical theory; hence Eq. (275) is also
valid for the corresponding quantum variables, x,y,px,py and H. Again, one
can introduce spherical variables—the angle  and the angular momentum p^,

hence p^ is a constant of motion. Second, the quantum-mechanical relativistic
hydrogen atom becomes a separable system in the new variables, in whichpr, the
radial momentum, is given as a function of the radial coordinate r by the

V.4 Action-Angle Variables and the Method of Complex Integration 265

The significance of Wentzel's theory of multiply periodic systems may be seen
from two facts. First, it provided an effective method of dealing with complex
atomic systems, which Born, Heisenberg and Jordan had failed to obtain
although they spoke, in connection with the method of principal-axes
transformation, 'about finding methods for direct calculations of the energy without
explicitly solving the problem of motion: methods which correspond to Sommer-
feld's method of complex integration' (BHJ, 1926, p. 581). What had been
expressed as a wishful thought in the paper of Born, Heisenberg and Jordan, had
now been accomplished by Wentzel by doing the most straightforward thing:
translating Sommerfeld's method word by word into quantum mechanics.
Wentzel had thus opened the way for treating, say, the problem of the helium atom.
Although this aspect encouraged those who were interested in the helium
problem, Wentzel's method of complex integration was not used in later
calculations; in fact it was never applied beyond what Wentzel did himself, because the
Schrödinger equation proved to be much more effective for obtaining the energy
states of complex atomic systems.
The other aspect arising from Wentzel's work had, however, an historic
importance. Wentzel had re-established the connection between quantum
mechanics and the Bohr-Sommerfeld semiclassical theory of multiply periodic
systems, a connection which seems to have been lost completely in the matrix
approach. This connection expressed itself by the fact that the classical and the
quantum-mechanical descriptions approach one another in a way similar to the
one which Wentzel had suggested in summer 1924 with his formula, Eq. (263),
for the quantum-theoretical acceleration. That is, there occurs a smooth
transition from the quantum to the classical theory whenever the space-time
description enters into the quantum-mechanical formalism. Wentzel would clarify the
connection between the quantum and the classical theory, which was still
somewhat hidden in his treatment of multiply periodic systems, in his later
contributions to quantum mechanics, in particular in his discussion of the
problem of determining eigenvalues in wave mechanics by means of a classical
approximation (Wentzel, 1926c). Wentzel's work thus contributed to the physical
interpretation of quantum mechanics by showing its connection with the classical
theory.
As for the agreement between Wentzel's formula, Eq. (273'a), for the
periodicity modulus of Sr and Sommerfeld's classical result, <§>pr dr, it was not complete.
The reason was that Sommerfeld had assumed, of course, that the action variable
p^ takes on values that are integral multiples of h/2m\ in Wentzel's formalism,
however, the action variables were only determined up to a constant which had
to be obtained from further considerations. In a footnote at the end of his paper,
Wentzel added: 'According to a personal communication, Mr. Pauli has
obtained Eq. [(273'a)] by a perturbation calculation and thus found that the angular

momentum J^ must be normalized as half-integral, which, as is well known,
contradicts the original Sommerfeld interpretation of the hydrogen fine structure.
The agreement with experience is re-established by the fact that one ascribes with
[Uhlenbeck and] Goudsmit a magnetic moment to the electron (compare
Heisenberg and Jordan, in press [Heisenberg and Jordan, 1926]' (Wentzel, 1926a, p.
94, footnote 1).
V.5 The Electron Spin, Fine Structure and Anomalous
Zeeman Effects
In fall 1925 Uhlenbeck and Goudsmit had proposed to associate an intrinsic
magnetic moment with each electron in order to describe the observed complex
structure of atomic spectra consistently. This magnetic moment /i should be
related to s, the angular momentum vector of its 'proper' rotation, by the
equation
The ratio /i/s, i.e., the value e/mec (where me and — e denote the mass and
charge of the electron, respectively, and c the velocity of light in vacuo), was
assumed to be twice as large as the one obtained from the orbital motion of a
point-like electron. As a consequence of the hypothesis of 'proper' rotation or
'spin' of the electron, a new model of atomic structure had emerged. Especially,
the motion of an electron in the Coulomb field of a nucleus (having an atomic
number Z and a mass much larger than me) and the action of an external
magnetic field would be determined by a Hamiltonian H, which could be
expressed as a sum of four terms, that is,
H= H0+ Hx + H2+ //3, (279)
where the first, dominant term, H0, accounts for the nonrelativistic motion of the
spinless electron around the nucleus in the absence of the external field. The
other three terms represent perturbations and are interpreted as follows. The
interaction energy of the electron, having an orbital angular momentum k and
spin s, with the external magnetic field of strength H, is described by the term
Hl9 where
"l=2^H"(k + 2s)- <279a>
If the electron moves with a finite angular momentum k in the Coulomb field of
the nucleus, an internal magnetic field, H,, will be induced in the atom, which
acts on the magnetic moment /i of the electron, producing a further magnetic
perturbation term, H2, in the Hamiltonian of the system. To evaluate the field

H,, Heisenberg had assumed (see Heisenberg to Pauli, 21 and 24 November
1925) that the electron is at rest and the nucleus moves with velocity v. Then,
according to classical electrodynamics, H, is given by the equation
H = Ze(vXr)_Zek

we have replaced the vector product (v X r), multiplied by me, by k, the angular
momentum of the electron, which is me(rXvel) or -me(r X v) = me(y X r).)
Hence the internal field contributes a perturbation term H2,

to the energy of the atom, where the factor r~3 denotes the average over a full

corrections to the energy have to be considered as an additional perturbation
term, H3, which Sommerfeld (1915c, p. 470, Eq. (18)) had obtained in classical
theory and could be written as
