where H0 is the energy of the unperturbed nonrelativistic atom.
These equations were supposed to constitute the basis of the magnetic and
relativistic term splitting of the atoms. In spite of his reservations concerning the
electron spin hypothesis, which we have mentioned earlier (Section V.l),
Heisenberg had started already in November 1925 to put the entire model 'through
the matrix mill' (Heisenberg to Pauli, 24 November 1925). Towards the end of
the year he had reported to Pauli the preliminary results he had obtained in
collaboration with Jordan. Although these appeared to be rather encouraging,
the question had still remained open whether electron spin fitted into the
quantum-mechanical scheme or even made any sense at all. Bohr, for example,
was tempted to accept Uhlenbeck and Goudsmit's hypothesis as denoting
important progress in the theory of atomic structure, while Pauli raised any
number of objections. In between the two protagonists was Heisenberg, who saw
the advantages of using 'Goudsmit's theory'—as he called it (Heisenberg to
Pauli, 24 December 1925)—in order to explain the complex structure and the
Zeeman effects of atomic spectra. But he was very bothered by the apparent
failure of the theory to describe the splitting quantitatively, especially the factor 2
in Eq. (211) which spoiled the agreement between the observed and calculated
fine-structure results. On the other hand, Heisenberg had also noted that, before
one could reach a final verdict against the spin hypothesis, one had first to

evaluate consistently (in quantum mechanics) the averages, r~2 and r~3,
occurring in Eqs. (279b) and (279c). This task involved an extension of the matrix
scheme to include also a treatment of aperiodic variables, such as the angle
variables. During January 1926 Heisenberg and Pauli, following the hints from
Born and Wiener's operator scheme, had been able to develop the quantum-
mechanical action-angle theory far enough that they could apply it for the
purpose of calculating the desired average values. In particular, for a one-
electron atom (with atomic number Z) whose electron had the angular
momentum p^, they had obtained the results
(l) = me±, and (1)J1)—^—, (281)
where
denoted the average over a full period (of the unperturbed motion of the
electron) of the time derivative of the angle  (the canonically conjugate variable
to the angular momentum p^). The value of <£> was given by the time derivative of
the variable w, the canonically conjugate variable to the action variable J (= nh)
of the unperturbed atom, that is,
*-w--^T- + —^> (282)
where R = 27T2e4me/h3 is Rydberg's constant (see Heisenberg to Pauli, 27
January 1926). Since <£> was thus found to be independent of the angular momentum k
of the atom, a difficulty arose with the magnetic perturbation term, Eq. (279b), in
the lowest state of the atoms; namely, in the s-state the denominator
pl - {(h/lir)1—which is equal to k(k + \)(h/2ir)2, with k the quantum number
of the angular momentum k of the electron—in the expression for r~3 becomes
zero and H2 consequently infinite. 'For the [Uhlenbeck-] Goudsmit theory,
however, this result is very sad,' Heisenberg commented on it in a letter to Pauli.
'If you have an idea, do please write to me; I don't know what to do with the
Zeeman effects without Goudsmit [i.e., without the spin hypothesis]' (Heisenberg
to Pauli, 27 January 1926).
Pauli agreed with Heisenberg's calculations and the conclusions he had drawn
from them. 'Therefrom it follows,' he wrote back, 'first, that the numbers for the
sum Ere] + Emag [Pauli's Ere] and Emag were the quantum-mechanical terms H2
and //3, Eqs. (279b) and (279c), respectively] following from our equations are
certainly completely inconsistent with the observations . . . Hence it appears that
on the basis of quantum mechanics the fine structure does not come out correctly
either with the [Uhlenbeck-] Goudsmit electrons or with point electrons [i.e., the
electrons without "proper" rotation and intrinsic magnetic moment]!' (Pauli to
Heisenberg, 31 January 1926).
A few days later, in a letter to Niels Bohr, Pauli reported 'of the great
catastrophe, to which the new quantum mechanics leads concerning the fine
V.5 The Electron Spin, Fine Structure and Anomalous Zeeman Effects 269
structure of the hydrogen spectrum' (Pauli to Bohr, 5 February 1926).356 He
listed in detail the various difficulties which arose in explaining the fine structure
in quantum mechanics, both with a point-like electron and an Uhlenbeck-
Goudsmit (i.e., spinning) electron, and claimed that neither model accounted for
the empirical data; while the point-electron led, in connection with the observed
selection rules, to a theoretical inconsistency, the electron with spin yielded
quantitatively wrong results. The "moral of the story,"' he concluded, 'seems to
be in the following: // is true that one can try to associate with the electron a
preferred axis with angular momentum and magnetism; the interaction energy of
such an electron with a nucleus, as well as the interaction energy of two such
electrons—so far as it deviates from that of point electrons and depends on the
orientation of the electron axes—cannot, however, be simply and only represented by
magnetic dipoles* (Pauli to Bohr, 5 February 1926, Pauli's emphasis). In Pauli's
view these difficulties—which also included the problems of explaining the
singlet-triplet structure of the spectra of helium and alkaline-earth elements
quantitatively, as well as the exclusion of several equivalent electrons in atoms—
had to do with a problem, noted already by Uhlenbeck and Goudsmit, namely,
whether an intrinsic angular momentum of the electron of the order of h/Air and
the small mass of the electron were mutually compatible at all (Uhlenbeck and
Goudsmit, 1925, p. 954, second column, footnote 2). Hence he expected that only
a treatment of the electron theory on a deeper level would give a satisfactory
answer to these questions.
And yet, a more fundamental level of atomic and electron theory, which Pauli
envisaged in February 1926, was really not necessary in order to remove the
difficulties in the calculation of the fine structure of hydrogen spectral lines.
Remarkably enough, Pauli had already indicated the solution of the problem in
his letter to Heisenberg at the end of January, when he made what he called 'a
totally stupid remark' ('eine ganz dumme Bemerkung'): namely, if one assumed
the magnetic term arising from the action of the internal field, H2, to have only
one-half of the value given in Eq. (279b), then the diagonalization of the matrix
replacing the sum H2 + //3, where H3 is taken from Eq. (279c), would exactly
reproduce Sommerfeld's fine-structure formula. But where would one possibily
find the necessary factor of £? Therefore: "A half, a half, a kingdom for the
factor £,"' Pauli exclaimed, paraphrasing King Richard's desperate appeal
('Also: "Ein halb, ein halb, ein Königreich für den Faktor ^,"' Pauli to Heisenberg,
31 January 1926).
356 As we have mentioned in Section V.1, Pauli had discussed the spin problem very often with
Bohr, both in letters and conversations. Bohr had tried to convince him of the correctness of
Uhlenbeck and Goudsmit's hypothesis and also suggested certain guesses for the quantum-theoretical
values of H3 and H2, notably (see Bohr to Pauli, 24 December 1925) £mag = C[± {/k{k + 1)] and
£rei = C(k + \)/k{k + 1), with C = 8ir2Z2e4me/n3h4c2. Pauli, in his reply, had rejected these
formulae because of several reasons (Pauli to Bohr, 30 December 1925). First, he argued that the angular
momentum quantum number k was zero in an 5-state and, therefore, the fine-structure splitting
would be infinite; second, he insisted on a derivation of the terms H2 and H3 from a quantum-
mechanical calculation.

Pauli saw no possibility of getting the factor \ from the theory, but Llewellyn
Hilleth Thomas, a young visitor to Bohr's Institute from Cambridge, England,
soon found it.357 By a systematic relativistic discussion of the motion of an
electron around the atomic nucleus, Thomas noticed that in calculating the
internal magnetic field H, one was not allowed to substitute, as Heisenberg had
done in deriving Eq. (280), simply the nucleus-electron relative velocity v.
Rather, the rotational character of the electron's motion had to be taken into
account. Thus, Thomas found that the rest frames of the electron (orbiting with
velocity v around the nucleus) at two infinitesimally neighbouring times, / and
/ + dt, could be related to each other by an infinitesimal Lorentz transformation
with velocity d\ = \dt plus an infinitesimal rotation described by the axial vector
dw = (l/2c)(v X \)dt (Thomas, 1926; see also Frenkel, 1926). The field, acting on
/a, the intrinsic magnetic moment of the electron, was, therefore, not given by the
expression on the right-hand side of Eq. (280), but the term (mec/e)(dyi/dt) had
to be subtracted from it; that is, the effective internal field was only one-half of
the field given by Eq. (280). Consequently, Eq. (279b) for the perturbation term
H2 had to be replaced by the equation
h2=\ Hi (\ Y • ■>. (279b')
and the factor \ that Pauli had searched for in vain was provided.358
Niels Bohr, the advocate of the electron spin hypothesis for several months,
was extremely happy with Thomas' result and he immediately informed Pauli
about it. Thus, on the same day that Thomas completed his note and sent it to
Nature, Bohr wrote to Pauli:
Now, however, comes the surprise for the learned relativity theoretician and the
scientist laden-with-responsibility [in Danish: "laerde Relativitetsteoretikere og ans-
varstyngede Videnskabsmaend," that is, Pauli]. A young Englishman [Llewellyn
Hilleth] Thomas, who spent the past half year here, has recently found that the
entire problem of the unfortunate factor 2 rests probably exclusively on an error in
the calculation of the relativistic motion of the electron and of the nucleus. He has
tried to give an elementary presentation of his consideration in a note, which he
intends to send to Nature, and I enclose a copy of it with the request for severe
criticism. (Bohr to Pauli, 20 February 1926)
Pauli studied Thomas' note, but he was not satisfied at all. He especially
criticized two facts: that Thomas had not calculated the expression for the energy
357 L. H. Thomas, born on 21 October 1903 in London, became a student at Cambridge in 1921,
obtaining there his B.A. in 1924 and his doctorate in 1927. During winter 1925-1926 he spent several
months in Copenhagen as Isaac Newton Student; there he found the general relativistic argument in
favour of electron spin. In 1929 he joined Ohio State University as an assistant professor; he was
promoted to associate professor in 1930 and made a full professor in 1936. In 1946 he joined the
Watson Scientific Computing Center in New York City, and in 1950 became a professor of physics at
Columbia University. In his later scientific career, Thomas worked especially on electron distribution
in atoms and molecules and on the use of computing machines in solving scientific problems.
358Uhlenbeck and Goudsmit has also come across the difficulty concerning the factor { in
connection with the quantitative application of Eq. (279b). (See Bohr to Pauli, 20 February 1926.)

V.5 The Electron Spin, Fine Structure and Anomalous Zeeman Effects 271
term arising from the interaction of the electron's magnetic moment with the
internal field explicitly, but only the change of the angular momentum of
the electron; and that he had discussed the situation only in frames
moving with the electron and not in the frame where the nucleus is at rest. He came
to the judgment: 'I hold Thomas' conclusion that, "The difficulty of
explaining at the same time the spectral structures therefore disappears," to be wrong or
at least not to be sufficiently justified ... In any case, I regard the publication of
the present note by Thomas in Nature as a mistake, and would be glad if you
[i.e., Bohr] would prevent it or achieve a substantial change in the text of the
note' (Pauli to Bohr, 26 February 1926).
The reason for Pauli's strong verdict was that he did not believe that any
kinematical investigation could resolve the difficulty. He emphasized this belief
very clearly in his letter to Bohr by saying: 'But I have the strong feeling that in
this case absolutely nothing will be achieved by formal learning {"formaler
Gelehrsamkeit") and that, if the assumption of the magnetic electron really strikes
at the truth, a more detailed investigation of the structure of the electron and
especially a theory of the electron-mass (eventually by taking into account
gravitational fields) will be necessary' (Pauli to Bohr, 26 February 1926). Pauli
admitted at the same time that he did not yet have the courage to undertake a
study of these profound problems, though he did not consider it an 'a priori
hopeless undertaking' ('ein von vornherein hoffnungsloses Unternehmen,9 Pauli to
Bohr, 26 February 1926). Evidently, Pauli divided the problem connected with
the role of the electron's properties in the explanation of atomic spectra into two
classes: the fundamental problems included the theory of the electron's mass, the
basic idea for the calculation of the separation of singlet and triplet terms in the
helium spectra, and the physical reasons for the existence of the exclusion
principle; a more modest task seemed to be to derive the fine structure of the
hydrogen atom and the anomalous Zeeman effects from the same unified
assumptions concerning the properties of the electron. (See Pauli to Bohr, 5
March 1926.)
Niels Bohr, though he sympathized with Pauli's opinions, nevertheless tried to
convince him that Thomas' calculations were correct and had solved the problem
of the factor \ satisfactorily. In particular, he argued that the use of the
coordinate reference frames moving with the electron was justified (Bohr to
Pauli, 3 March 1926). Still Pauli did not give in; in his letter to Bohr, dated 5
March 1926, he again repeated his objections against Thomas' kinematic
treatment, claiming that the transformation of the results from the moving frames of
reference to the coordinate system in which the nucleus was at rest might lead
back to the old difficulties about the factor \. A few days later, on 8 March,
Samuel Goudsmit, who had spent several weeks in Copenhagen, passed through
Hamburg; he brought news about the details of Thomas' calculations for the
purpose of finally converting the nonbelieving Pauli. But his efforts failed
completely, and Pauli immediately sent a letter to Copenhagen, addressing it this
time to Kramers. Today Mr. Goudsmit was here and I was very happy to see
him,' Pauli wrote. 'What he told me about Thomas' calculations and Thomas'

manuscripts, which he had brought along, have strengthened my opinion most
strongly that I am perfectly right in the arguments, which I have tried to
formulate in my last two letters to Professor Bohr' (Pauli to Kramers, 8 March
1926). Even in greater detail than he had written previously to Bohr, he discussed
the question of the different coordinate systems, appealing to Kramers' thorough
knowledge of relativity theory and requesting him to check the details. This time
it cannot, I believe,' he wrote, 'be difficult at all to reach an agreement, because
we are not dealing with a religious or political matter, indeed not even with a
quantum-theoretical question, but rather with a problem of classical physics'
(Pauli to Kramers, 8 March 1926).
The last remark was correct: the decision whether Thomas' calculation gave
the correct answer or not was based on a classical argument. Pauli also did not
have to wait until Kramers replied. The answer was already contained in a letter
which Bohr had written soon after receiving Pauli's letter of 5 March. In
particular, Bohr had drawn attention to one fact: namely, that the goal of the
calculation had been to obtain the average value of the internal magnetic field //,
or the associated perturbation energy; hence it should not matter what specific
frame of reference one used, 'for the only thing, about which one can talk by the
very nature of the problem, is the change of the orientation of the electron's
[angular momentum] vector after one revolution in the electron orbit, where the
velocity relative to the nucleus is the same as before [the revolution]' (Bohr to
Pauli, 9 March 1926). Then, finally, Pauli gave in:
Many thanks for your letter of 9 [March] and the enclosed remarks of Thomas [he
replied]. Now all that remains for me is to capitulate completely ! ... In any case, I
must now answer both questions of your letter in the sense you desired: 1. Thomas'
calculation of the secular change of the electron's axis [i.e., of the electron's angular
momentum] is correct in the approximation he considered. 2. Of course, I always
had the opinion that the magnitude of this change is decisive for the fine structure.
The corresponding energy change of the atom basically states nothing else
physically; it must, I believe, always result formally automatically, if one describes the
associated secular perturbation by equations of motion of the canonical type. (Pauli
to Bohr, 12 March 1926)
The same day Pauli wrote a letter to Kramers, and on 13 March a postcard to
Goudsmit, in which he withdrew his reservations against the electron spin
hypothesis and acknowledged the correctness of the factor \ in Eq. (279b').
With Pauli's capitulation the path was clear to solve what Bohr and Pauli had
called the 'modest' part of the electron problem; namely, to perform quantum-
mechanical calculations of the anomalous Zeeman effects and the fine structure
of the hydrogen lines. In fact, Heisenberg had reported about these calculations,
which he had carried out together with Pascual Jordan, on 15 February 1926 at
the meeting of the Lower Saxony Section {Gauverein Nieder Sachsen) of the
German Physical Society in Braunschweig.359 All one had to do now was to bring
359See the announcement of Heisenberg^ talk, entitled 'Über die Anwendung der Quantenmechanik
auf das Problem der anomalen Zeemaneffekte' ('On the Application of Quantum Mechanics to the
Problem of the Anomalous Zeeman Effects'), in Verh. d. Deutsch. Phys. Ges. (3) 7 (1926), p. 13.
V.5 The Electron Spin, Fine Structure and Anomalous Zeeman Effects 273
in the factor \ obtained by Thomas. This was done by Jordan, for Heisenberg
had left Göttingen after the end of the winter semester for an extended vacation,
and a few days after 12 March, the date of Pauli's capitulation, he (Jordan) sent
the joint paper on the anomalous Zeeman effects—which also included the fine
structure of hydrogen lines—to Zeitschrift für Physik, where it was published
three months later (Heisenberg and Jordan, 1926).360
The main theoretical task, which Heisenberg wanted to achieve in the paper
with Jordan, was to calculate the eigenvalues of the quantum-theoretical
formulation of the Hamiltonian H, Eq. (279), with the perturbation terms given by Eqs.
(279a), (279b') and (279c), and to obtain from them the anomalous Zeeman
effects and fine structure of spectral lines. To achieve this goal, the authors
proceeded in several steps. First, they studied the question of whether the
well-known selection and intensity rules, which had been obtained from the
analysis of the anomalous Zeeman effect data, could be derived from the matrix
mechanical treatment of the problem. The calculation of the amount of magnetic
splitting turned out to be strenuous work, involving as it did the exact evaluation
of certain inverse powers of the matrix of the radial variable for realistic
three-dimensional atoms. With the extension of the matrix scheme to include
angle variables, such as the one developed by Heisenberg and Pauli in January
1926, the authors succeeded in obtaining the desired results, which they
summarized in the abstract of their paper in the following words: 'For explaining the
anomalous Zeeman effect, Uhlenbeck and Goudsmit have applied Compton's
hypothesis of the rotating electron. In the present paper [we] investigate the
quantum mechanical behaviour of the atomic model characterized by this
hypothesis. The result is that the Zeeman effects and the fine structures of the
doublet spectra can be explained completely by the said hypothesis' (Heisenberg
and Jordan, 1926, p. 263).361
The actual calculations of Heisenberg and Jordan were straightforward, for
they followed exactly the pattern of matrix methods which had been developed
360Since Heisenberg was on vacation, he did not immediately receive the news about
developments concerning the problem of electron spin. Thus, he did not witness Pauli's withdrawal of
objections against Thomas' calculation. On 4 April 1926 Heisenberg wrote to Jordan from Munich:
'Since the day before yesterday I am again back in Germany [probably from a skiing trip to Austria],
[and I am] filled with the best will to begin a new life and to understand again something of physics.
Could you be so kind as to write to me what you have done with our unhappy work [on the
anomalous Zeeman effects], whether you believe in the factor 2 [in the denominator of Eq. (279b/)],
and whether you have meanwhile brought into order the radiation theory and all quantum theory of
the world?' Jordan sent him the desired information immediately, especially a copy of the finished
manuscript on the anomalous Zeeman effects (which he had already submitted for publication three
weeks earlier), and Heisenberg wrote back: 'I admire your industry in rewriting our clumsy piece of
work' (Heisenberg to Jordan, 9 April 1926). Thus, he agreed with Jordan's final version of the joint
paper. Still, he wanted to know more details about the story of the factor 2, for he added: That you
do not write to me why Thomas is right with the damned factor 2 is a shrieking scandal' (Heisenberg
to Jordan, 9 April 1926).
361 Heisenberg and Jordan referred here to the fact that already Arthur H. Compton had
introduced the idea of a rotating electron in August 1921 (Compton, 192Id). Uhlenbeck and
Goudsmit took note of this fact in their later papers (Goudsmit and Uhlenbeck, 1926, p. 625,
footnote 2; Uhlenbeck and Goudsmit, 1926, p. 264).
274 Chapter V Modifications and Extensions of Matrix Mechanics
in the three-man paper and in Pauli's treatment of the hydrogen atom. In order
to diagonalize the quantum-mechanical Hamiltonian corresponding to Eqs.
(279), (279a), (279b') and (279c) with the help of perturbation theory, the authors
had first to determine the degree of degeneracy of the unperturbed Hamiltonian
H0.362 Although H0 is degenerate with respect to k, the angular momentum of the
electron, that is, the same energy eigenvalues Wn arise for different values of the
quantum number k (which runs from 0,1,2, . . . to /cmax, with /cmax = n - 1,
where n is the principal quantum number—see Pauli's treatment of the hydrogen
atom in Section IV.5), for the purpose of calculating the anomalous Zeeman
effect one may assume that it is a diagonal matrix in H0. The reason why this
assumption can be made is that Hx and H2, the perturbation terms given by Eqs.
(279a) and (279b'), respectively, depend only on k but not on its conjugate
(angle) variable; hence already infinitesimally small perturbations of the type Hx
or H2 will remove the degeneracy of H0 with respect to the values of k without
changing the other properties of the system. H0 is also degenerate with respect to
both the values of the spin s and the values of the total angular momentum M
(composed of k and s). Both of these degeneracies are removed if an infinitely
small external magnetic field H is applied in the z-direction.
There is, however, still one coordinate of the system with respect to which H0
remains degenerate, as can be seen from Eq. (279b') for the perturbation term
due to the interaction of the spinning electron with the internal field. The
expression for H2 contains both k and s but not their conjugate angle variables.
The only angle which occurs is the one between k and s, due to the scalar
product of these vectors; hence one of the angle variables will not be determined,
and H0 must be considered degenerate with respect to the degree of freedom
described by it. Heisenberg and Jordan chose this angle variable in such a way
that H0 became degenerate with respect to the values of sz, the z-component of
the spin s. Moreover, they observed that since the spin did not occur in the
perturbation term //3, Eq. (279c), the perturbation calculation had to be carried
out only with the terms Hx and H2, Eqs. (279a) and (279b'). The relativity term
H3 thus contributed merely an additive constant to the final result, depending, of
course, on the values of n (the so-called principal quantum number) and k which
the state under investigation assumed.
Heisenberg and Jordan now turned to the evaluation of Hx + H2, the sum of
the perturbation terms, Eqs. (279a) and(279b'), writing it as
Hl + H2 = li^(kz + 2sz)

not have proper matrix elements. (See Sections V.3 and V.4.)

assumes values ms(h/2m) such that k+m>ms> — k + m. For instance, if
5 = \ (which applies only when one electron is considered) and m = k + \, with

These formulae were identical with the ones obtained by Sommerfeld when he

accordance with the Bohr-Sommerfeld theory of atomic structure (Sommerfeld,
1922a; see also Heisenberg, 1922a, p. 284, Eq. (18)).364 The theoretical
understanding of the anomalous Zeeman effects in the old approach (which we
discussed in Volume 1, Chapter IV) was, however, fundamentally different from
the present calculation based on quantum mechanics; the coincidence of the
results obtained by so different means was, therefore, deemed fortunate.
Heisenberg was really 'excited and enthusiastic about the fact that the old formulae
of Voigt actually did come out of quantum mechanics' (Heisenberg,
Conversations).
The difference between the methods used by Sommerfeld, on the one hand,
and by Heisenberg and Jordan, on the other, showed up in particular in the
calculation of the intensities of the Zeeman lines. Voigt had discussed the

Heisenberg and Jordan's matrix calculation, in the case of s = \, accounted
perfectly for the observations of the anomalous Zeeman effect in alkali doublets,
and all the expressions obtained were equivalent to Sommerfeld's successful
formulae of 1922. The new theory described equally well the situation for the
spectra of alkaline earths. The latter have series of singlet and triplet spectral
lines when no external magnetic field is present, which may be readily explained
by assuming that the spin angular momenta of the two outer electrons
(responsible for the radiation) either subtract from each other to give s = 0 or add up to
give s= l.367 It is not difficult to show that the application of the matrix
mechanical perturbation scheme to the triplet system leads, in general, to a cubic
characteristic equation for the eigenvalues of Wm, the magnetic perturbation
energy; that is, each set of states denoted by the quantum numbers, k, m and s
(where s assumes the value 1), is normally split by the perturbation into three
states according to the three different values of ms (= -1,0,+1). Similar
considerations apply to the higher multiplets. In the case of the singlet, however,
one may disregard the spin altogether.
Heisenberg was also happy to derive another result, a 'summation principle'
('Summationsprinzip9), from the equations of perturbation theory. Let ml be the
smallest and m2 the largest value of ms admitted for a state described by the set
of quantum numbers, k, s and m, then it follows from the corresponding
characteristic equation that the sum of the energy terms is given by the
equation368

The right-hand side of this equation depends linearly on H, the field strength of
the external field. Heisenberg had already arrived at this 'summation principle' in
the special case of doublets and triplets in his very first treatment of the
anomalous Zeeman effects (Heisenberg, 1922a).
Heisenberg and Jordan drew further conclusions applicable to all multiplets,
that is, independent of the specific system under investigation, by considering

From Eq. (292) it also becomes evident that a difficulty, which had been

Equation (292), though derived strictly for only one-electron atoms, could be

V.5 The Electron Spin, Fine Structure and Anomalous Zeeman Effects 281
electrons]' (Heisenberg and Jordan, 1926, p. 277). In spite of Gregor Wentzel's
arguments against this conclusion (on the basis of a classical calculation),
Heisenberg remained convinced that the atomic model including spin would
allow one to treat the problems of complex structure consistently.371
The methods of matrix mechanics thus turned out to be very successful in
dealing with the multiplet structure and the anomalous Zeeman effects.
Especially, Lande's empirical rules followed automatically. The question remained,
however, whether also certain violations of Lande's rules, which had been
observed in connection with the so-called partial Paschen-Back effect (see Back,
1925b), could be explained in the new theory. The partial Paschen-Back effect
occurred if the magnetic field strength was large enough so that the condition
jit» A = X(k) was satisfied for one state (the one with the higher angular
momentum quantum number k) and not for the other state involved in the transition. In
that case certain Zeeman lines of the magnetic triplet arising from the
combination of P and D terms had not been found. Lucie Mensing then took up the
problem. By calculating the transformation matrix for the triplets according to
the procedure of Heisenberg and Jordan, she obtained the result that the element
for the transition from the state with quantum numbers j = k and m = 0 to the
state with quantum numbers ms = 0 and m = 0, ± 1 was zero (Mensing, 1926b).
Hence, according to matrix mechanics, the corresponding transition lines would
not occur, as was confirmed by Back's data. For doublet terms, on the other
371 In his analysis of X-ray doublets, Wentzel had previously separated two types of doublets: the
'relativistic' doublets whose separation was of relativistic origin but proportional to Z4 and
screening doublets whose separation was also of relativistic origin but proportional to Z4ff, due to the
'screening' of the charge of the nucleus by inner electrons (see Sommerfeld and Wentzel, 1921). In
spring 1926 Wentzel doubted that an expression of the type, (4w2e3R/ h2c2n3k(k — l))Z4ff, would
describe the separation of screening doublets, which could be phenomenologically described by the
formula
kv= R

-a(Z- 50)4 + r,(/i, k)a2(Z - s,)6 + T2(n, k)a\Z - s2)8 +
where s0, s,, s2 . . . were the screening constants and Tx{n,k), T2(n, k), etc., the coefficients depending
on the quantum numbers n and k, and a( = lire2/he) denoted the fine-structure constant. In March
1926 he submitted a paper in which he proposed to calculate s0 in the first term in the classical
manner, obtaining values which were considerably larger than the observed ones (Wentzel, 1926b).
From his calculations he concluded: it is difficult to find a plausible reason to explain the
discrepancy. Perhaps the potential law for the spherical shells [which he had used to describe the
screening effect to the order a] is not suitable in quantum mechanics to describe the interaction
between the electron and the shell in first approximation? Or perhaps the model of the top-like
electron [spinning electron] is still not sufficient to represent all the fine details of spectroscopic
phenomena?' (Wentzel, 1926b, p. 914).
Wentzel apparently favoured the second possibility. Heisenberg, on the other hand, did not trust
Wentzel's calculations, and he wrote to Jordan: i find Wentzel's paper on screening horrible; it
would have been a wonder if anything correct had resulted from it. I do not at all believe in physical
difficulties, say with the S-terms [in which Wentzel had found the largest discrepancies between the
theoretical and the empirical screening constants s0]. On the other hand, the mathematical calculation
is not easy' (Heisenberg to Jordan, 28 July 1926). Pauli also shared Heisenberg's point of view; he
argued, in particular, that Wentzel's occasional use of half-integral quantum numbers for the angular
momentum could not be justified on the basis of quantum mechanics including the spin hypothesis.
(See, Pauli to Wentzel, 5 July 1926.)

hand, Mensing's calculation showed that the transition elements were always
nonzero, and the experimental data did not indeed exhibit any anomalies in this
case. Moreover, with respect to the nonzero intensities of the lines, experiments
and the quantum-mechanical results agreed completely. Lucie Mensing, the
young lady who proved herself capable of performing the most delicate
theoretical calculations of anomalous Zeeman effects, was offered a post-doctoral
research position at Tübingen where Back and Lande were the leading lights of
atomic spectroscopy.
V.6 Key to the Helium Problem
It might seem rather surprising that Pauli, who had been concerned so much with
the programme of extending the matrix methods to include action-angle
variables and with the problem of explaining the fine structure of hydrogen, was not
at least co-author of one of the two papers, i.e., WentzeFs paper on the multiply
periodic systems (Wentzel, 1926a) or Heisenberg and Jordan's paper on the
complex structure and anomalous Zeeman effects (Heisenberg and Jordan,
1926). Being unhappy with the course of events, he just stayed away from any
publication. Indeed, in March 1926 Pauli had several reasons to be dissatisfied
with the progress of his scientific work. During the previous few months he had
wasted much time on arguments against the electron spin hypothesis until he
finally withdrew, in his letter to Niels Bohr (dated 12 March 1926), his objections
against the relativistic calculations of L. H. Thomas. Thus, an important
conceptual aspect of the theory of atomic structure, to which Pauli had devoted himself
since fall 1922, had been established by a graduate student from Cambridge,
England, in spite of his authoritarian criticism. At the same time, Paul Dirac,
another Cambridge research student, had developed the quantum-mechanical
scheme of action-angle variables much faster than Pauli, although a number of
the fundamental ideas from which both of them departed had been the same.
The explanation of this failure of such a seasoned theoretical physicist as Pauli to
compete with practically unknown graduate students like Thomas and Dirac
may be sought to a large extent in Pauli's attitude towards atomic physics in
general and the priorities he had set up for himself in approaching specific
problems.
Early in 1926 Pauli, more than anybody else working on quantum mechanics,
still believed the theory to be only in a preliminary stage of development.
Therefore, he still adhered to the cautious procedure he had learned from Niels
Bohr several years earlier when the previous atomic theory had run into serious
contradictions with certain empirical facts: namely, to look with suspicion at
every result obtained from theory and not accept it until it had been fully
established by agreement with experiment. It was in this spirit that he had
372Concerning this invitation Pauli wrote to Lande: i heard from Miss Mensing that you intend
to get her as an assistant. I am very glad about it; it will be very good for her' (Pauli to Lande, 2 June
1926),

V.6 Key to the Helium Problem
attacked, in October 1925, the problem of calculating the energy states of the
hydrogen atom with the help of matrix mechanics. Afterwards, since he was
convinced of the correctness of Sommerfeld's fine-structure formula of 1916, he
gave priority to extending matrix methods in such a way that the energy states of
the relativistic hydrogen atom could be obtained.373 When the puzzle with the
factor \ had been solved in favour of Thomas' calculation, the quantum-
mechanical derivation of the fine-structure formula became trivial and Pauli left
it to Heisenberg and Jordan to publish it in their paper on the anomalous
Zeeman effects (Heisenberg and Jordan, 1926). Another theoretical problem of
this kind—i.e., one in which the results could be tested by comparison with the
empirical data—was to calculate the intensities of the hydrogen spectral lines,
and Pauli had thought about it in particular since December 1925.374 In the
beginning of February 1926 this problem still concerned him, for he wrote to
Niels Bohr: 'At the moment I am occupied with carrying further the solutions of
the matrix equations for the hydrogen atom in order also to arrive at the final
formulae for the intensities' (Pauli to Bohr, 5 February 1926). During March and
April he continued his attempt but, even though he made use of the method of
action-angle variables, he did not achieve his goal.375
Pauli had been anxious to exploit matrix methods as far as possible, rather
than make use of a new formalism which might be more adequate to deal with
the intensity problem. Thus, he had not really examined the possibilities that
were offered in this respect by the operator scheme of Born and Wiener. Erwin
Schrödinger's first paper on wave mechanics (Schrödinger, 1926c) took him,
therefore, by surprise, for he had not expected that the operator methods would
lead to anything useful in practice. The set of functions, which Born and Wiener
had associated with the quantum-mechanical system under consideration (Born
and Wiener, 1926, §2), and which Pauli had scrupulously avoided in his
formalism dealing with angle variables, now reappeared triumphantly as Schrödinger's
eigenf unctions. However, Pauli made use of the new approach very soon; after
proving the equivalence of the wave and matrix methods, he was 'occupied with
the problem of computing from the eigenfunctions—calculated by Schrödinger
—the transition probabilities for hydrogen' (Pauli to Jordan, 12 April 1926).
Though he obtained certain results on the intensities of Balmer lines, he did not
publish them immediately, but rather waited for an experimental verification, in
373 How important Pauli considered the correct explanation of the hydrogen fine structure may be
gleaned from remarks in a letter to Sommerfeld, dated 9 February 1926. There he remarked: 'In any
case, I completely share the point of view which you adopted at the end of your paper [Sommerfeld
and Unsold, 1926], namely, no matter whether one tries to interpret the energy in the relativistic, or in
the magnetic, or in any other model-dependent sense, one must retain your 1916 formula for the
fine-structure levels quantitatively' (Pauli to Sommerfeld, 9 February 1926).
374That Pauli had begun to work seriously on the problem of calculating the intensities of spectral
lines becomes evident from a letter of Heisenberg's. In it Heisenberg wished Pauli 'a beautiful
Christmas and a good New Year, filled with hydrogen transition probabilities, theory of helium, etc'
(Heisenberg to Pauli, 24 December 1925).
375In March 1926 Pauli wrote again to Bohr; 'Meanwhile I have further laboured on the
intensities of Balmer lines, and still hope to be able to master this problem' (Pauli to Bohr, 5 March
1926).

spite of the fact that he knew that Schrödinger was calculating similar things and
he had to hurry.376 He did not have to worry long about the question of
publication. Schrödinger had included some preliminary results of his own
calculation on the intensities of Balmer lines in a communication which he
submitted to Annalen der Physik in the beginning of May (Schrödinger, 1926f, p.
476); Pauli informed Schrödinger about his results, and Schrödinger, in a
postscript added in proof to his paper, gave the full intensity formulae of the
hydrogen lines obtained by Pauli (Schrödinger, 1926f, pp. 489-490).
The continuing concern with the intensities of the hydrogen lines prevented
Pauli from working on other important problems of the day. After the question
of electron spin had been settled, and the formulae describing the fine structure
of the hydrogen lines and the empirically observed anomalous Zeeman effects
had been derived from a systematic application of the quantum-mechanical
scheme, one of the few remaining questions which concerned the complex
structure of spectral lines was the problem of explaining the helium spectra. Pauli
had hoped that Bohr and Goudsmit, who had been collaborating together in
Copenhagen earlier in 1926, would find a solution.377 However, no progress came
out of these efforts. As Goudsmit recalled later on about his discussions with
Bohr:
That was a complete fiasco. Bohr knew what the next problem was, [but] I did not
understand it; it was not [up] my alley. By looking at the helium spectrum Bohr
understood right away that [if] you turned over one of the two spins [of the
electrons in the helium atom] the energy suddenly would be entirely different. We
got together every day in the hope that his talking to me would put some idea in my
mind which would solve the problem. It didn't. So after five or six weeks he gave
me a first class ticket back to Holland on a sleeper . . . with the admonition that I
had to get off at Hamburg and tell Pauli about the Thomas factor 2. (Goudsmit,
AHQP Interview, 7 December 1963, p. 25)
376Thus, Pauli wrote to Lande; 'Recently I have derived—based on the connection between
Schrödinger's [work] and that of Göttingen—formulae for the absolute strengths of the Balmer lines.
Ladenburg shall check them with the help of dispersion measurements. Since, however, Schrödinger
is calculating similar things, I do not yet know where, how and when I shall publish the results' (Pauli
to Lande, 2 June 1926).
Since 1925 Rudolf Ladenburg had studied, together with his collaborators Hans Kopf ermann and
Agathe Carst at the Physikalische Reichsanstalt in Berlin, the anomalous dispersion formula. (See
Ladenburg's discussion remark in Meissner, 1925b, p. 689.) He had reported about his results on
various occasions, first on 14 February 1926 at the Lower Saxony Section (Gauverein Niedersachsen)
of the German Physical Society in Braunschweig, where Pauli had been present (see Verh. d. Deutsch.
Phys. Ges. (3) 7, 1926, p. 13). Later in 1926 he obtained preliminary results on the intensity ratio of
the two strongest lines in the Balmer spectrum; this value seemed to confirm the predictions of
Schrödinger and Pauli (Ladenburg, 1926b, p. 1213). However, the experimental errors were still
considerable, and it took another year until more accurate data were obtained.
377Samuel Goudsmit went to Copenhagen in February 1926 to work with Bohr on spectroscopic
questions arising from the incorporation of electron spin in atomic theory. 'Goudsmit was here during
the past 14 days,' Bohr wrote to Pauli on 20 February 1926, 'and he and I have discussed in detail
the question of atomic structure and of spectral laws . . . Goudsmit and I hope to write something
together in the near future on our views concerning these matters, and I shall report to you in detail
about it' (Bohr to Pauli, 20 February 1926).

V.6 Key to the Helium Problem
In Hamburg, Goudsmit did not succeed in persuading Pauli of the correctness
of Thomas' calculation.378 Moreover, Pauli was 'not very pleased to hear from
Goudsmit that he had not made the slightest progress in the two most important
problems [of spectroscopy], which are still unsolved; namely, the calculation of
the separation of singlet and triplet terms (e.g., ortho- and parhelium) and the
physical foundation of the formal exclusion rules for equivalent orbits' (Pauli to
Kramers, 8 March 1926). As Goudsmit recalled, he was out of his depth in
dealing with these questions:
I invented all kinds of magnetic interactions [to explain the large energy differences
between the corresponding singlet and triplet terms of the helium atom, i.e., terms
having identical quantum numbers n and k but different spin quantum number s\
and I have a letter from Bohr in which he tells me that I am all wet. But still I wrote
[to Bohr] about it later; I kept on thinking about it. Then he called [upon]
Heisenberg who indeed found the solution—the antisymmetric wave functions and
so on. That was way beyond me. I would never have been able to guess anything like
it. (Goudsmit, AHQP Interview, 7 December 1963, p. 25)379
From mid-November to mid-March 1926 Heisenberg did not submit any
paper for publication that dealt with new methods in or applications of matrix
mechanics. This, for Heisenberg, was an unusually long time, and he later tried
to explain the reason for his silence as follows: Tt was partly due to the absence
of Born [who was in America], partly due to the teaching which I had to do, and
partly also a kind of exhaustion. I felt, "Well, now let's wait for a time'"
(Heisenberg, Conversations, p. 302).380 Though Heisenberg's work with Jordan
on the treatment of anomalous Zeeman effects had been in progress for some
time, he did not wait around for the question of spin to be settled in favour of
Uhlenbeck and Goudsmit by Thomas' calculation; as soon as his teaching
obligations for the winter semester 1925-1926 were over, Heisenberg left Göt-
378 Goudsmit passed through Hamburg on 8 March 1926. (See Pauli to Kramers, 8 March 1926,
quoted in Section V.5.)
379In his letter to Bohr, dated 12 April 1926, Goudsmit proposed a strong mutual coupling of the
spins of the two electrons arising from the quantization of the sum of the two spin vectors, s, and
s2(which Goudsmit called Rl and R2). Bohr, in his reply, stated: i must point out, however, that it is
very difficult to see in your considerations a sufficient mechanical justification' (Bohr to Goudsmit,
17 April 1926). He claimed especially that there had to be a correspondence between classical and
quantum-theoretical forces; hence a strong spin-spin force had somehow to be understood on the
classical level. Bohr added that Kramers and Pauli, who were both in Copenhagen at the time, shared
his opinion.
380The three-man paper (BHJ, 1926) was received by Zeitschrift für Physik on 16 November 1925.
After that, on 21 December 1925, Heisenberg submitted a review article to Mathematische Annalen,
dealing with the foundations of quantum mechanics (which he had presented in lectures in Hubert's
mathematical seminar) (Heisenberg, 1926a). In the winter semester 1925-1926 he gave the main
lecture course on the kinetic theory of matter, replacing Max Born who was (in America) on leave of
absence. During that time the debate on the question of electron spin delayed the publication of
Heisenberg and Jordan's paper on the anomalous Zeeman effects (Heisenberg and Jordan, 1926).
Pascual Jordan also did not publish any paper during the winter semester because he was completely
absorbed in writing the book (with James Franck) on the excitation of atoms by collisions (Franck
and Jordan, 1926).

tingen for a vacation extending to March and most of April 1926.381 He needed
some relaxation, for he was looking forward to going to Copenhagen afterwards
to replace Hendrik Kramers as a member of the Institute of Theoretical Atomic
Physics—Bohr's Institute—and lecturer at the University. Having turned down
the offer of an associate professorship [or Extraordinariat] at the University of
Leipzig, he arrived in Copenhagen towards the end of April, refreshed in body
and spirit and full of new ideas and ambition.382
In Copenhagen, Heisenberg plunged immediately into one of the outstanding
problems being discussed there: the calculation of the energy states of the helium
atom. No progress had been made in it since about the middle of March when
Pauli had withdrawn his serious objections against the electron spin hypothesis
and written to Bohr: 'Now I only hope that my gibes about the "new heresy"
("neue Irrlehre") [i.e., the electron spin hypothesis] would have the result that
your zeal would be so incited that you would be able to finish off soon the
remaining points of the programme (helium spectrum, separation of the singlet-
triplet system, equivalent orbits)!' (Pauli to Bohr, 12 March 1926).383 Heisenberg
took his assignment very seriously and, within a few days of his arrival in
Copenhagen, reported to Pauli about his initial progress:
Then I want to write to you that we have found a rather decisive argument thai
your exclusion of equivalent orbits [i.e., of the electron orbits in helium denoted by
the same values of the four quantum numbers n,j, k and ms] is connected with the
singlet-triplet separation. Consider the energy written as a function of the transition
probabilities. Then a large difference results, if one—at the energy of H-atoms—has
transitions to 1S, or if, according to your ban [exclusion principle], one puts them
equal to zero. That is, par- and ortho-[helium] do have different energies,
independently of the interaction between the magnets. (Heisenberg to Pauli, 5 May 1926)
381 During the first part of his semester vacation Heisenberg did not occupy himself with physics
at all. On returning to Munich, he wrote to Jordan on 4 April that he was starting to do physics
again. He then learned from Jordan that the spin question had been decided favourably in the
meantime, as Thomas' calculation had been found to be correct. Bohr, in a letter dated 20 February
1926, had already informed Heisenberg about Thomas' calculation; Heisenberg, however, had not
been able to understand Thomas' note (Thomas, 1926), a copy of which Bohr had sent him. (See
Heisenberg to Bohr, 26 February 1926: 'Naturally, it will be a great success if one manages to
conquer the factor 2. I must confess, however, that—with all good will—I have not succeeded until
now in understanding the note.')
382 In a postcard to Jordan, dated 9 April 1926, Heisenberg mentioned that he would arrive in
Göttingen on the evening of 20 April, and leave on 24 or 25 April for Copenhagen. Before he could
do so, however, he received the offer from Leipzig and travelled, therefore, from Göttingen to Leipzig
and Dresden in order to discuss matters with the Leipzig faculty and the Saxonian Ministry of
Education on 25 and 26 April. (See the letter of Richard Courant to Niels Bohr, 24 April 1926.) The
position at Leipzig, which had been occupied by George Jaffe, was later offered to Pauli and finally
to Gregor Wentzel who accepted.
383After returning from Copenhagen to Hamburg, Pauli wrote to Wentzel in the beginning of
May about the state of affairs: 'In the case of spectra of the alkaline-earth type, there are still
unexplained difficulties: one does not see how the separation between the singlet and triplet terms,
which is many times greater than the triplet separation, arises [in the mechanical model]; moreover,
one does not yet have an interpretation for my "housing office for equivalent orbits" (" Wohnungsamt
für äquivalente Bahnen" [i.e., the exclusion principle]). (Both problems are perhaps connected.)' (Pauli
to Wentzel, 8 May 1925).

V.6 Key to the Helium Problem
And, just three weeks later, Heisenberg could write to Max Born who had
meanwhile returned from the United States: 'Since coming here I have very
seriously worked on [the problem of] the helium spectrum, and I believe that now
all the essential things are all right; however, I have not yet completed the
quantitative calculation' (Heisenberg to Born, 26 May 1926).
There were several reasons for Heisenberg's rapid progress in dealing with the
problem of the helium atom in quantum mechanics. First, he was evidently more
qualified to work on quantum mechanics than both Goudsmit and Bohr who had
tackled this problem earlier. Goudsmit's abilities, in any case, lay in the analysis
of empirical data, while Bohr did not have enough experience in applying the
complicated formalism of matrix mechanics, without which no progress could be
made. Second, Heisenberg had had a long personal association with the helium
problem ever since he had attempted, in fall 1922, to account for the observed
energy states of helium by using half-quantum numbers in the theory of multiply
periodic systems. While the half-quantum numbers had been discarded in 1923
as being in contradiction with the principles of the then quantum theory (see, for
instance, Born and Heisenberg, 1923b, p. 243), they had returned in fall 1925 in
the guise of electron spin. After Heisenberg had accepted the spin hypothesis he
could also reasonably expect that a repetition of his old, unpublished calculations
within the scheme of matrix mechanics would yield results in agreement with the
observed data.384
However, before being able to calculate the energy states of helium,
Heisenberg had to handle two difficulties which he stated in a letter to Max Born:
'1. Because of the equivalent orbits and Pauli's ban [exclusion principle],
quantum mechanics did not seem to be applicable to the problem of several-electron
atoms. 2. The large separation between the singlet and triplet systems could not
be explained by a magnetic interaction of the rotating electrons; one had
therefore to assume with Hund a large force of unknown origin between the
magnets' (Heisenberg to Born, 26 May 1926).385
384Heisenberg had reported about his calculation of the helium atom with half-integral quantum
numbers in letters to his teacher Arnold Sommerfeld during winter 1922-1923 (Heisenberg to
Sommerfeld, 28 October, 4 December 1922, 4 and 15 January 1923). At that time he had tried to
obtain the ionization potential of helium, and by assuming the angular momentum values of the
electron orbits in the ground state to be \h/2m and — \h/2m, respectively, he had obtained a result
very close to the empirical value of 24.5 V. (See Volume 2, Section II.3.)
385 A calculation of the usual (electromagnetic) interaction between the magnetic moments of the
two electrons yielded a splitting which was smaller than the splitting within a given multiplet due to
the interaction of the magnetic moment of one electron with the internal field H,, Eq. (279b7)- (See,
e.g., Hund, 1927, pp. 123-124.) This result had been noticed by Heisenberg soon after he learned
about the electron spin hypothesis, i myself have very great reservations against Goudsmit's attempt
to establish zoology [i.e., the spectroscopic terms] in such a simple way,' he had written to Pauli, and
added: 'For the alkaline-earth atoms the energy difference between singlet and triplet terms with
equal k must result only from the different interaction of the electron magnets upon each other. This
yields a completely wrong order of magnitude' (Heisenberg to Pauli, 24 November 1925). Therefore,
if one wanted to keep up the magnetic origin of the singlet-triplet splitting, one had to assume a
strong, so-far-unknown interaction between the magnetic moments of the electrons in the helium
atom.

288 Chapter V Modifications and Extensions of Matrix Mechanics
Heisenberg had known both of these difficulties from before and had
discussed them with Pauli since fall 1925. He attributed great importance to the first
question, namely, how to express the exclusion principle in the quantum-
theoretical formalism in which, at first sight, there seemed to be no place for it, as
it seemed primarily to be concerned with the explanation of the anomalous
Zeeman effects of many-electron atoms. Thus, on 16 November 1925, he had
written to Pauli: 'I have also thought a little about the "stress" ("Zwang") and I
still have the hope that it might arise when two (or several) electrons are
coupled,' adding in parenthesis the remark, 'and in fact just on account of your
exclusion of equivalent orbits' (Heisenberg to Pauli, 16 November 1925). The
later calculations on the anomalous Zeeman effects (Heisenberg and Jordan,
1926) had shown, however, that the electron spin hypothesis accounted very well
for the observed phenomena and there was no need to postulate an extra 'stress'
('Zwang9). However, the problem of explaining quantitatively the differences
between corresponding terms of the singlet and triplet series in the spectra of
helium and alkaline-earth atoms had remained. On the basis of a thorough
analysis of the empirical data Heisenberg decided to attack simultaneously the
two difficulties about which he had written to Born.
Heisenberg's discussions in Copenhagen, especially with Bohr, were first of all
concerned with what could possibly be learned from the known empirical facts
and whether these facts offered any hint for their theoretical treatment. 'The
normally existing grouping of alkaline-earth terms in a singlet system and a
triplet system,' Pauli had noted in his Handbuch article, ' . . . contradicts,
however, the purely relativistic interpretation of the complex structure. In this
grouping the separation between both term systems is large compared to the
triplet splitting' (Pauli, 1926b, p. 254). The value of the energy difference between
the components of the triplet had been found to be of the order of the
corrections due to fine structure (Millikan and Bowen, 1925a); hence it was
accounted for in matrix mechanics by the combined effect of the magnetic
coupling of the electron spin and the relativistic correction, as had already been
shown by Heisenberg and Jordan (1926). The singlet-triplet energy difference
seemed to suggest, on the other hand, that it might arise from the interaction
energy of the two electrons, for as Pauli had remarked earlier: 'Although the
dependence of the separation between singlet and triplet systems on the atomic
number has not yet been observed, nevertheless it seems to be natural to assume
that this separation is caused by the dependence of the electric interaction energy
of both valence electrons on the orientation of their orbits and will, therefore,
first behave like a screening doublet' (Pauli, 1926b, p. 254). That is, the singlet-
triplet energy difference had the order of magnitude of the correction due to
'screening' in Rydberg's empirical term formulae.
Another remarkable fact about the intensities of lines had been observed in
the spectra of alkaline-earth atoms: whereas the transitions between terms of the
same sequence, say triplets, were of strengths similar to those of the lines in the
neighbouring alkali atoms, the intercombination lines were 'in general weak and

V.6 Key to the Helium Problem
irregular in the case of real alkaline-earth elements Mg, Ca, Sr, Ba, in which the
distance between singlet and triplet terms is large compared to the distance of
triplet terms from each other' (Pauli, 1926b, p. 218). This situation showed up
most prominently in the spectrum of helium, the purest two-electron system in
nature: consistent with the fact that the ratio of the component splitting in
ortho-helium to the average distance of the ortho-helium multiplet from the
corresponding parhelium singlet is much smaller than in the alkaline-earth
atoms, the intercombination lines in helium were found to be particularly
weak.386
All these empirical facts suggested strongly that, in order to understand
two-electron systems, one had first to establish a proper theory of helium spectra,
and the most important task of this theory was to determine the energy
difference between the ortho-helium and parhelium terms from the Coulomb
repulsion of the electrons. That is, from the Coulomb repulsion of the electrons a
new strong force had to arise that would explain the large singlet-triplet splitting.
At this point Heisenberg was able to exploit his earlier acquaintance with the
helium problem. Especially, he could refer to an idea which had been
emphasized by Born and himself several years previously (Born and Heisenberg, 1923a).
They had then discovered that, in atomic systems having two or more electrons,
the phases between the motions of the electrons assumed fixed values when the
perturbation—i.e., the electron-electron interaction—was switched on; for these
systems, which they had called 'accidentally degenerate,' they had developed a
proper perturbation scheme.387 The phase relations gave rise to a certain
resonance effect which had been discussed in Göttingen quite frequently in
connection with a mechanical example. As Heisenberg recalled: There is the old
classical experiment which is shown in every lecture course: two pendula being
coupled by a piece of rubber. It was natural to study this system in quantum
theory, and we certainly discussed it already very early in the period before
quantum mechanics, especially in connection with dispersion theory'
(Heisenberg, Conversations, p. 362).
In the dispersion approach, which had been used especially since Kramers'
work in 1924, the motions of electrons in atoms were represented by harmonic
386At the time when Pauli wrote the Handbuch article on quantum theory—i.e., in summer
1925—only the strongest intercombination lines between the ortho-helium and parhelium states had
been observed (Lyman, 1924).
It should be mentioned at this point that the triplet structure of ortho-helium lines had not yet
been (experimentally) settled in 1926 when Heisenberg developed the theory of the helium spectrum.
Thus, in one of the best known tables in those days the ortho-helium lines were listed as 'doublets';
however, it was also indicated that the Zeeman effect of the 'helium' doublet lines had a character
different from those of the alkali-doublets (see Paschen and Götze, 1922, p. 28, especially footnote 3).
This empirical situation did not prevent Pauli from writing in his Handbuch article: 'Nevertheless it is
obvious to compare, apart from the complex structure, both term systems of helium with the two
term systems of alkaline earths, since, at any rate, one is dealing with a two-electron system in the
case of helium as well.' (Pauli, 1926b, p. 218)
387 Actually, Max Born had discovered the existence of such phase relations earlier in a discussion
of models of the hydrogen atom (Born, 1922).

oscillations of given frequencies. Thus, the coupling of the valence-electrons in
an atom could be described by the following simplified model: two identical
oscillators, each having the same mass m and frequency v (=o)/2ir), were
coupled by an (in general small) interaction term, m\qxq2, where qx and q2
denote the position variables of the oscillators 1 and 2. In classical mechanics,
the system will develop a resonance and a beat frequency will interfere with the
original frequency giving rise to two new, shifted frequencies. This effect may be
demonstrated easily by performing a canonical transformation of the variables,
the momenta px and p2 and the position coordinates qx and q2. The Hamilto-
nian, //, of the system—i.e., H = (\/2m)(p2 + m2co2q2) + (l/2ra)(/?f + m2co2ql)

where the new position variables are given by the equations
q\ = -jz- (?, + q2) and q2= -L (qx - q2), (293a)

and the new frequencies, v\ and v'2, by the equations
2ttv\ = co\ = V^2 + A and 2irv2= u'2 = Vco2 - A . (293b)
Hence, there arise two specific motions, one with frequency vx in which the two
oscillators vibrate in phase, and the other with lower frequency v2 in which they
vibrate with an opposite phase.
Heisenberg quickly recognized that the reformulation of the classical example
of coupled oscillators in quantum mechanics would yield an analogous result.
That is, if one considered two identical oscillators which were coupled by a small
perturbation term, m\qxq2, where qx and q2 are the position variables of the
individual oscillators, then exactly the same coordinate transformation as in the
classical theory, Eq. (293a), could be made. Again, the transformation leads to a
Hamiltonian having the form given by Eq. (293), and the eigenvalues of this
Hamiltonian are
Wn,n,= (n\ + \)hv\ + (nj + i)fo£, (294)
where n\ and n2 assume integral values, 0, 1, 2, ... , etc., and v\ and v'2 denote
the two frequencies given by Eq. (293b).
In the three-man paper, where a universal algebraic formalism of perturbation
theory applicable to arbitrary quantum-mechanical systems had been developed,
Born, Heisenberg and Jordan had mentioned explicitly the fact that now 'two
originally identical, non-degenerate systems (where all the frequencies of the
individual system should be different) coupled by arbitrary forces,' could be
treated (BHJ, 1926, p. 589). The authors had, however, not treated any example,

V.6 Key to the Helium Problem
although the two coupled harmonic oscillators did have exactly the properties
which they mentioned.388 The algebraic perturbation theory was first used by
Heisenberg and Jordan in their calculations of the anomalous Zeeman effects,
where they considered an atomic system whose Hamiltonian (including the terms
Hx and H2, Eqs. (279a) and (279b'), due to magnetic interaction) was degenerate
in the z-component of the electron spin (Heisenberg and Jordan, 1926, Section
3). The perturbation calculation then yielded multiplets which were separated
(for small magnetic field strengths) by magnitudes of the same order as the
fine-structure terms that arose both from magnetic interactions and relativistic
corrections. It was the result of this calculation which convinced Heisenberg that
the interaction arising from the addition of electron spin alone to the existing
mechanical description of atoms would not be sufficient to explain the
differences between the terms of different sequences in the spectrum of helium. Such a
large difference, he was certain, would only be caused by a perturbation term
containing the Coulomb replusion between the two electrons. That was the basic
idea which Heisenberg followed and tried to formulate mathematically, and it
indeed provided the key to the solution of the helium problem.
Between 5 May, when he wrote to Pauli about his initial thoughts, and 26
May, when he sketched the scheme in his letter to Born, Heisenberg developed
the method for calculating the energy states of the helium atom. He began by
considering a system consisting of two electrons moving around a (heavy)
nucleus. In zeroth approximation, the influence of the electrons on each other
and effects due to spin were neglected and the Hamiltonian, H0, was assumed to
be made up of two terms, Ha and Hb, each referring to the motion of one
electron (denoted by the indices a and b, respectively). He assumed, in agreement
with the earlier discussions of many-electron atoms, that in this approximation
the second electron just altered the effective charge of the nucleus; that is, the
effective charge took on values between e and 2e, depending upon whether the
second electron moved inside or outside the orbit of the other electron. Thus, the
Hamiltonians of the two electrons, i.e., Ha and Hb, were identical, and, as a
result, there existed a peculiar degeneracy of the Hamiltonian H°. The reason for
this was that any eigenvalue W„m of H°, in which the electrons a and b were not
in equivalent orbits, that is,
W°nm=Wan + Wbm with Wn*Whm, (295)
388One may naturally ask the question why Born, Heisenberg and Jordan, in fall 1925, did not
illustrate the use of their general perturbation scheme by treating a few, obvious examples. 'When we
wrote that paper,' Heisenberg recalled, 'our general situation was this: We saw that we had entered a
new field where one could pick up new things wherever one went. Therefore, we just took what came
our way but without following it further, because we realized that to follow it further would take
some time and we would have to do some work' (Heisenberg, Conversations, p. 363). Surely, the
application of the abstract formalism of degenerate systems, as developed by Max Born in the
three-man paper (BHJ, 1926, Chapter 3), to calculate something as practical as the states of helium
did not seem to be the most pressing problem. 'But it may be that we discussed it among ourselves,'
Heisenberg remarked later. 'We had already in mind that when we had finished the paper [i.e., BHJ,
1926] then, sooner or later, we must try the helium problem and the problem of two equal [i.e.,
identical] coupled systems' (Heisenberg, Conversations, p. 364).

where use has been made of the symmetry of the matrix Hl with respect to the

Heisenberg did not write down the above equations in his letter to Born
because he assumed that Born would immediately make the simple calculation
himself, but he pointed out their consequence:391 'For all following
considerations the decisive result now is this: The term system can be divided into two
separate parts (• and X), such that transitions occur only within the • system or
within the X system, but never from the • system to the X system.' And he drew
a figure to illustrate this (see Fig. 2). Heisenberg then explained: The transitions
from [the states] • to [the states] X vanish not only in the dipole radiation, but
also in the quadrupole radiation and in all higher approximations, provided only
that the two [unperturbed] systems are really totally equivalent, as for instance
two electrons revolving around one nucleus. One can also never induce
transitions by collisions from one system to the other. You will be able to verify this
easily' (Heisenberg to Born, 26 May 1926).
Heisenberg found the results of these simple considerations so important that,
about two weeks after he had written the letter to Born, he submitted a paper
entitled. 'Mehrkörperproblem und Resonanz in der Quantenmechanik' ('Many-Body
Problem and Resonance in Quantum Mechanics') to Zeitschrift für Physik
(Heisenberg, 1926b), in which he gave a full discussion of the points he had
mentioned to Born. Especially, he proved there the statement that no transitions
occur from the terms denoted by • to terms denoted by X in the following way.
The radiation amplitudes, f„imit„2m2, of the unperturbed system are symmetric
with respect to an exchange of the electrons a and b (i.e., /„,„,, „2/fl2 = fmi„ltm2„2).

and (297b).392 Thus, for example, in the case of the specific transition between

Heisenberg noted explicitly in his paper:
The line intensities within the two term series are in first approximation the same as the
intensities between the corresponding original terms of the single term system (a and b), since
the amplitudes of the type f , which correspond to simultaneous transitions of both
systems, disappear in first approximation. Only for combinations with those states, which
correspond to equivalent states of the partial systems a and b, is the intensity in the one term
series—due to Eqs. [(298c, d)]—twice as large as in the original system; the other term series
contains no such states. Therefore, the "total intensity" is not changed by the resonance in the
first approximation. (Heisenberg, 1926b, p. 419)
V.6 Key to the Helium Problem 295

XXX
Figure 3 Term Spectrum of Helium without Spin. The Energy Increases from Left to
Right.
The results contained in Eqs. (298a-d) encouraged Heisenberg to treat the
helium atom as a system showing what he called the 'resonance effect,' i.e., a
behaviour characterizing systems which can approximately be separated into two
partial systems such that 'the absorption frequency of one system coincides with
the emission frequency of the other system, and vice versa9 (Heisenberg, 1926b, p.
416). The helium spectra indeed possessed many features similar to the one
derived in the case of two coupled linear oscillators (of the same frequency)
which Heisenberg had discussed in his letter to Born and, in more detail, in the
above-mentioned paper on the many-body problem and resonance. Thus, he
explained, for example, in his letter to Born:
Now the application to helium is as follows: 1. We assume that the electrons have
no magnets [i.e., are spinless]. Then the term spectrum evidently looks like [Fig. 3]
and the two systems, in which the term spectrum separates, are parhelium [denoted
by the dots (•), in agreement with Fig. [2]] and ortho-helium [denoted by the crosses
(X)]. With this explanation one understands immediately, first, that the separation
between par- and ortho-helium is of the order of magnitude of the Rydberg
correction; second, that no combinations occur between the two systems in this
approximation; and third, that the ls-term [i.e., the term denoted by (11) in Fig. [2]
or the first dot (•) in the first row of Fig. [3]] in the ortho-system is missing.
(Heisenberg to Born, 26 May 1926)
Indeed, the singlet-triplet difference of corresponding terms—as, for instance,
of (12) and (21) or of (24) and (42) in Fig. 2—is obtained from Eqs. (297a) and
(297b) as
knm = 2H\nm,mn), (299)
where H\nm,mn) may be interpreted physically as the difference of the
perturbation energies which the Coulomb repulsion between the electrons adds to the
energies, W% and W^, of the single electron orbits. The same Coulomb repulsion
was also responsible for the so-called Rydberg correction in the empirical helium
term formula; hence Eq. (299) provided a qualitative understanding of the
helium spectra. And, as Heisenberg wrote to Born: 'Quantitatively I have
computed the singlet-triplet separation for/?-terms; this agrees rather well [with
the data] for He and Li+' (Heisenberg to Born, 26 May 1926).
This coarse model for treating the helium atom did not, of course, solve all the
problems which Heisenberg had set for himself. It did not account for effects due
to electron spin, nor did it make any use of the exclusion principle (whose role in
atoms having several electrons had become well established). In order to
approach these problems, Heisenberg developed modifications of his simplified
296 Chapter V Modifications and Extensions of Matrix Mechanics
model, which he mentioned as items two and three in his letter to Born. He
began by describing a second model as: '2. If one now puts arbitrary magnets on
the electrons [i.e., the electrons are associated with a magnet having a magnetic
moment /a in a fixed direction, say along the z-axis], then the picture I of the
terms [see Fig. 3] remains exactly the same, but now the electrons are in general
not equivalent anymore, hence weak inter-combinations occur' (Heisenberg to
Born, 26 May 1926). That is, if the interaction between the electrons' magnetic
moments (in a fixed direction in space) and the internal magnetic field H,
(caused by the helium core at the position of the electrons—see Section V.5) is
considered, then the energy of a given orbit (i.e., the average energy of the
electron moving in an orbit, neglecting the Coulomb repulsion between the
electrons) depends on the relative orientation of the magnetic moment /* (and,
therefore, also of the spin s, according to Eq. (278)) with respect to the plane of
the orbit. Although this magnetic energy (whose strength is equal to the scalar
product /a • H,) is comparatively small, it removes the initial degeneracy of the
two-electron system. The perturbation calculation, including the Coulomb term
and the magnetic term, then leads more or less to the same spectra as shown in
Fig. 2 or Fig. 3. However, each term (nm) gets slightly shifted and transitions
between the dotted (•) and crossed (X) sequences begin to take place; their
intensities are determined by the magnetic interaction and are, therefore, small.
Though model 2 represented more features of the helium atom than the
spinless model, Heisenberg was not yet satisfied. In particular, this model did not
quite explain the structure of the helium spectra, for, with a fixed direction of the
spin, both terms would be singlets. Moreover, it was difficult to understand how
nature would create a preferred direction of electron spin. Heisenberg, therefore,
proceeded to deal with a third model, which he described to Born as follows:
'3. If one puts the right [Uhlenbeck-] Goudsmit magnet without a given direction
on each electron, then the electrons are again equivalent. But the separation
[after the Coulomb repulsion term Hl is switched on] into the two systems is
different from the previous one. To begin with, for each term of I, four positions
of the magnet must occur, hence four new terms will result [instead of one in I]'
(Heisenberg to Born, 26 May 1926). Indeed, Heisenberg knew from his earlier
considerations of the magnetic interaction (e.g., from the discussion of the
anomalous Zeeman effects) that the spins of two electrons might add up to
produce either a singlet (total spin 0) or a triplet (total spin 1) having three
separate states. This term scheme, to a good approximation, is represented by
Fig. 4, which Heisenberg drew in his letter to Born, calling it system II: in it the
spin singlet term and the middle term of the spin triplet (i.e., the term of the spin
triplet with zero component of the spin in the direction of the internal magnetic
field) coincided. Now it seemed that the division into two term sequences lost its
meaning: on the one hand, each quadruplet could be divided into a singlet and a
triplet system representing qualitatively both term spectra of helium; on the
other, weak transitions between all terms appeared to be possible. However, the
last conclusion was wrong, for there occurred a new, different separation of term
sequences, which was denoted again by dots (•) and crosses (X) in Fig. 4.
