Heisenberg reported to Born: The one system (• in II) has the singlets in
parhelium and the triplets in ortho-helium, while the other ( X) has the triplets in
parhelium and the singlets in ortho-helium. The two systems, which are thus
characterized [that is, the terms of the two sequences • and X] cannot now
combine at all; on the other hand, weak inter-combinations between parhelium
and ortho-helium [states] are now possible within one system [• or X ] ([they have
the] order of magnitude of [the ratio of] magnetic interaction to singlet-triplet
[energy difference])' (Heisenberg to Born, 26 May 1926).
The above claims rested on two observations which Heisenberg discussed in
some detail in his published paper on the spectra of atoms with two electrons
(Heisenberg, 1926c, Part II, § 1). First, it is evident from Fig. 4 that in each of the
quadruplets of the first and second row the two states (having the same
component, ms, of the total spin in the direction of the inner field H,) are
degenerate if one neglects the mutual interaction between [ia and nb, the
magnetic moments of the two electrons a and b. However, taking into account
the latter interaction—which again is considerably smaller than the interaction
between the internal magnetic field of the atom and the electrons' magnetic
moments—one may repeat a perturbation calculation by using a scheme similar
to the one given in Eqs. (296), but with a different perturbation Hamiltonian. As
a result the two degenerate states of each quadruplet, which have zero ms-value,
will split into a separate pair with an energy difference of the order of the
interaction energy between the magnetic moments /ia and p,b. The higher term of
each pair of states with zero ms may then be grouped together with the two
remaining states of the quadruplet, to which both electrons contribute the total
ms-value, either +1 or - 1; the remaining lower state of each pair, however,
becomes an isolated state. Thus, each quadruplet is ordered into a singlet (with
total spin momentum zero) and a triplet (with total spin momentum h/lir and
the components ms= +1,0,-1). Moreover, no singlet of the upper term
sequence in Fig. 4 will combine with any triplet state of the same sequence;
similarly there will be no singlet-triplet transitions among the terms of the lower
sequence in Fig 4. Strong transitions occur between different states of singlets
and between different states of triplets in the same term sequence.
The second important point to be noted is that the singlet terms of the lower
sequence (denoted by X) show a special symmetry when the two electrons of the

atom exchange their orbits and their spin components (i.e., mf and msb) in the
direction of the internal field. As a result, nonvanishing transition amplitudes
might be obtained between the triplet states of the upper sequence and the singlet
states of the lower sequence (in Fig 4), whose magnitude is determined by the
interaction between the two magnetic moments. Similarly, transitions are to be
expected between the singlet states of the upper sequence and the triplet states of
the lower sequence. The calculation of the transition amplitudes, which included
the spin degrees of freedom, confirmed these possibilities. It also showed that
transitions between the triplets of the upper series and the triplets of the lower
series were forbidden, as were the transitions between the singlet states of the
upper and the singlet states of the lower sequence.394
At one point the model 3 in Heisenberg's letter to Born was not correct; it
turned out to be too rich in so far as it provided two ortho- and two parhelium term
sequences. In order to reproduce the empirical facts which implied one term
sequence for each (ortho- or par-) helium spectrum, Heisenberg proposed the
following hypotheses: The system • [in Fig. 4] represents the helium spectrum
and [the system] X does not exist at all.' This hypothesis, he argued in his letter
to Born, would not contradict the purely quantum-mechanical calculation 'since
all the probabilities of transition to the other system vanish anyway; the term
spectrum is "complete" ("vollständig") in your sense.'395 The exclusion of one of
394The reason is that the transformation matrix S, whose elements enter into the matrix equation
/' = S~ ]fS (where / describes the matrix of the amplitudes of the unperturbed system—which is
degenerate both in the orbital and spin angular momentum quantum numbers), is now a product of
two transformation matrices, S' and 5". S' refers to the transformation matrix which has been
introduced in connection with the perturbation term H] stemming from the Coulomb repulsion of
the electron. (This perturbation term removes the orbital degeneracy of the electrons.) S" removes the
degeneracy in ms, i.e., it has been introduced in connection with the perturbation term stemming
from the interaction of the two magnetic moments of the electrons. Now, for instance, if one
calculates the amplitude of transition between a triplet state of the upper sequence and a singlet state
of the lower sequence, all the elements of the matrix 5* ~]—which multiply / from the left-hand
side—are identical and equal to 1/^2 . On the other hand, the elements of the matrix S on the
right-hand side stem from the second columns of the matrices S' and S". Both are antisymmetric
with respect to the quantum number involved, as can be seen from Eq. (297b). However, if both the
orbital and the spin quantum numbers are exchanged, the two minus signs in the matrix elements
Smnmn (in the second Eq. (297b)) cancel. Hence all the matrix elements under consideration again
become identical and equal to 1/V2, and finite transition amplitudes are obtained.
It should be mentioned that it is also possible to prove that transitions which are not allowed
according to the above results can also not be excited by collisions of atoms. Heisenberg proved this
by replacing the two-electron system by a system of coupled oscillators in which nonlinear terms (in
the oscillator coordinates qx and q2) are taken into account. Again he found that the separation
between the two term sequences is strictly maintained. Since the effect of collisions between atoms
can be described in terms of these nonlinear couplings, no new transitions emerge in two-electron
systems even if collisions occur between them (Heisenberg, 1926b, pp. 415-416).
395 In the joint paper with Norbert Wiener, Born had postulated the existence of a closed set of
functions associated with each operator in quantum mechanics (Born and Wiener, 1926, p. 92); in
particular, this set had been defined by the fact that any operator of a mechanical system applied to a
function leads to another function of the set. The functions may be chosen to have the form
exp(2777>/)> where the v are the quantum-mechanical frequencies. Now if only transitions between
states of one term sequence occur, no frequencies other than those given by the equation hv = Wn
— Wm, where Wn and Wm are the terms of the sequence, arise. Hence this term sequence leads to a
closed and complete set of functions, {exp(2777>/)}, as postulated by Born and Wiener.

V.6 Key to the Helium Problem
the term sequences was not only possible, to Heisenberg it even seemed to be a
necessary requirement because it 'agrees with the Bose statistics, which reduces
the statistical weights by a factor of two and automatically yields Pauli's ban
[exclusion of equivalent orbits]' (Heisenberg to Born, 26 May 1926).
Thus, Heisenberg finally brought in the exclusion principle which was known
to play an important role in atomic systems containing several electrons. He had
also demonstrated, as he had mentioned three weeks earlier to Pauli (Heisenberg
to Pauli, 5 May 1926), how the exclusion of equivalent orbits, that is, orbits
which have all of their quantum numbers (including ms) identical, was connected
with the singlet-triplet energy difference in helium. Moreover, he had made a
move in the direction of realizing Pauli's programme of a deeper understanding
of the exclusion principle, for, as he noted: 'If only one (•) of the two systems
occurs in nature, then it yields, on one hand, a reduction of the statistical
weights, just in the sense proposed by Bose; on the other hand, Pauli's exclusion
of equivalent orbits is automatically satisfied for the correct choice of the system'
(Heisenberg, 1926b, p. 422). By generalizing this consideration to the case of
atomic systems containing n electrons, in which he proposed to retain only that
one of the n\ noncombining sequences (arising from the perturbation
calculations) which contained no equivalent orbits, Heisenberg concluded that 'Pauli's
exclusion prescription and Bose's rule are the same, that they do not contradict
quantum mechanics, and that one can calculate the energy values and all other
quantities in quantum mechanics' (Heisenberg to Born, 26 May 1926).
In relating together Pauli's exclusion principle and the Bose statistics [for
light-quanta] Heisenberg, who was hardly an expert in statistical mechanics, had
been particularly misled by the observation that Bose's counting method
consisted in reducing «!, the statistical weight of a state containing n identical
particles (equal to 2 in the case of a two-electron system), to unity. Enrico Fermi
had already discovered a little earlier that there was another counting method
which, obeying Pauli's exclusion principle, would also reduce the statistical
weight in the same way, but that it was different from the method proposed by
Bose for light-quanta and applied by Einstein to a monatomic ideal gas (Fermi,
1926a). Although Fermi's work was also published in an issue of Zeitschrift für
Physik before the end of May (Fermi, 1926b), Heisenberg did not sense the
connection between Fermi's ideas and his own investigations on two-electron
atoms.396
Pauli, who, in June 1926, received from Heisenberg a copy of the manuscript
396Paul Dirac, like Heisenberg, made use of Schrödinger's wave mechanical approach in treating
quantum systems of several particles; without taking notice of Fermi's papers, he rediscovered Fermi
(-Dirac) statistics a few months later (Dirac, 1926f). Even more remarkable, Pauli, who, based on his
record at that time, was a greater expert on statistical mechanics than either Heisenberg or Dirac, did
not at all recognize the connection established by Fermi and Dirac between the statistics of identical
particles, the many-electron problem and the exclusion principle. At first, Pauli maintained that it
(the Fermi-Dirac statistics) was all wrong, but several months later, after he had carefully studied the
fluctuation term in gases, he wrote to Heisenberg: 'I now think much more kindly about the
Fermi-Dirac statistics, and several arguments appear to me to speak in its favour' (Pauli to
Heisenberg, 19 October 1926).

on the helium problem (see Heisenberg to Pauli, 8 June 1926), was not satisfied
with Heisenberg^ attempt at a deeper justification of the exclusion principle by
applying 'unattractive additional constraints' ('unschöne Zusatzverbote' Pauli to
Wentzel, 5 July 1926) to the quantum-mechanical equations. He hoped for a
better argument; but since, for the time being he was unable to find it,
Heisenberg went ahead with his helium calculation and eventually submitted a
paper on the spectra of two-electron systems towards the end of July
(Heisenberg, 1926c). In this paper he simplified some of his previous arguments by
applying a different method: Schrödinger's wave mechanics. Thus, for example,
he easily obtained the result that in the case of electrons with spin (i.e., the model
3 in his letter to Born) the resulting term systems denoted by dots (•) and crosses
(X), respectively, would not combine; the reason was that the crossed terms had
to be described by Schrödinger functions, which were antisymmetric in both the
space and spin coordinates of the electrons, while the dotted terms had to be
described by totally symmetric Schrödinger functions, and no transitions
occurred between any symmetric and antisymmetric states. The second, and for
Heisenberg the more important, point, was a practical one: by the end of May he
was well prepared to embark upon the detailed computation of helium states. In
principle, the tools of matrix mechanics had already been extended—by
Cornelius Lanczos, Born and Norbert Wiener, Pauli and Heisenberg himself, and by
Gregor Wentzel and Paul Dirac—to the extent that the two-electron system
could be treated. However, at the same time Erwin Schrödinger's method of the
wave equation had also been published (Schrödinger, 1926c), and Heisenberg did
not hesitate using it (rather than the clumsier matrix methods) to obtain the
elements of perturbation matrices occurring in equations of the type of Eqs. (296)
for the energy states of the helium atom.397 Thus, a calculation which was
considered as a climactic proof of the validity of matrix mechanics, provided an
immediate triumph of the alternative scheme of wave mechanics.
However, the key to the helium problem—the resonance effect in coupled
equivalent quantum systems—had been found within the Göttingen formulation
of quantum mechanics. This resonance effect would play a crucial role in the
solution of two outstanding problems of atomic theory: the problem of covalent
binding of molecules, such as the hydrogen molecule, and the problem of
understanding the origin of ferromagnetism. While the hydrogen molecule was
first treated by Walter Heitler and Fritz London, who associated the covalent
397 Heisenberg knew from Pauli that Schrödinger's scheme was equivalent to the extended matrix
formalism and could be used, especially, to calculate matrix elements elegantly. In his paper on the
two-electron atoms, Heisenberg stuck to matrix methods and considerations as far as principles were
concerned; however, when it came to calculate the matrix elements of the perturbation terms in the
Hamiltonian, he remarked simply: The calculation of the quantities is performed in the simplest way
according to Schrödinger's method of eigenfunctions' (Heisenberg, 1926c, p. 503).
Let us add here in passing that Gregor Wentzel, who had discussed the Stark effect (including
that of the second order in the electric field strength) at the joint meeting of the Gauverein
Württemberg, Baden und Bayern of the German Physical Society on 16 May 1926 in Stuttgart, did not
publish his results in contrast to his announcement (see, Verh. d. Deutsch. Phys. Ges. (3) 7, p. 26,
(1926)). The obvious reason was that meanwhile Schrödinger had come out with his calculations of
the Stark effect by means of the wave mechanical methods (Schrödinger, 1926f).

V.6 Key to the Helium Problem
binding with the exchange forces arising from the resonance effect (Heitler and
London, 1927), Heisenberg himself attacked the problem of ferromagnetism
beginning in fall 1926. There one had to cope with a similar difficulty as in the
helium problem, namely, how only the weak spin-spin interactions (i.e., the
interactions between the magnetic moments arising from the spin due to Eq.
(278)) can create the 'strong molecular field' introduced by Pierre Weiss many
years earlier (Weiss, 1907). Heisenberg discovered that in the phenomenon of
ferromagnetism it was again the Coulomb forces between the electrons that gave
rise to the molecular field, whereas the mutual interaction of electron spins only
created the empirically observed regular alignments of the magnetic moments of
the atoms in the solid state (Heisenberg, 1928).398
Thus, in spite of the fact that Pauli's question concerning the physical
explanation of the exclusion principle could not yet be answered, the helium
calculation opened the path towards an understanding of complex atomic
systems.399 In any case, Heisenberg was very optimistic. 'I am convinced,' he
wrote to Jordan a few days after submitting his paper on helium spectra to
Zeitschrift für Physik, 'that the spectra of all chemical elements can be obtained
(apart from the satellite structure [i.e., those caused by a magnetic moment of the
nucleus, as in Pauli's treatment of the hyperfine structure (Pauli, 1924c)]) from
quantum mechanics in a unique manner without physics [i.e., additional physical
assumptions] by bone-headed calculation' (Heisenberg to Jordan, 28 July 1926).
398 Heisenberg first suggested his ideas about solving the problem of ferromagnetism in a letter to
Pauli. He wrote:
I have myself thought a little about the theory of ferromagnetism, superconductivity, and
similar obscenities ("Schweinerein"). The idea is this: In order to use Langevin's theory of
ferromagnetism, one has to assume a large coupling force between the spinning electrons (only
these truly rotate). As in the case of helium, this force should be provided indirectly by the
resonance. I believe that one can prove in general that the smallest energy results when the
spin-vectors are parallel to each other. The energy differences in question are of the electrical
order of magnitude, but they vanish very fast with increasing distance [between the atoms]. I
have the feeling (without knowing the matter in the least) that this might suffice in principle for
an interpretation of ferromagnetism. In order to decide the question why most materials are not
ferromagnetic, but some are, one must of course calculate quantitatively; one could perhaps
make it plausible that in the case of Fe, Co and Ni the situation [for ferromagnetism] is most
favourable. It is the same for conductivity; here the resonance migration of electrons ä la Hund
may explain the situation. (Heisenberg to Pauli, 4 November 1926)
399In trying to approach the problem of explaining the exclusion principle, Pauli, in fall 1926,
turned to fluctuation considerations. Since the fluctuation formula for particles obeying Fermi
statistics (which corresponds to Einstein's formula, Eq. (181)) contains a wave-like term with a
negative sign, Pauli speculated: 'Perhaps this behaviour might be interpreted by assuming phase
relations between the different de Broglie eigenvibrations; this might give a hint for a future physical
explanation of the fundamental basis of Fermi-statistics and therewith also of the "equivalence rule"
[i.e., the exclusion principle]' (Pauli, 1927, pp. 95-96). Pauli continued to study the problems
connected with spin and statistics over many years, returning to them time and again during the
following three decades, before a more satisfactory 'explanation' of the exclusion principle from
general quantum-theoretical considerations could be given; this was the so-called spin-statistics
theorem. (See, e.g., Pauli, 1955.)
Interestingly enough, in fall 1926 Heisenberg also concerned himself with the treatment of
fluctuations in quantum-mechanical systems (Heisenberg, 1926e). However, Heisenberg's work dealt
with the connection between the concepts of matrix mechanics and wave mechanics, In pursuing this
question futher, Heisenberg arrived at the uncertainty relations, which constituted a fundamental step
in the physical interpretation of quantum mechanics (Heisenberg, 1927b).
